(De)-regularized Maximum Mean Discrepancy Gradient Flow
Zonghao Chen zonghao.chen.22@ucl.ac.uk, Aratrika Mustafi abm6733@psu.edu, Pierre Glaser pierreglaser@msn.com, Anna Korba anna.korba@ensae.fr, Arthur Gretton arthur.gretton@gmail.com, Bharath K. Sriperumbudur bks18@psu.edu

Abstract
We introduce a (de)-regularization of the Maximum Mean Discrepancy (DrMMD) and its Wasserstein gradient flow. Existing gradient flows that transport samples from source distribution to target distribution with only target samples, either lack tractable numerical implementation ( f f -divergence flows) or require strong assumptions and modifications, such as noise injection, to ensure convergence (Maximum Mean Discrepancy flows). In contrast, DrMMD flow can simultaneously (i) guarantee near-global convergence for a broad class of targets in both continuous and discrete time, and (ii) be implemented in closed form using only samples. The former is achieved by leveraging the connection between the DrMMD and the χ 2 \chi^{2} -divergence, while the latter comes by treating DrMMD as MMD with a de-regularized kernel. Our numerical scheme employs an adaptive de-regularization schedule throughout the flow to optimally balance the trade-off between discretization errors and deviations from the χ 2 \chi^{2} regime. The potential application of the DrMMD flow is demonstrated across several numerical experiments, including a large-scale setting of training student/teacher networks.



Zonghao Chen, Aratrika Mustafi, Pierre Glaser, Anna Korba, Arthur Gretton, and Bharath K. Sriperumbudur

f
-divergences, spectral regularization

1
Introduction

Many applications in computational statistics and machine learning involve approximating
a probability distribution
\pi
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(in terms of samples) when only partial information on
\pi
is accessible.
For example, in Bayesian inference,
\pi
is known up to an intractable normalizing constant for complex models.
The setting of interest in this work is the so-called generative modeling setting
(
Brock et al., 2019
;
Ho et al., 2020
;
Song et al., 2021
;
Franceschi et al., 2024
)
where one assumes access to a set of samples from the target distribution
\pi
, with the goal being to generate new samples from
\pi
.
Recently, a popular framework to perform this task involves solving a minimization problem in

\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the space of probability distributions over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, by choosing the objective function to be a
dissimilarity
function
\mathcal{D}(\cdot\|\pi)
(a distance or divergence) between probability distributions that satisfies:
\mathcal{D}(\nu\|\pi)=0
if and only if
\nu=\pi
. Since only samples from
\pi
are available, this problem is solved approximately—yet, the approximate minimizers may
converge to
\pi
as the number of available samples increases.
In particular, in the space of probability distributions with bounded second moment
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, a common approach is to solve this optimization problem by running a (sample-based) approximation of the Wasserstein gradient flow of the functional
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{D}(\cdot\|\pi)
, which defines a path of distributions with steepest descent for
\mathcal{F}
with respect to the Wasserstein-2 distance.

In generative modeling, the choice of
\mathcal{D}(\cdot\|\pi)
depends on two crucial aspects: First, its flow should admit consistent and preferably tractable numerical implementations using only samples from
\pi
, and second, under reasonable assumptions, it should guarantee convergence of its flow to
\pi
, the unique
global
minimizer. Combined, these two properties ensure that, in the large sample limit, this algorithm generates new samples from the target.
Unfortunately, verifying these two properties simultaneously has proved to be a surprisingly challenging task. For instance, recent approaches based on Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD)

(
Arbel et al., 2019
;
Hertrich et al., 2024b
)
, the sliced-Wasserstein distance
(
Liutkus et al., 2019
)
and the Sinkhorn divergence
(
Genevay et al., 2018
)
typically admit consistent finite-sample implementations,
but their global convergence guarantees—when they exist—do not apply to most practical targets
\pi
of interest. To guarantee global convergence, one could instead choose
\mathcal{D}
as an
f
-divergence.
For instance, the (reverse) KL divergence
and
\chi^{2}
-divergence are geodesically convex
(
Villani and others, 2009
, Definition 16.5)
when the target is log-concave (i.e.
\pi\propto e^{-V}
with
V
convex)
(
Ohta and Takatsu, 2011
)
, and hence their flows enjoy better convergence behaviour.
However, while the population Wasserstein gradient flows of the
\chi^{2}
and KL divergences are well-defined
(
Jordan et al., 1998
;
Chewi et al., 2020
)
, they do not come naturally with consistent and tractable sample-based implementations.
Multiple approaches propose to solve a surrogate optimization problem with samples at each iteration of the flow
(
Gao et al., 2019
;
Ansari et al., 2021
;
Simons et al., 2022
;
Birrell et al., 2022
;
Gu et al., 2024
;
Liu et al., 2024a
)
; however, it remains to be formally established whether these surrogate problems preserve the desirable convergence guarantees of
f
-divergence flows.

In the face of the trade-offs present in the current approaches, a natural question arises:
Does there exist a divergence functional
\mathcal{D}(\cdot\|\pi)
whose gradient flow both globally converges, and admits a tractable, consistent sample-based implementation?

In this work, we take a step towards a positive answer by constructing a “de-regularized” variant of the Maximum Mean Discrepancy (
\operatorname{DrMMD}
) and its associated Wasserstein gradient flow.
We prove that the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
gradient flow converges exponentially to the global minimum up to a controllable barrier term for targets
\pi
that satisfy a Poincaré inequality, in both continuous and discrete time regimes. To do so, we establish and leverage a connection between the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
and the
\chi^{2}
divergence, an
f
-divergence whose gradient flow benefits from strong convergence guarantees.
By alternatively viewing
\operatorname{DrMMD}
as MMD with a regularized kernel,
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow comes with a consistent and tractable implementation when only samples from the target
\pi
are available.
In addition, given the empirical success of using adaptive kernels in MMD-based generative models
(
Galashov et al., 2025
;
Li et al., 2017
;
Arbel et al., 2018
)
,
our paper shows theoretically that using adaptive kernels through adaptive regularization indeed improves the convergence of MMD gradient flow.

This paper is organized as follows.
Section

2
introduces the necessary background on reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces (RKHS), the
\operatorname{MMD}
,
\chi^{2}
-divergence, and Wasserstein gradient flows.
Section

3
introduces
\operatorname{DrMMD}
and shows that
\operatorname{DrMMD}
is a valid probability divergence that
metrizes weak convergence.
Section

4
uses
\operatorname{DrMMD}
as the optimization objective to define a Wasserstein gradient flow in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and analyzes the convergence of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow in continuous time.
Sections
5
and
6
define an implementable
\operatorname{DrMMD}
particle descent scheme with both time and space discretization and analyze its convergence.

Section

7
discusses other Wasserstein gradient flows related to our
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow.

Section

8
shows experiments that confirm our theoretical results. The proofs of all results are provided in Section
10
, with the technical results being relegated to an appendix.

2
Background

In this section, we present the definitions and notation used throughout the paper.

2.1
Notations

Let
\mathcal{L}^{d}
be the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denotes the set of all Borel probability measures
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with finite second moment.
For
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\mu\ll\pi
denotes that
\mu
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\pi
. We use
\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mathrm{d}\pi}
to denote the Radon-Nikodym derivative. We recall the standard definition of the Kullback-Leibler divergence,
\operatorname{KL}(\mu\|\pi)=\int\log(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mathrm{d}\pi})\mathrm{d}\mu
if
\mu\ll\pi
,
+\infty
else.

For a continuous mapping
T:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
T_{\#}\mu
denotes the push-forward measure of
\mu
by
T
.
For any
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
L^{2}(\pi)
is the Hilbert space of (equivalence class of) functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\int|f|^{2}d\pi<\infty
.
We denote by
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}
the norm and the inner product of
L^{2}(\pi)
.
We
denote by
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the space of infinitely differentiable functions from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{R}
with compact support.
For a vector valued functions
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
, we abuse the notation of
L^{2}(\pi)
and claim
g\in L^{2}(\pi)
if
g_{i}\in L^{2}(\pi)
for all
i=1,\ldots,p
along with
\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\pi)}:=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\|g_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\pi)}
.

If
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is differentiable, we denote by
\nabla f
the gradient of
f
and
\mathbf{H}f
its Hessian.
f
is
\alpha
-strongly convex if
\mathbf{H}f\succeq\alpha\mathrm{I}
, i.e,
\mathbf{H}f(x)-\alpha\mathrm{I}
is positive semi-definite for any
x
, where
\mathrm{I}
is the identity matrix (also denotes an identity operator depending on the context). For a vector valued function
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, if
g_{i}
is differentiable for all
i=1,\cdots,d
,
\nabla\cdot g
denotes the divergence of
g
. We also denote by
\Delta g
the Laplacian of
g
, where
\Delta g=\nabla\cdot\nabla g
.
We use
\|\cdot\|_{F}
to denote the matrix Frobenius norm.

a\wedge b
and
a\vee b
denote the minimum and maximum of
a
and
b
, respectively.

2.2
Reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces

For a positive semi-definite kernel
k:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, its corresponding reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS)
\mathcal{H}
is a Hilbert space with inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
and norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}}

(
Aronszajn, 1950
)
, such that (i)
k(x,\cdot)\in\mathcal{H}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and (ii) the reproducing property holds, e.g. for all
f\in\mathcal{H}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
f(x)=\langle f,k(x,\cdot)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
.
We denote by
\mathcal{H}^{d}
the Cartesian product RKHS consisting of elements
f=(f_{1},\dots,f_{d})
with
f_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
with inner product
\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{d}}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\langle f_{i},g_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
.

When
\int k(x,x)\mathrm{d}\pi(x)<\infty
,
\mathcal{H}
can be canonically injected into
L^{2}(\pi)
using the operator
\iota_{\pi}:\mathcal{H}\to L^{2}(\pi),\,f\mapsto f
with adjoint
\iota_{\pi}^{\ast}:L^{2}(\pi)\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
given by

\displaystyle\iota_{\pi}^{\ast}f(\cdot)=\int k(x,\cdot)f(x)\mathrm{d}\pi(x).

The operator
\iota_{\pi}
and its adjoint can be composed to form an
L^{2}(\pi)
endomorphism
\mathcal{T}_{\pi}\coloneqq\iota_{\pi}\iota_{\pi}^{\ast}
called the
integral operator
, and a
\mathcal{H}
endomorphism
\Sigma_{\pi}:=\iota_{\pi}^{\ast}\iota_{\pi}=\int k(\cdot,x)\otimes k(\cdot,x)d\pi(x)

(where
(a\otimes b)c\coloneqq\langle b,c\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}a
for
a,b,c\in\mathcal{H}
) called the
covariance operator
.

\mathcal{T}_{\pi}
is compact, positive, self-adjoint, and can thus be diagonalized into
an orthonormal system in
\left\{e_{i}\right\}_{i\geq 1}
of
L^{2}(\pi)
with associated eigenvalues
\varrho_{1}\geq\cdots\varrho_{i}\geq\cdots\geq 0
.

In this paper, we make the following assumption on our kernel.

Assumption 1
.

k:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is a continuous and
c_{0}
-universal kernel, and there exists
K>0
such that
\sup_{x}k(x,x)\leq K
.

We refer the reader to
Carmeli et al. (2010)
for the definition of
c_{0}
-universal kernel. The implication of
Assumption

1
is that the RKHS
\mathcal{H}
is compactly embedded into
L^{2}(\pi)

(
Steinwart and Scovel, 2012
, Lemma 2.3)
, and hence
k(x,x^{\prime})
has a absolute, uniform and pointwise convergent Mercer representation
(
Steinwart and Scovel, 2012
, Corollary 3.5)
,

\displaystyle k(x,x^{\prime})=\sum_{i\geq 1}\varrho_{i}e_{i}(x)e_{i}(x^{\prime}),

(1)

for any
x
and
x^{\prime}
in the support of
\pi
.
Since the kernel is
c_{0}
-universal, the RKHS
\mathcal{H}
is dense in
L^{2}(\pi)
for all Borel probability measures
\pi

(
Sriperumbudur et al., 2011
, Section 3.1)
and
\{e_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
becomes an orthornormal basis of
L^{2}(\pi)

(
Steinwart and Scovel, 2012
, Theorem 3.1)
.

The power of the integral operator
\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{r}
is defined as
\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{r}f:=\sum_{i\geq 1}\varrho_{i}^{r}\left\langle f,e_{i}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}e_{i},f\in L^{2}(\pi)
.
For
f\in\operatorname{Ran}(\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{r})
, there exists
q\in L^{2}(\pi)
such that
f=\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{r}q
.
The exponent
r
quantifies the smoothness of the range space relative to the original RKHS
\mathcal{H}
with
0<r<\frac{1}{2}
(
resp.

r>\frac{1}{2}
) yields spaces that are less (
resp.
more) smooth than
\mathcal{H}
with
\operatorname{Ran}(\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{1/2})
being isometrically isomorphic to
\mathcal{H}

(
Cucker and Zhou, 2007
;
Fischer and Steinwart, 2020
)
.

We make an additional assumption—commonly employed in the kernel-based gradient flow literature
(
Glaser et al., 2021
;
He et al., 2024
;
Korba et al., 2020
;
Arbel et al., 2019
)
—on the regularity of the kernel that will be employed in studying the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
gradient flow.

Assumption 2
.

k:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is twice differentiable in the sense of
(
Steinwart and Christmann, 2008
, Definition 4.35)
, i.e., for
i,j\in\{1,\cdots,d\}
, both
\partial_{i}\partial_{i+d}k
and
\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\partial_{i+d}\partial_{j+d}k
exist and are continuous.
There exist constants
K_{1d},K_{2d}>0
such that
\left\|\nabla_{1}k(x,\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{d}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left\|\partial_{i}k(x,\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\sqrt{K_{1d}}
and
\left\|\mathbf{H}_{1}k(x,\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{d\times d}}:=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\left\|\partial_{i}\partial_{j}k(x,\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\sqrt{K_{2d}}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Many kernels satisfy both
Assumption

1
and
2
, including the class of bounded, continuous, and translation invariant kernels on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
whose Fourier transforms have finite second and fourth moments. This is easy to verify by employing the Fourier transform representation of the RKHS
(
Wendland, 2004
, Theorem 10.12)
and noting that the finiteness of the RKHS norm of
\nabla_{1}k(\cdot,x)
and
\mathbf{H}_{1}k(\cdot,x)
for all
x
corresponds to the existence and finiteness of the second and fourth moments of the Fourier transform of the kernel, respectively. This condition is satisfied by
the Gaussian kernel, Matérn kernels of order
\nu
with
\nu+\frac{d}{2}\geq 2
and the inverse multiquadratic kernel.

2.3
Maximum mean discrepancy and
\chi^{2}
-divergence

The Maximum Mean Discrepancy (
\operatorname{MMD}
)
(
Gretton et al., 2012
)
between
\mu
and
\pi
is defined as the RKHS norm of the difference between the mean embeddings
1
1

1

Such mean embeddings are well-defined under
Assumption

1
.

m_{\mu}:=\int k(x,\cdot)d\mu(x)
and
m_{\pi}:=\int k(x,\cdot)d\pi(x)
.

\displaystyle\operatorname{MMD}(\mu\|\pi)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\|\int k(x,\cdot)d\mu(x)-\int k(x,\cdot)d\pi(x)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}=\left\|m_{\mu}-m_{\pi}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}.

The function
m_{\mu}-m_{\pi}
is often referred to as the “witness function”.
When the kernel
k
is
c_{0}
-universal,
\operatorname{MMD}(\mu\|\pi)=0
if and only if
\mu=\pi
, and the
\operatorname{MMD}
metrizes the weak topology between probability measures
(
Sriperumbudur et al., 2010
;
Sriperumbudur, 2016
)
.
Given samples
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
and
(y_{1},\dots,y_{m})
from
\mu
and
\pi
respectively, the
\operatorname{MMD}
can be consistently estimated in multiple ways
(
Gretton et al., 2012
)
. For instance, one can compute its “plug-in” estimator, e.g.
\operatorname{MMD}(\hat{\mu}\|\hat{\pi})
, where
\widehat{\mu}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}}
and
\widehat{\pi}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{y_{i}}
.

The
\chi^{2}
-divergence — a member of the family of
f
-divergences
(
Rényi, 1961
)
— is defined as
the variance of the Radon-Nikodym derivative
\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}
under
\pi
:

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(\mu\|\pi)\coloneqq\int\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{d\pi}-1\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}\pi,

when
\mu\ll\pi
, and
+\infty
otherwise.
The
\chi^{2}
-divergence has a variational form
(
Nowozin et al., 2016
;
Nguyen et al., 2010
)
:

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(\mu\|\pi)=\sup_{h\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int hd\mu-\int\left(h+\frac{1}{4}h^{2}\right)d\pi,

where
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denote the set of all measurable functions from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{R}
.
When
\mu\ll\pi
, we prove in
Lemma

B.1
that the optimal
h^{\ast}=\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\in L^{2}(\pi)
so that it is sufficient to restrict the variational set to
L^{2}(\pi)
in contrast to
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for general
f
-divergences. Since in most cases,
\chi^{2}(\hat{\mu}\|\hat{\pi})=+\infty
, the
\chi^{2}
-divergence does not admit plug-in estimators, and estimating it consistently involves more complicated strategies
(
Nguyen et al., 2010
)
.

2.4
Wasserstein gradient flows

Gradient flows are dynamics that use local (e.g.
differential
)
information about a given functional in order to minimize it as fast as possible.
Their exact definition depends on the nature of the input space;
in the familiar case of Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the gradient flow of a sufficiently regular
F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
given some initial condition
x_{0}
is given by the solution
(x_{t})_{t\geq 0}
of
\partial_{t}x_{t}=-v(x_{t})
,
where
v
is the Fréchet subdifferential of
F
, a generalization of the notion of derivative to non-smooth functions
(
Kruger, 2003
)
.

Gradient flows can be extended from Euclidean spaces to the more general class of
metric spaces

(
Ambrosio et al., 2005
)
.
When the metric space in question is
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
endowed with
the Wasserstein-
2
distance, this gradient flow is called the Wasserstein gradient flow
(\mu_{t})_{t\geq 0}
. The Wasserstein gradient flow of
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
takes the particular form
(
Ambrosio et al., 2005
, Lemma 10.4.1)
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\nabla\cdot\left(\mu_{t}v_{t}\right)=0,

(2)

where
v_{t}
is the Fréchet subdifferential of
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
evaluated at
\mu_{t}

(
Ambrosio et al., 2005
, Definition 11.1.1)
. (
2
) is an instance of the
continuity equation
with velocity field
v_{t}
: under these dynamics, the mass of
\mu_{t}
is transported in the direction
v_{t}
that decreases
\mathcal{F}
at the fastest rate at each time
t
.
While (
2
) can be time-discretized in various ways
(
Santambrogio, 2017
;
Ambrosio et al., 2005
)
, in this work, we will focus on the
forward Euler
scheme, defined as
\mu_{n+1}\coloneqq(\mathrm{I}-\gamma v_{n})_{\#}\mu_{n}
where
\gamma>0
is a step size parameter. Such a scheme is also known as the
Wasserstein Gradient Descent
of
\mathcal{F}
.

Just as gradient descent in Euclidean spaces, an instrumental property to characterize the convergence of the Wasserstein gradient descent of a functional
\mathcal{F}
is given by its
geodesic

convexity and smoothness. Among various ways, one can consider to characterize convexity and smoothness through lower and upper bounds on the Wasserstein Hessian of the functional
\mathcal{F}

(
Villani and others, 2009
, Proposition 16.2)
.
Given any
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
that defines a constant speed geodesic
2
2

2

See
Appendix A: Further Background on
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2})
for the definition.
starting at
\mu
:
\rho_{t}=(\mathrm{I}+t\nabla\phi)_{\#}\mu
for
0\leq t\leq 1
, the Wasserstein Hessian of a functional
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
at
\mu
, denoted as
\operatorname{Hess}\mathcal{F}_{\mid\mu}
, is an operator from
L^{2}(\mu)
to
L^{2}(\mu)
:
3
3

3

Strictly speaking,
\operatorname{Hess}\mathcal{F}_{\mid\mu}
is an operator over the tangent space
\mathcal{T}_{\mu}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
which is a subset of
L^{2}(\mu)

(
Villani and others, 2009
)
.

\displaystyle\langle\operatorname{Hess}\mathcal{F}_{\mid\mu}\nabla\phi,\nabla\phi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}=\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\Big|_{t=0}\mathcal{F}(\rho_{t}).

A functional
\mathcal{F}
is said to be
geodesically

M
-smooth at
\mu
if
\langle\operatorname{Hess}\mathcal{F}_{\mid\mu}\nabla\phi,\nabla\phi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq M\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
, and is said to be
geodesically

\Lambda
-convex at
\mu
if
\langle\operatorname{Hess}\mathcal{F}_{\mid\mu}\nabla\phi,\nabla\phi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}\geq\Lambda\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
. Additionally,
\mathcal{F}
is
geodesically semiconvex
if
-\infty<\Lambda<0
and
geodesically strongly convex
if
\Lambda>0
.
Generally, a functional
\mathcal{F}
that is both smooth and strongly convex is preferred, because its Wasserstein gradient descent has an exponential rate of convergence under a small enough step size
\gamma

(
Boyd and Vandenberghe, 2004
, Section 9.3.1)
(
Bonet et al., 2024
)
.

Given some probability measure
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the
\operatorname{MMD}
flow (
resp.

\chi^{2}
flow) is the Wasserstein gradient flow of the functional
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{MMD}}(\cdot)=\operatorname{MMD}(\cdot\|\pi)
(
resp.

\mathcal{F}_{\chi^{2}}(\cdot)=\chi^{2}(\cdot\|\pi)
).
As with the
\operatorname{MMD}
, the
\operatorname{MMD}
flow has an analytic finite sample implementation and
may be used to construct generative modeling algorithms
(
Hertrich et al., 2024b
;
Hertrich et al., 2024a
)
.
The Wasserstein Hessian of
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{MMD}}
for smooth kernels is not positively lower bounded
(
Arbel et al., 2019
, Proposition 5)
, however, so
\operatorname{MMD}
flow only converges up to an unknown barrier
(
Arbel et al., 2019
, Theorem 6)
, with global convergence only under a strong (and unverifiable) assumption
(
Arbel et al., 2019
, Proposition 7)
.
More recent works
(
Boufadène and Vialard, 2024
)
have demonstrated the global convergence of the
\operatorname{MMD}
flow when using the
Coulomb kernel
. This kernel is non-smooth, however, which complicates numerical implementations. In contrast, the Wasserstein Hessian of
\mathcal{F}_{\chi^{2}}
is positively lower bounded
(
Ohta and Takatsu, 2011
)
for log-concave targets
\pi
, so
\mathcal{F}_{\chi^{2}}
is
geodesically strongly convex
and
\chi^{2}
flow enjoys exponential rate of convergence.
The exponential convergence of
\chi^{2}
flow towards the global minimum in fact holds for a broader class of targets
\pi
that satisfy a Poincaré inequality
(
Chewi et al., 2020
)
.
The
\chi^{2}
flow has so far lacked a tractable sample-based implementation, however, so it has not been widely used in practice.

In the following sections, we will introduce a new Wasserstein gradient flow that combines the computational advantages of the MMD flow with the convergence properties of the
\chi^{2}
flow.

3
(De)-regularized Maximum Mean Discrepancy (
\operatorname{DrMMD}
)

In this section, we introduce a (de)-regularized version of maximum mean discrepancy, or
\operatorname{DrMMD}
in short.
The
\operatorname{DrMMD}
is rooted in a unified representation of
the MMD and the
\chi^{2}
-divergence, given in the following proposition, which is proved in
Section

10.1
.

Proposition 3.1

(
\operatorname{MMD}
and
\chi^{2}
-divergence)
.

Suppose

\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\in L^{2}(\pi)
for
\mu,\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then

\displaystyle\operatorname{MMD}^{2}(\mu\|\pi)=\left\|\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{1/2}\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right)\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\pi)}

\displaystyle\text{ and }\,\,\chi^{2}(\mu\|\pi)=\left\|\mathrm{I}\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right)\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\pi)}.

Remark 3.1
.

The
\chi^{2}
identity follows from the definition but is provided for comparison purposes.
Together, these identities express both the MMD and the
\chi^{2}
-divergence as functionals of the (centered) density ratio
\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1
.
While the
\chi^{2}
-divergence directly
computes the
L^{2}(\pi)
norm of the centred ratio, the
\operatorname{MMD}^{2}
first computes the image by the operator
\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{1/2}
before taking the
L^{2}(\pi)
norm.
The smoothing effect of the compact operator
\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{1/2}
—note that
\mathcal{T}_{\pi}
is compact if
k
is bounded as assumed in
Assumption

1
—has both positive and negative consequences:

\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{MMD}}(\cdot)=\operatorname{MMD}^{2}(\cdot\|\pi)
admits finite sample estimators but is not geodesically convex, making the first-order optimization of MMD objective (as done in generative modeling) challenging
(
Arbel et al., 2019
)
.
In contrast,
\mathcal{F}_{\chi^{2}}(\cdot)=\chi^{2}(\cdot\|\pi)
is geodesically convex for log-concave targets
\pi

(
Ohta and Takatsu, 2011
)
but is hard to estimate with samples.

With these facts in mind, we introduce a divergence whose purpose is to combine the beneficial properties of both the
\chi^{2}
-divergence and
\operatorname{MMD}
.
To do so, this divergence computes the
L^{2}(\pi)
norm of the image of
\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1
by an alternative operator which interpolates between
\mathrm{I}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{1/2}
. We set this operator to be
((\mathcal{T}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I})^{-1}\mathcal{T}_{\pi})^{1/2}
, where
\lambda>0
is a regularization parameter.
The operator
((\mathcal{T}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I})^{-1}\mathcal{T}_{\pi})^{1/2}
can be seen as a (de)-regularization of the operator
\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{1/2}
used by the MMD—- a similar idea has been used in kernel Fisher discriminant
analysis
(
Mika et al., 1999
)
, goodness-of-fit testing
(
Balasubramanian et al., 2021
;
Hagrass et al., 2024b
)
, and two-sample testing
(
Harchaoui et al., 2007
;
Hagrass et al., 2024a
)
. We call the resulting divergence the (De)-regularized Maximum Mean Discrepancy (
\operatorname{DrMMD}
).

Definition 1

(
\operatorname{DrMMD}
)
.

Suppose
\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\in L^{2}(\pi)
where
\mu,\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Then the (de)-regularized maximum mean discrepancy (
\operatorname{DrMMD}
) between
\mu,\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is defined as

\displaystyle\operatorname{DrMMD}(\mu||\pi)

\displaystyle=(1+\lambda)\left\|\left((\mathcal{T}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I})^{-1}\mathcal{T}_{\pi}\right)^{1/2}\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2},

(3)

where
\lambda>0
.

While all three operators,

\mathrm{I}
,
\mathcal{T}_{\pi}
and
(\mathcal{T}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I})^{-1}\mathcal{T}_{\pi}
are diagonalizable in the same eigenbasis of
L^{2}(\pi)
, the key difference between them lies in the behavior of their eigenvalues. The identity operator
\mathrm{I}
has all eigenvalues
1
, the integral operator
\mathcal{T}_{\pi}
has eigenvalues
(\varrho_{i})_{i\geq 1}
which decay to zero as
i\rightarrow\infty
and the (de)-regularized integral operator
(\mathcal{T}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I})^{-1}\mathcal{T}_{\pi}
has eigenvalues
\left(\varrho_{i}/(\varrho_{i}+\lambda)\right)_{i\geq 1}
which either decay to zero or converge to 1 depending on the choice of
\lambda
as
i\rightarrow\infty
.

(\mathcal{T}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I})^{-1}\mathcal{T}_{\pi}
is known in the statistical estimation literature as Tikhonov regularization; alternative definitions of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
could be obtained by using other regularizing operators, such as Showalter regularization in
Engl et al. (1996)
. In this paper, we primarily focus on Tikhonov regularization and leave other types of regularization for future work.

One of the stated purposes of the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
is to retain the computational benefits of the MMD, which are crucial for its use in particle algorithms for generative modeling.
To this end,
we provide an alternative representation of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
which does not involve the density ratio
\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}
directly, but only kernel expectations.

Proposition 3.2

(Density ratio–free and variational formulations)
.

\operatorname{DrMMD}
can be alternately represented as

\displaystyle\operatorname{DrMMD}(\mu||\pi)

\displaystyle=(1+\lambda)\left\|\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(m_{\mu}-m_{\pi}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

(4)

\displaystyle=(1+\lambda)\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\left\{\int h\mathrm{~d}\mu-\int\left(\frac{h^{2}}{4}+h\right)\mathrm{d}\pi-\frac{\lambda}{4}\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right\}

(5)

with
h_{\mu,\pi}^{\ast}=2\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1}(m_{\mu}-m_{\pi})
being the witness function.

The proof is in
Section

10.2
.
The density ratio-free representation (
4
) contains three expectations under
\mu
and
\pi
: the mean embeddings
m_{\mu}
and
m_{\pi}
, and the covariance operator
\Sigma_{\pi}
.
Given samples
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{M}\sim\mu
and
\{y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}\sim\pi
, we can construct a plug-in finite sample estimator in (
71
) by replacing
\mu
and
\pi
with their empirical counterparts:
\hat{\mu}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\delta_{x_{i}}
and
\hat{\pi}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{y_{i}}
.

The density ratio-free representation (
4
) frames
\operatorname{DrMMD}
as acting on the difference of
m_{\mu}
and
m_{\pi}
similarly to MMD.
In fact, up to a multiplicative factor
(1+\lambda)
,
\operatorname{DrMMD}
is
\operatorname{MMD}
computed with respect to another kernel
\tilde{k}
defined as

\displaystyle\tilde{k}(x,x^{\prime})=\left\langle\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}k(x,\cdot),\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}k(x^{\prime},\cdot)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}.

(6)

The derivations are provided in
Section

10.3
. The kernel
\tilde{k}
is symmetric and positive semi-definite by construction, and its associated reproducing kernel Hilbert space is
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}
. The density ratio-free representation of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
in
Proposition

3.2
is already known in
(
Balasubramanian et al., 2021
;
Harchaoui et al., 2007
;
Hagrass et al., 2024a
;
Hagrass et al., 2024b
)
in the context of non-parametric hypothesis testing.

3.1
Properties of
\operatorname{DrMMD}

In this section, we establish various properties of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
.
As discussed earlier,
\operatorname{DrMMD}
is constructed to interpolate between
\chi^{2}
-divergence and MMD to exploit the advantages associated with each. The following result formalizes the interpolation property.

Proposition 3.3

(Interpolation property)
.

Let
\mu,\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. If
Assumption

1
holds and
\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\in L^{2}(\pi)
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0}\operatorname{DrMMD}(\mu\|\pi)=\chi^{2}(\mu\|\pi),\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow\infty}\operatorname{DrMMD}(\mu\|\pi)=\mathrm{MMD}^{2}(\mu\|\pi).

Proposition

3.3
, whose proof can be found in
Section

10.4
, shows that
\operatorname{DrMMD}
asymptotically becomes a probability divergence in the small and large
\lambda
regimes.
We seek to use
\operatorname{DrMMD}
as a minimizing objective in generative modeling algorithms; however, we need to ensure that
\operatorname{DrMMD}
is a probability divergence for
any
fixed value of
\lambda
. This result holds, as shown next.

Proposition 3.4

(
\operatorname{DrMMD}
is a probability divergence)
.

Under
Assumption

1
,
for any
\lambda\in\left(0,\infty\right)
,
\operatorname{DrMMD}
is a probability divergence, i.e.,
\operatorname{DrMMD}(\mu\|\pi)\geq 0
, with equality iff
\mu=\pi
.
Moreover,
\operatorname{DrMMD}
metrizes the weak topology between probability measures, i.e.,
\operatorname{DrMMD}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\to 0
iff
\mu_{n}
converges weakly to
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

As MMD with
c_{0}
-universal kernels metrizes the weak convergence of distributions
(
Sriperumbudur, 2016
;
Simon-Gabriel et al., 2023
)
,
Proposition

3.4
, whose proof can be found in
Section

10.5
, shows that for any
\lambda>0
,
\operatorname{DrMMD}
is “MMD–like” topologically speaking, and is different from the
\chi^{2}
-divergence which induces a strong topology
(
Agrawal and Horel, 2021
)
.

Remark 3.2
.

\operatorname{DrMMD}
is a specific case of a so-called “Moreau envelopes of
f
-divergences in reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces” introduced in
Stein et al. (2025)
, when the
f
-divergence is taken as the
\chi^{2}
-divergence. This connection is uncovered by the variational formulation of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
in (
5
).
In contrast to general
f
-divergences, the Moreau envelope of the
\chi^{2}
-divergence enjoys a closed-form expression, as we highlight in this paper with various analytical formulas for
\operatorname{DrMMD}
.
The interpolation property (
Proposition

3.3
) and the metrization of weak convergence (
Proposition

3.4
) are proved concurrently in Corollaries 12 and 13 of
Stein et al. (2025)
, relying on formulation via Moreau envelopes.
In our case, we use direct computations thanks to the closed form of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
.
4
4

4

We would like to clarify that
Stein et al. (2025)
appeared on arxiv when our paper was already under review at ICML 2024.

4
Wasserstein Gradient Flow of
\operatorname{DrMMD}

Having introduced the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
in the previous section, we now construct and analyze its
Wasserstein Gradient Flow
(WGF).
As discussed in
Section

2
, WGFs define dynamics
(\mu_{t})_{t\geq 0}
in Wasserstein-2 space that minimize a given functional
\mathcal{F}
by transporting
\mu_{t}
in the direction of steepest descent, given by the
Fréchet subdifferential
of
\mathcal{F}
evaluated at
\mu_{t}
.
Given some target distribution
\pi
from which we wish to sample, the WGF of
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{DrMMD}}(\cdot)=\operatorname{DrMMD}(\cdot\|\pi)
, called
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow, has the potential to form the basis of a generative modeling algorithm, since
\mu_{t}
progressively minimizes its distance (in the DrMMD sense) to the target
\pi
.

To fulfill this potential, two additional ingredients are necessary. The first is to formally establish that
\mu_{t}
reaches the global minimizer
\pi
, and
the second is to design a tractable finite-sample algorithm that inherits the convergence properties of the original
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow.
We defer the second point to
Section

5
and focus in this section on showing how
\operatorname{DrMMD}
benefits from its interpolation towards
\chi^{2}
-divergence such that the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow achieves near-global convergence for a large class of target distributions.

4.1
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow: Definition, existence, and uniqueness

To prove that the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow is well-defined and admits solutions, the key is to show that the
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{DrMMD}}
admits Fréchet subdifferentials, as formalized in the following proposition.

Proposition 4.1

(
\operatorname{DrMMD}
gradient flow)
.

Let
\lambda>0
, and
\mu_{0},\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)
. Under
Assumption

1
and
2
, the functional
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{DrMMD}}
admits Fréchet subdifferential of the form
(1+\lambda)\nabla h_{\mu_{t},\pi}^{\ast}
, where
h_{\mu_{t},\pi}^{\ast}
is the witness function defined in
Proposition

3.2
. Consequently, the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow is well-defined and is the solution to the following equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mu_{t}-\nabla\cdot\left(\mu_{t}(1+\lambda)\nabla h_{\mu_{t},\pi}^{\ast}\right)=0.

(7)

In addition, the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow starting at
\mu_{0}
is unique because
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{DrMMD}}
is semiconvex, i.e., for any
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\operatorname{Hess}{\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{DrMMD}}}_{\mid\mu}\nabla\phi,\nabla\phi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}\right|\leq 2(1+\lambda)\frac{2\sqrt{KK_{2d}}+K_{1d}}{\lambda}\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}.

(8)

The proof is in
Section

10.6
.
By recalling the discussion of Wasserstein Hessian in
Section

2.4
, (
8
) indicates that
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{DrMMD}}
is both
geodesically smooth
and
geodesically semiconvex
, which is expected because
\operatorname{DrMMD}
is equivalent to
\operatorname{MMD}
with a regularized kernel
\tilde{k}
defined in (
6
) and
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{MMD}}
is both
geodesically smooth
and
geodesically semiconvex

(
Arbel et al., 2019
, Proposition 5)
.

Proposition

4.1
is proved concurrently in Corollaries 14 and 20 of
Stein et al. (2025)
relying on formulation via Moreau envelopes, while our proof uses the closed-form expression for
\operatorname{DrMMD}
.

4.2
Near-global convergence of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow

Having defined the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow, we are now concerned with its convergence to the target
\pi
.
Since
\operatorname{DrMMD}
is constructed to interpolate between the
\operatorname{MMD}
and the
\chi^{2}
-divergence,
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow is expected to recover the convergence properties of the
\chi^{2}
flow.
With this goal in mind, we first study the Wasserstein Hessian of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
and prove that it becomes
asymptotically
positive as
\lambda\to 0
for strongly log-concave targets
\pi
.
Next, to obtain a
non-asymptotic
convergence rate, we take another route and show that the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow converges to
\pi
exponentially fast in KL divergence up to a barrier term that vanishes in the small
\lambda
regime, provided that
\pi
satisfies a Poincaré inequality.

4.2.1
Near-Geodesic Convexity of
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{DrMMD}}

One popular approach to proving that the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow
(\mu_{t})_{t\geq 0}
converges to the target
\pi
in terms of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
is to show that
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{DrMMD}}
is
geodesically convex

(
Ambrosio et al., 2005
, Theorem 4.0.4)
, or, equivalently in our definition in
Section

2.4
, its Wasserstein Hessian is positive definite.
In the next proposition, we show that the Wasserstein Hessian of
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{DrMMD}}
is indeed positive definite for small enough
\lambda
; however, as we will see below (
Remark

4.2
), the form of the result will not allow us to show convergence besides in the limit
\lambda\rightarrow 0
, which leads us to take a different approach in subsequent sections.

Proposition 4.2

(Near-geodesic convexity of
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{DrMMD}}
)
.

Let
\mu,\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\mu,\pi\ll\mathcal{L}^{d}
and
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Under
Assumption

1
and
2
,
let
\pi
be
\alpha
strongly log-concave, i.e.,
\pi\propto\exp(-V)
,
\mathbf{H}V\succeq\alpha\mathrm{I}
, and assume additionally that
x\mapsto\mathbf{H}V(x)
is continuous. Then for all
\mu
such that
x\mapsto\nabla\log\mu(x)
is continuous and
\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\in\mathcal{H}
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\operatorname{Hess}{\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{DrMMD}}}_{\mid\mu}\nabla\phi,\nabla\phi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}\geq\alpha(1+\lambda)\int\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}(x)\|\nabla\phi(x)\|^{2}d\mu(x)-R(\lambda,\mu,\nabla\phi),

(9)

where
\lim_{\lambda\to 0}R(\lambda,\mu,\nabla\phi)=0
.

The proof can be found in
Section

10.7
.
To obtain this result, we relate the Wasserstein Hessian of
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{DrMMD}}
with that of
\mathcal{F}_{\chi^{2}}
.
When
\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\in\mathcal{H}
, they coincide asymptotically as
\lambda\to 0
, showing that the interpolation properties of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
to the
\chi^{2}
-divergence hold at the level of Wasserstein derivatives.
Together with the fact that the Wasserstein Hessian of
\mathcal{F}_{\chi^{2}}
is positive definite for
\alpha
-strongly log-concave
\pi

(
Ohta and Takatsu, 2011
)
, we obtain the lower bound in (
9
).
It is noteworthy that although
\operatorname{DrMMD}
can be viewed as squared
\operatorname{MMD}
with a regularized kernel
\tilde{k}
, the near-geodesic convexity in
Proposition

4.2
is not observed for standard
\operatorname{MMD}
with a fixed kernel
k
because the latter does not interpolate towards
\chi^{2}
-divergence.

Remark 4.1

(Geodesic convexity/smoothness trade-off in
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{DrMMD}}
)
.

The geodesic

smoothness and near-convexity of the functional
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{DrMMD}}
are characterized by (
8
) and (
9
) respectively via upper and lower bounds on the Wasserstein Hessian of
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{DrMMD}}
. However, (
8
) and (
9
)
impose
contradictory
conditions on the (de)-regularization parameter
\lambda
: (
8
) indicates that
\operatorname{DrMMD}
is smoother if
\lambda
is larger while (
9
) indicates that
\operatorname{DrMMD}
is more convex if
\lambda
is small enough.
Consequently, there is a trade-off between the geodesic convexity and smoothness of
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{DrMMD}}
, which will play an important role in
Section

5
.

Remark 4.2
.

Proposition

4.2
shows that for fixed
\mu
and
\nabla\phi
, there exists
\lambda
small enough yet positive such that

\left\langle\operatorname{Hess}{\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{DrMMD}}}_{\mid\mu}\nabla\phi,\nabla\phi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0
at
\mu
.
The remainder term
R(\lambda,\mu,\nabla\phi)
is only controlled in the limit as
\lambda\rightarrow 0
, however, which complicates the use of
Proposition

4.2
to show global convergence of the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow. In the next section, we employ a different set of techniques that rely on the Poincaré condition on
\pi
, a condition which, as we show, will ensure a sufficient dissipation of KL divergence along the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow to obtain
non-asymptotic
near-global convergence.

4.2.2
Near-Global Convergence of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow via Poincaré inequality

Even when a functional
\mathcal{F}
is not geodesically convex, convergence guarantees for its Wasserstein gradient flow
(\mu_{t})_{t\geq 0}
can still be obtained if the target
\pi
satisfies certain functional inequalities. Consider the
\chi^{2}
flow
(\nu_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, for example: if
\pi
satisfies the Poincaré inequality, then
(\nu_{t})_{t\geq 0}
converges exponentially fast to
\pi
in terms of KL divergence
(
Chewi et al., 2020
, Theorem 1)
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\operatorname{KL}(\nu_{T}\|\pi)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{2T}{C_{P}}\right)\operatorname{KL}(\nu_{0}\|\pi).

(10)

Recall that
\pi
satisfies a Poincaré inequality
(
Pillaud-Vivien et al., 2020
, Definition 1)
if
for all functions
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
f,\nabla f\in L^{2}(\pi)
, there exists a constant
C_{P}>0
such that

\int f(x)^{2}d\pi(x)-\left(\int f(x)d\pi(x)\right)^{2}\leq C_{P}\|\nabla f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\pi)}.

(11)

The smallest constant
C_{P}
for which (
11
) holds is called the Poincaré constant.
The Poincaré inequality is widely used for studying the convergence of Langevin diffusions
(
Chewi et al., 2024
)
and
\chi^{2}
flow
(
Chewi et al., 2020
;
Trillos and Sanz-Alonso, 2020
)
.
The Poincaré condition is implied by the strong log-concavity of
\pi
, and is weaker than strong log-concavity because it also allows for nonconvex potentials.
The set of probability measures satisfying a Poincaré inequality includes distributions with sub-gaussian tails or with exponential tails. This set is also closed under bounded perturbations and finite mixtures (see
Vempala and Wibisono (2019)
and
Chewi et al. (2024)
for a more detailed discussion).

Given the interpolation property of DrMMD to
\chi^{2}
-divergence, (
10
) suggests investigating the convergence of the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow
(\mu_{t})_{t\geq 0}
in KL divergence under a Poincaré inequality.
To this end, we first derive an upper bound on
\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{t}\|\pi)
along the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow.

Theorem 4.1

(KL control of the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow)
.

Suppose
k
satisfies Assumptions
1
and
2
, and the target
\pi
and
\operatorname{DrMMD}
gradient flow
\left(\mu_{t}\right)_{t\geq 0}
satisfy the following conditions:

1.

\pi
satisfies a Poincaré inequality with constant
C_{P}
.

2.

\mu_{t},\pi\ll\mathcal{L}^{d}
.

3.

\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}-1\in\operatorname{Ran}(\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{r})
with
r>0
, i.e., there exists
q_{t}\in L^{2}(\pi)
such that
\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}-1=\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{r}q_{t}
.

4.

\left\|\nabla\left(\log\pi\right)^{\top}\nabla\left(\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}\leq\mathcal{J}_{t}
and
\left\|\Delta\left(\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}\leq\mathcal{I}_{t}
.

5.

For all
i=1,\ldots,d
,
\lim\limits_{x\to\infty}\left(h_{\mu_{t},\pi}^{\ast}(x)-2\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)\right)\left(\partial_{i}\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)\right)\pi(x)\to 0
.

Then, for any
T\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{T}\|\pi)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{2(1+\lambda)}{C_{P}}T\right)\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{0}\|\pi)

\displaystyle+4(1+\lambda)\lambda^{r}\int_{0}^{T}\exp\left(-\frac{2(1+\lambda)}{C_{P}}(T-t)\right)\left\|q_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}(\mathcal{J}_{t}+\mathcal{I}_{t})dt.

(12)

The proof, which can be found in
Section

10.8
, leverages the fact that the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
can approximate not only the
\chi^{2}
-divergence, but also its
Wasserstein gradient
. The
\operatorname{DrMMD}
’s approximation properties can be combined with functional inequalities to obtain an upper-bound for the continuous-time dissipation of KL divergence along the flow, given by:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{t}\|\pi)\leq-\frac{2(1+\lambda)}{C_{P}}\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{t}\|\pi)+\underbrace{4(1+\lambda)\lambda^{r}\left\|q_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}\left(\mathcal{J}_{t}+\mathcal{I}_{t}\right)}_{\text{Approximation error}},

(13)

from which
Theorem

4.1
follows upon applying the Growall’s lemma
(
Gronwall, 1919
)
.
The first term is strictly negative, while the second term is an approximation error term arising from
\operatorname{DrMMD}
not perfectly matching the
\chi^{2}
-divergence for
\lambda>0
.
When
\lambda=0
,
Theorem

4.1
recovers the exponential decay of KL divergence along
\chi^{2}
flow in (
10
).

Remark 4.3
.

(i) The second condition assumes that
\mu_{t}
and
\pi
have densities.

(ii) The third condition that
\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}-1\in\operatorname{Ran}(\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{r})
is a regularity condition on the density ratio so that it can be well approximated by the witness function
h_{\mu_{t},\pi}^{\ast}
.
This assumption is known as the range assumption in the literature of kernel ridge regression
(
Cucker and Zhou, 2007
;
Fischer and Steinwart, 2020
)
.
We posit that this assumption can be relaxed to
\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}-1\in L^{2}(\pi)
as in
Proposition

3.3
if only asymptotic convergence is needed with no explicit rate.

(iii) The fourth condition is another regularity condition on the density ratio along the flow. This condition is automatically satisfied under a stronger range condition (
r=\frac{1}{2}
) in the third condition, i.e.,
\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}-1\in\mathcal{H}
, along with a moment condition on the score function
\nabla\log\pi
.
To see this, notice that we can further write (derivations are provided in
Section

10.8
)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \left\|\nabla\left(\log\pi\right)^{\top}\nabla\left(\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}&\leq\sqrt{K_{1d}}\|q_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}\left\|\nabla\log\pi\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}\\
\left\|\Delta\left(\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}&\leq\sqrt{K_{2d}}\|q_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}.\end{aligned}

(14)

As an illustration, we examine the DrMMD flow under a Gaussian approximation in
Appendix C: An Illustrative Example for Explicit Forms of
\mathcal{I}_{t}
,
\mathcal{J}_{t}
,
\|q_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}
, following
Lambert et al. (2022)
;
Liu et al. (2024b)
, with a Gaussian kernel and Gaussian target
\pi
, for which explicit upper bounds
\mathcal{I}_{t}
and
\mathcal{J}_{t}
can be derived.

(iv) The fifth condition is a boundary condition that allows integration by parts equality in the proof. We highlight that many works (e.g., Theorem 1 of
He et al. (2024)
, Theorem 2 of
Nitanda et al. (2022)
, Lemma 6 of
Vempala and Wibisono (2019)
) on Wasserstein gradient flow apply integration by parts without explicitly stating this condition.

Additionally, if
\|q_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}\leq Q
,
\mathcal{J}_{t}\leq\mathcal{J},\,\mathcal{I}_{t}\leq\mathcal{I}
for all
0\leq t\leq T
, then
Theorem

4.1
will ensure KL convergence of the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow up to a controllable barrier term, e.g.
near global convergence
.

Corollary 4.1

(Near global convergence of the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow)
.

In addition to the assumptions of
Theorem

4.1
, if
\left\|q_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}\leq Q
,
\mathcal{J}_{t}\leq\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}_{t}\leq\mathcal{I}
for all
0\leq t\leq T
, where
Q,\mathcal{J},
and
\mathcal{I}
are universal constants independent of
\lambda
, then for any
T\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{T}\|\pi)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{2(1+\lambda)}{C_{P}}T\right)\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{0}\|\pi)+2\lambda^{r}C_{P}Q\left(\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I}\right).

The proof of
Corollary

4.1
follows directly from upper-bounding the second term of (
12
) with universal constants
\mathcal{Q}
,
\mathcal{J}
, and
\mathcal{I}
, and using the closed-form expression of the resulting integral.

Corollary

4.1
provides a condition under which the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow will exhibit an exponential rate of convergence (linear convergence) in terms of KL divergence up to an extra approximation error term which vanishes as
\lambda\to 0
.
If
\left\|q_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}\leq Q,\mathcal{J}_{t}\leq\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}_{t}\leq\mathcal{I}
for all
0\leq t\leq T
as shown in
Corollary

4.1
, the approximation error is of explicit order
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{r})
. Therefore, in the continuous time regime, to have a smaller approximation error, it is beneficial to use a small (de)-regularization parameter
\lambda
, so that
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow operates closer to the regime of
\chi^{2}
flow.
However, as we will see in the next section, when it comes to time-discretized
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow, i.e.,
\operatorname{DrMMD}
gradient descent, there is a trade-off between the approximation error and the time discretization error such that the selection of
\lambda
would require more careful analysis to strike a good balance.
Finally, a smaller Poincaré constant
C_{P}
results in both a faster rate of convergence and a smaller barrier.

Previously,
Arbel et al. (2019)
established (sublinear) global convergence of the
\operatorname{MMD}
flow in terms of
\operatorname{MMD}
distance by assuming that a Lojasiewicz inequality (or a variant of it if additionally performing noise injection, see
Arbel et al., 2018
, Proposition 8
) holds along the flow.
Our result thus complements that of
Arbel et al. (2019)
by showing that MMD-type functionals can achieve near-global convergence for targets satisfying a Poincaré inequality regardless of whether such inequalities hold, by studying their behavior in the
f
-divergence interpolation regime.

Remark 4.4
.

Since
\operatorname{DrMMD}
is asymmetric in its arguments, the reader may wonder why we focus on the gradient flow of
\operatorname{DrMMD}(\cdot||\pi)
instead of
\operatorname{DrMMD}(\pi||\cdot)
.
From the convergence standpoint,
Theorem

4.1
shows that
\operatorname{DrMMD}(\cdot||\pi)
converges globally with an exponential rate up to a small barrier when
\pi
satisfies Poincaré inequality along with an extra regularity condition on the density ratio.
This favorable convergence property is no longer true for
\operatorname{DrMMD}(\pi||\cdot)
.
Practically speaking, the Wasserstein gradient of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
requires inverting a kernel integral operator.
It is more efficient to do this just once with a kernel integral operator with respect to
\pi
as in (
3
), rather than with respect to
\mu_{t}
, which would happen at each time (as done by
He et al. 2024
).

Computing the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow is intractable since the dynamics are in continuous-time, and in practice, we do not have access to
\pi
, but only samples from it.
In the next two sections, we build a tractable approximation of the DrMMD flow which provably achieves near-global convergence under similar assumptions as the ones in
Section

4
. Compared to the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow, this approximation combines a discretization in time (introduced in
Section

5
) with a particle-based space-discretization (introduced in
Section

6
). While the space-discretization techniques that we used are well-known in the Wasserstein gradient flow literature, our time-discretized scheme deviates from standard approaches, which are insufficient to guarantee near-global convergence in our case.

5
Time-discretized
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow

In this section, we first construct and analyze the forward Euler scheme of (
7
), a simple time-discretization of
the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow which we call
\operatorname{DrMMD}
Descent. Compared to the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow, the convergence of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
descent is affected by an additional smoothness-related time-discretization error that blows up as
\lambda
approaches 0, thus preventing near-global convergence.
To address this issue, we propose in
Section

5.2
an alternative discrete-time scheme that adapts the value of the regularization coefficient
\lambda
across the descent iterates, which we call
Adaptive
\operatorname{DrMMD}
Descent
. We show that this scheme converges in the KL divergence up to a barrier term that vanishes as the discretization step size goes to zero.

5.1
\operatorname{DrMMD}
Descent

The forward Euler discretization of the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow (or
\operatorname{DrMMD}

Descent
in short) with

step size
\gamma>0
consists of sequence of probabilities
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
defined by the recursion

\displaystyle\mu_{n+1}=\left(\mathrm{I}-\gamma(1+\lambda)\nabla h_{\mu_{n},\pi}^{\ast}\right)_{\#}\mu_{n},\quad\mu_{n=0}=\mu_{0},

(15)

where
h_{\mu_{n},\pi}^{\ast}=2\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1}\left(m_{\mu_{n}}-m_{\pi}\right)
is the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
witness function.
This scheme was previously considered in
Arbel et al. (2019)
; in particular, Proposition 4 of
Arbel et al. (2019)
shows that the discrete-time
\operatorname{MMD}
dissipation rate along the
\operatorname{MMD}
Descent iterates follows the (continous time) rate of the
\operatorname{MMD}
flow up to an error term proportional to the step size
\gamma
and the smoothness parameters of the problem
5
5

5

In the
\operatorname{MMD}
flow case, the main smoothness parameters is the Lipschitz constant of the kernel.
.

Next, we turn to study the convergence of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
descent. One way is to treat
\operatorname{DrMMD}
as
\operatorname{MMD}
with a regularized kernel
\tilde{k}
and follow Proposition 4 of
Arbel et al. (2019)
, however this does not quantitatively take into account the role of (de)-regularization parameter
\lambda
that balances the trade-off between geodesic convexity and smoothness of
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{DrMMD}}
.
Instead, we adopt the same strategy of
Section

4
that exploits the interpolation property of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
towards
\chi^{2}
-divergence: in the following proposition, we study the dissipation of KL divergence along the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
Descent when the target
\pi
satisfies a Poincaré inequality, in which the role of (de)-regularization parameter
\lambda
is highlighted in the approximation and discretization errors.

Proposition 5.1

(Descent lemma in KL)
.

Suppose
k
satisfies
Assumption

1
and
2
, and suppose the target
\pi
and
\operatorname{DrMMD}
gradient descent iterates
\left(\mu_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfy the following:

1.

\pi
satisfies a Poincaré inequality with constant
C_{P}
and its potential is
\beta
-smooth, i.e.,
\pi\propto\exp(-V)
with
\mathbf{H}V\preceq\beta\mathrm{I}
.

2.

\mu_{n},\pi\ll\mathcal{L}^{d}
.

3.

\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}-1\in\operatorname{Ran}(\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{r})
with
r>0
, i.e., there exists
q_{n}\in L^{2}(\pi)
such that
\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}-1=\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{r}q_{n}
.

4.

\|q_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}\leq Q
,
\left\|\nabla V^{\top}\nabla\left(\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}\leq\mathcal{J}
,
\left\|\Delta\left(\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}\leq\mathcal{I}
for all
n=1,\cdots,n_{max}
.

5.

For all
i=1,\ldots,d
,
\lim\limits_{x\to\infty}\left(h_{\mu_{n},\pi}^{\ast}(x)-2\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}(x)\right)\left(\partial_{i}\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}(x)\right)\pi(x)\to 0
.

6.

There exists a constant
1<\zeta<2
such that for all
n=1,\ldots,n_{\max}
, the step size
\gamma
satisfies

\displaystyle\gamma\leq\frac{\zeta-1}{2\zeta(1+\lambda)\sqrt{\chi^{2}\left(\mu_{n}\|\pi\right)\frac{K_{2d}}{\lambda}}}.

(16)

Then for all
0\leq n\leq n_{\max}
and
0<\lambda\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n+1}\|\pi)-\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)&\leq-\frac{2}{C_{P}}\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\gamma\\
&+\underbrace{4\gamma\lambda^{r}Q\left(\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I}\right)}_{\text{Approximation error}}+\underbrace{8\gamma^{2}(\beta+\zeta^{2})\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\frac{K_{1d}+K_{2d}}{\lambda}}_{\text{Discretization error}}.\end{aligned}

(17)

The proof is provided in
Section

10.9
.
Conditions 1-5 of
Proposition

5.1
are similar to conditions 1-5 of
Theorem

4.1
, which assumes a functional inequality on the target
\pi
and regularity on the density ratio
\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}-1
.
In a similar spirit to
Theorem

4.1
, the fourth regularity condition is automatically satisfied under a stronger range assumption on
\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}-1
.
For the sake of brevity, we directly assume uniform upper bounds in the fourth condition rather than writing it out in a separate corollary like
Corollary

4.1
.
Compared to the continuous time regime, two extra conditions are necessary. The first one is a smoothness condition on the potential,
\mathbf{H}V\preceq\beta\mathrm{I}
, which is commonly used in the convergence analysis of discrete-time Langevin-based samplers
(
Dalalyan, 2017
;
Dalalyan and Karagulyan, 2019
;
Durmus et al., 2019
;
Dalalyan et al., 2022
;
Vempala and Wibisono, 2019
)
.
It can be relaxed to
\nabla V
being Hölder-continuous with exponent
s\in[0,1]

(
Chatterji et al., 2020
)
.
The second one, (
16
), is an upper bound on the step size
\gamma
, aligning with the principle that step size should be small enough for the discrete-time scheme to inherit the properties of its continuous analog. This condition will be more thoroughly discussed in
Remark

5.4
when all the conditions on
\gamma
in
Theorem

5.1
and
Theorem

5.2
are presented.

Remark 5.1

(Approximation-discretization trade-off of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
Descent)
.

If we compare the discrete-time KL dissipation of (
17
) with its continuous-time counterpart in (
13
), we see that the first two terms on the RHS of (
17
) admit continuous-time analogs present in (
13
). The discrete-time KL dissipation contains an additional (positive) term representing the
time discretization error
: unlike the approximation error term that vanishes as
\lambda
approaches 0, this term actually diverges as
\lambda
approaches 0. Therefore, replicating the arguments of the continuous-time result of
Theorem

4.1
in the discrete-time regime would yield a barrier that does not vanish as
\lambda\to 0
, hinting at a trade-off between approximation and discretization similar to that of
Remark

4.1
. In the next section, we propose a refined adaptive discrete-time descent scheme that addresses the convergence issues.

5.2
Adaptive
\operatorname{DrMMD}
Descent

The
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow and descent dynamics are defined for a value of
\lambda
that remains fixed throughout time.
The KL dissipation provided in
Proposition

5.1
is
a function of
\lambda
, however; thus, to obtain a sequence of measures with better convergence guarantees than the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
descent, we now construct and analyze a sequence of iterates obtained by selecting, at each iteration, the value of
\lambda
minimizing the sum of the approximation error and time-discretization error presented in (
17
). This sequence, which we term
Adaptive
\operatorname{DrMMD}
descent
, is given by

\displaystyle\mu_{n+1}=\left(\mathrm{I}-\gamma(1+\lambda)\nabla h_{\mu_{n},\pi}^{\ast}\right)_{\#}\mu_{n},\quad\lambda_{n}=\left(2\gamma\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\frac{(\beta+\zeta^{2})(K_{1d}+K_{2d})}{Q(\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I})}\right)^{\frac{1}{r+1}}.

(18)

The particular choice of
\lambda_{n}
above minimizes the sum of the approximation error and time-discretization error presented in (
17
).
This optimal choice indicates that
\lambda_{n}
should shrink towards
0
as
\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)
decreases along
\operatorname{DrMMD}
gradient descent: at the early stages of the scheme, it is desirable to have a larger
\lambda_{n}
, corresponding to a smoother objective functional and enabling larger step sizes; then as
\operatorname{DrMMD}
gradient descent iterates
\mu_{n}
get closer to
\pi
, a smaller
\lambda_{n}
enables the scheme to operate closer to the
\chi^{2}
flow regime, which metrizes a stronger topology and can better witness the difference between
\mu_{n}
and
\pi
.
Additionally, as the potential
V
becomes less smooth, i.e.,
\beta
gets larger, then
\lambda_{n}
should also increase to account for the loss of smoothness from
V
.
Unlike the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
descent in
Section

5.1
, since the Wasserstein gradient updating
\mu_{n}
comes from a different
\operatorname{DrMMD}
at each iteration, the adaptive scheme of (
18
) constitutes a significant departure from related works in Wasserstein gradient descent
(
Glaser et al., 2021
;
Arbel et al., 2019
;
Korba et al., 2021
;
Hertrich et al., 2024b
;
Hertrich et al., 2023
;
Chewi et al., 2020
)
.

Remark 5.2

(Adaptive kernel)
.

Recent applications of MMD-based generative modeling algorithms with adaptive kernels (in particular, time-dependent kernel hyperparameters) demonstrate improved empirical performance over fixed kernels in both Wasserstein gradient flow on
\operatorname{MMD}

(
Galashov et al., 2025
)
and generative adversarial networks with an
\operatorname{MMD}
critic
(
Li et al., 2017
;
Arbel et al., 2018
)
: the latter can be related to gradient flow on the critic where
\mu_{n}
is restricted to the output of a generator network
(
Franceschi et al., 2024
, see e.g.)
. As the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
is an
\operatorname{MMD}
with a regularized kernel
\tilde{k}
that depends on
\lambda_{n}
, the Adaptive
\operatorname{DrMMD}
Descent thus falls into the former category.

Galashov et al. (2025, Proposition 3.1)
demonstrates faster convergence for
\operatorname{MMD}
gradient flow with an adaptive kernel, for the parametric setting of Gaussian distributions
\pi
and
\mu_{t}
.
Our analysis is the first to prove theoretically that adaptive kernels can result in improved convergence for more general nonparametric settings. We believe that the theoretical analysis of adaptivity by varying other hyperparameters (such as the kernel bandwidth for RBF kernels) remains an interesting avenue for future work.

By leveraging the quasi-descent lemma in KL divergence in
Proposition

5.1
, we are able to establish the following theorem, which provides a near-global convergence result of the Adaptive
\operatorname{DrMMD}
gradient descent iterates in KL divergence.

Theorem 5.1

(Near-global convergence of adaptive
\operatorname{DrMMD}
gradient descent)
.

Suppose
k
satisfies
Assumption

1
and
2
and
K\leq 1
, and suppose the target
\pi
and adaptive
\operatorname{DrMMD}
gradient descent iterates
\left(\mu_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfy the following conditions:

1.

\pi
satisfies a Poincaré inequality with constant
C_{P}
and its potential is
\beta
-smooth, i.e.
\pi\propto\exp(-V)
with
\mathbf{H}V\preceq\beta\mathrm{I}
.

2.

\mu_{n},\pi\ll\mathcal{L}^{d}
.

3.

\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}-1\in\operatorname{Ran}(\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{r})
with
r>0
, i.e., there exists
q_{n}\in L^{2}(\pi)
such that
\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}-1=\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{r}q_{n}
.

4.

\|q_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}\leq Q
,
\left\|\nabla V^{\top}\nabla\left(\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}\leq\mathcal{J}
,
\left\|\Delta\left(\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}\leq\mathcal{I}
for all
n=1,\cdots,n_{max}
.

5.

For all
i=1,\ldots,d
,
\lim\limits_{x\to\infty}\left(h_{\mu_{n},\pi}^{\ast}(x)-2\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}(x)\right)\left(\partial_{i}\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}(x)\right)\pi(x)\to 0
.

6.

There exists a constant
1<\zeta<2
such that the step size
\gamma
satisfies

\displaystyle\gamma\leq\frac{1}{8}\left(\frac{\zeta-1}{\zeta}\right)^{\frac{2r+2}{2r+1}}\left(\frac{1}{Q}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2r+1}}\left(\frac{1}{K_{2d}}\frac{1}{\beta+\zeta^{2}}\right)^{\frac{r}{2r+1}}\wedge\frac{1}{4}\frac{\zeta-1}{\zeta}\frac{1}{Q^{2}K_{2d}}\wedge\frac{C_{P}}{2}\wedge 1.

(19)

Then by taking (de)-regularization parameter
\lambda_{n}=\left(2\gamma\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\frac{(\beta+\zeta^{2})(K_{1d}+K_{2d})}{Q(\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I})}\right)^{\frac{1}{r+1}}\wedge 1
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n_{max}}\|\pi)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{2n_{max}\gamma}{C_{P}}\right)\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{0}\|\pi)

\displaystyle+4\gamma^{\frac{r}{r+1}}C_{P}Q^{\frac{2r+1}{r+1}}\Big((K_{1d}+K_{2d})(\beta+\zeta^{2})\Big)^{\frac{r}{r+1}}(\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I})^{\frac{1}{r+1}}.

(20)

The proof can be found in
Section

10.10
.
The conditions 1-5 of
Theorem

5.1
are the same as conditions 1-5 of
Proposition

5.1
. Since the RHS of (
19
) are all constants, condition 6 is satisfied when the step size
\gamma
is small enough.

The implication of
Theorem

5.1
is that
\operatorname{DrMMD}
gradient descent exhibits an exponential rate of convergence (linear convergence) in terms of KL divergence up to an extra barrier of order
\mathcal{O}(\gamma^{\frac{r}{r+1}})
.
The barrier term shows up as the result of picking the optimal regularization parameter
\lambda_{n}
that best trades off the approximation error and discretization error in
Proposition

5.1
.

Theorem

5.1
is reminiscent of the convergence result of the Langevin Monte Carlo sampling algorithm, whose KL divergence also decreases exponentially up to an extra barrier, but of order
\mathcal{O}(\gamma)

(
Vempala and Wibisono, 2019
, Theorem 2)
.
Unlike the continuous-time result of
Theorem

4.1
, in which the barrier can be made arbitrarily small by taking small enough regularization, taking the step size
\gamma
in
Theorem

5.1
to be arbitrarily small will significantly impact the rate of convergence, even though it is exponential in terms of
n_{\max}
.
By making the step sizes adaptive with the number of iterations and imposing an extra condition, the barrier term actually vanishes, as demonstrated in the following theorem.

Theorem 5.2

(Global convergence of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
gradient descent)
.

Suppose that
k
satisfies Assumptions
1
and
2
, and that the conditions in
Theorem

5.1
on
\operatorname{DrMMD}
gradient descent iterates
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, target distribution
\pi
, regularization coefficient
\lambda_{n}
and step size
\gamma_{n}
are satisfied.
If additionally, the step size
\gamma_{n}
satisfies

\displaystyle\gamma_{n}\leq\frac{1}{(K_{1d}+K_{2d})(\beta+\zeta^{2})}\left(\frac{1}{Q(\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I})}\right)^{\frac{1}{r}}\left(\frac{1}{8C_{P}}\right)^{\frac{r+1}{r}}\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)^{\frac{1}{r}},

(21)

for all
n=1,\cdots,n_{max}
,
then

\displaystyle\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n_{max}}\|\pi)\leq\prod_{n=1}^{n_{max}}\left(1-\frac{1}{C_{P}}\gamma_{n}\right)\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{0}\|\pi).

(22)

The proof can be found in
Section

10.11
.
Compared with (
20
), (
22
) provides a cleaner upper bound without the barrier term and leads to global convergence.

Remark 5.3

(Iteration complexity)
.

We now turn to analyze the iteration complexity of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
gradient descent from Theorems
5.1
and
5.2
.

(i) From
Theorem

5.1
, given an error threshold
\delta>0
,
\operatorname{DrMMD}
descent would reach
\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{n_{\max}}\|\pi)\leq\delta
after
n_{max}\geq\frac{C_{P}}{2\gamma}\log\frac{\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{0}\|\pi)}{\delta}=\mathcal{O}((\frac{1}{\delta})^{\frac{r+1}{r}}\log\frac{1}{\delta})
iterations. By comparison, when
\pi
satisfies a Poincaré inequality, Langevin Monte Carlo (LMC) has an iteration complexity of
\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{\delta})
up to logarithmic terms
(
Chewi et al., 2024
, Theorem 7)
.
As an approximation to the
\chi^{2}
flow, the iteration complexity of DrMMD flow is worse than LMC, because at each iterate of DrMMD flow, there is an extra approximation error in addition to time-discretization error (see our Proposition 5.1). With the optimal choice of regularization
\lambda
that balances these two errors, the overall per-step error is of order
\mathcal{O}(\gamma^{1+\frac{r}{r+1}})
.
In contrast, at each iteration, LMC only incurs the time-discretization error, which is of order
\mathcal{O}(\gamma^{2})
, smaller than that of DrMMD flow.
As a result, LMC exhibits better iteration complexity than our DrMMD flow; however, LMC requires knowledge of the score of
\pi
, while DrMMD flow only requires samples from
\pi
.

(ii) From
Theorem

5.2
, we consider two cases. On the one hand, if there exists a threshold
N_{0}
such that
\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\geq n^{-r}
holds for all
n\geq N_{0}
, then we select step size
\gamma_{n}\asymp C_{P}n^{-1}
for all
n\geq N_{0}
such that both (
19
) and (
21
) are satisfied, and consequently
\prod_{n=N_{0}}^{n_{max}}\left(1-\frac{1}{C_{P}}\gamma_{n}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{n_{\max}}\right)\to 0
so the iteration complexity of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
gradient descent is
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)
. On the other hand, if such a threshold
N_{0}
does not exist, then there exists a subsequence
n_{1},n_{2},\ldots,n_{S},\ldots
such that
\chi^{2}(\mu_{n_{s}}\|\pi)\leq n_{s}^{-r}
for all
s\geq 1
. Since KL divergence is smaller than
\chi^{2}
-divergence
(
Van Erven and Harremos, 2014
)
, we have
\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n_{s}}\|\pi)\leq n_{s}^{-r}
for all
s\geq 1
. Notice that KL divergence is monotonically decreasing based on (
63
), we have
\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\leq n_{s}^{-r}
for all
n_{s}\leq n\leq n_{s+1}
so that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)=0
.
Unfortunately, we are not able to derive iteration complexity in this case because the growth rate of
\{n_{s}\}_{s\geq 1}
is unknown.

Remark 5.4

(Step size
\gamma
)
.

Theorem

5.1
imposes a condition on the step size
\gamma
in (
19
) and
Theorem

5.2
imposes an additional condition in (
21
).
These conditions subsume the condition (
16
) on step size in
Proposition

5.1
. (See derivations in
Section

10.10
.) The conditions (
19
) and (
21
) become more stringent as the potential
V
becomes less smooth, i.e., when
\beta
gets larger, similar to the analysis in Langevin Monte Carlo
(
Balasubramanian et al., 2022
;
Vempala and Wibisono, 2019
)
and Stein Variational Gradient Descent
(
Korba et al., 2020
)
.
The condition also becomes more stringent as the density ratio becomes less regular, i.e., when
r
gets closer to
0
and
Q,\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}
get larger, similar to
He et al. (2024)
.

The adaptive
\operatorname{DrMMD}
descent schemes of (
15
)
and (
18
) defined via push-forward operations can be equivalently expressed by the following update scheme that defines a trajectory of samples
(y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
whose distributions are precisely the Adaptive
\operatorname{DrMMD}
descent iterates
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,

\displaystyle y_{n+1}=y_{n}-\gamma(1+\lambda_{n})\nabla h_{\mu_{n},\pi}^{\ast}(y_{n}),\quad y_{0}\sim\mu_{0}.

(23)

Unfortunately, (
23
) is still intractable in practice because
h_{\mu_{n},\pi}^{\ast}
depends on the unknown distribution
\mu_{n}
. Therefore, an additional discretization in space is needed to approximate (
23
) within a tractable algorithm. We propose to do so in the next section through a system of interacting particles, i.e.,
\operatorname{DrMMD}
particle descent.

6
\operatorname{DrMMD}
particle descent

Suppose we have
M
samples from the target distribution
\{x^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{M}\sim\pi
and
N
samples from the initial distribution
\{y^{(i)}_{0}\}_{i=1}^{N}\sim\mu_{0}
.
The
\operatorname{DrMMD}
particle descent is defined as:

\displaystyle y_{n+1}^{(i)}=y_{n}^{(i)}-\gamma(1+\lambda_{n})\nabla h_{\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\pi}}^{\ast}(y_{n}^{(i)}),

(24)

where
\hat{\mu}_{n}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{y_{n}^{(i)}}
and
\hat{\pi}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\delta_{x^{(i)}}
denote respectively the empirical distribution of the particles at time step
n
and the target, and
where
h_{\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\pi}}^{\ast}=2\left(\Sigma_{\hat{\pi}}+\lambda_{n}\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1}(m_{\hat{\mu}_{n}}-m_{\hat{\pi}})
. Unfortunately, the KL divergence is ill-defined on empirical distributions
\hat{\mu}_{n}
, which means the analysis of
Theorem

5.1
is no longer applicable to study the convergence of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
particle descent. Therefore, in the next theorem, we instead resort to the Wasserstein-2 distance to analyze the convergence of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
particle descent.
For simplicity of presentation below, we assume
K\leq 1
.

Theorem 6.1
.

Suppose that
k
satisfies Assumptions
1
and
2
with
K\leq 1
, and that all the conditions in
Theorem

5.1
on
\operatorname{DrMMD}
gradient descent iterates
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, target distribution
\pi
, regularization coefficient
\lambda_{n}
and step size
\gamma
are satisfied. In addition, suppose
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
has bounded fourth moment and the target
\pi
satisfies a Talagrand-2 inequality with constant
C_{T}
.
Let the number of samples
M,N
satisfy

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\gtrsim\left(\frac{1}{\gamma}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{1}{\min\limits_{i=1,\ldots,n_{\max}}\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{i}\|\pi)Z\wedge 1}\right)^{\frac{2}{r+1}}\exp\left(\frac{8n_{\max}\gamma^{\frac{r}{r+1}}R}{\left(\min\limits_{i=1,\ldots,n_{\max}}\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{i}\|\pi)Z\right)^{\frac{1}{r+1}}\wedge 1}\right),

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle\gtrsim\left(\frac{1}{\gamma}\right)^{\frac{2r}{r+1}}\exp\left(\frac{8n_{\max}\gamma^{\frac{r}{r+1}}R}{\left(\min\limits_{i=1,\ldots,n_{\max}}\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{i}\|\pi)Z\right)^{\frac{1}{r+1}}\wedge 1}\right)\vee\left(\frac{1}{\gamma}\right)^{\frac{r(d\vee 4)}{2r+2}},

(25)

where
\gtrsim
means
\geq
up to constants,
R=K_{1d}+\sqrt{KK_{2d}}
is a constant that only depends on the kernel, and
Z
is a constant that only depends on
\beta,\zeta,K_{1d},K_{2d},Q,\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\E\left[W_{2}\left(\hat{\mu}_{n_{\max}},\pi\right)\right]\leq\sqrt{2C_{T}}\exp\left(-\frac{n_{\max}\gamma}{C_{P}}\right)\sqrt{\operatorname{KL}\left(\mu_{0}\|\pi\right)}+\mathcal{O}\left(\gamma^{\frac{r}{2r+2}}\right),

where the expectation is taken over initial samples
\{y_{0}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N}
drawn from
\mu_{0}
.

The proof is provided in
Section

10.12
.
Theorem

6.1
shows that for sufficiently large sample size
M
and
N
,
\operatorname{DrMMD}
particle descent exhibits an exponential rate of convergence (linear convergence) in terms of Wasserstein-2 distance up to an extra barrier of order
\mathcal{O}\left(\gamma^{\frac{r}{2r+2}}\right)
.

Remark 6.1

(Talagrand-2 inequality)
.

We say that the target distribution
\pi
satisfies a Talagrand-2 inequality with constant
C_{T}
if for any
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle W_{2}(\mu,\pi)\leq\sqrt{2C_{T}\operatorname{KL}(\mu\|\pi)}.

A Talagrand-2 inequality implies the Poincaré inequality in (
11
) with constant
C_{P}\leq C_{T}
, so the condition that
\pi
satisfies a Talagrand-2 inequality is stronger than the condition in Theorems
4.1
and
5.1
.
A Talagrand-2 inequality allows linking of the two key components of
Theorem

6.1
: the population convergence in terms of KL divergence proved in
Theorem

5.1
, and the finite-particle propagation of chaos bound in terms of Wasserstein-2 distance proved in
Proposition

10.1
.
Talagrand inequality is widely used in finite-particle convergence analysis of Wasserstein gradient flows
(
Shi and Mackey, 2024
)
.

Remark 6.2

(Iteration and sample complexity)
.

The choice of
\gamma=\mathcal{O}\left(\delta^{\frac{2r+2}{r}}\right)
yields

\E\left[W_{2}\left(\hat{\mu}_{n_{\max}},\pi\right)\right]\leq\delta
with an iteration complexity of
n_{\max}=\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)^{\frac{2r+2}{r}}\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right)
, which equals the square of the iteration complexity in
Theorem

5.1
because Wasserstein-2 distance is of the same order as the square root of KL divergence.
From
Theorem

5.1
, we have

\displaystyle\min\limits_{i=1,\ldots,n_{\max}}\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{i}\|\pi)\leq\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n_{\max}}\|\pi)=\mathcal{O}\left(\delta^{2}\right).

Therefore, from (
25
), the sample complexity is at least

\displaystyle M=\operatorname{poly}\exp\left(\delta^{-\frac{2}{r}-\frac{2}{r+1}}\right),\quad N=\operatorname{poly}\exp\left(\delta^{-\frac{2}{r}-\frac{2}{r+1}}\right)\mathcal{O}\left(\delta^{-{d\vee 4}}\right).

The poly-exponential sample complexity originates from the propagation of chaos in interacting particle systems
(
Kac, 1956
)
. Although
\mathcal{O}\left(\delta^{-d\vee 4}\right)
is subsumed by the poly-exponential term, we still make it explicit to show that
N
suffers from the curse of dimensionality as expected from the Wasserstein-2 distance between an empirical distribution and a continuous distribution
(
Kloeckner, 2012
;
Lei, 2020
)
.
For comparison, the sample complexity of SVGD in Theorem 3 of
Shi and Mackey (2024)
is
\mathcal{O}(\exp\exp\left(\delta^{-2}\right))
.
Similar results have also been established for mean-field Langevin dynamics
(
Suzuki et al., 2023
;
Chen et al., 2024
)
.

Recently,
Banerjee et al. (2025)
proposed a refined finite-particle analysis of Stein Variational Gradient Descent (SVGD), which gives a uniform-in-time convergence bound, i.e., the bound does not blow up exponentially fast as the number of iterations
n_{\max}
grows.
The analysis of
Banerjee et al. (2025)
, however, heavily relies on the relation that the time derivative of the KL divergence in the course of SVGD equals the squared kernel Stein discrepancy (KSD), a fact which does not hold for our DrMMD flow.
Recently,
Chen et al. (2025)
extended that analysis to finite-particle MMD gradient descent, but their analysis requires noise injection.
We leave a more refined analysis of our finite-particle convergence result to future work.

Having established the convergence of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
gradient flow/descent, we next show that
\operatorname{DrMMD}
particle descent admits a closed-form implementation.

Proposition

6.1
shows that
h_{\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\pi}}^{\ast}
in (
24
), defined through the inverse of covariance operators, is computable using Gram matrices.

Proposition 6.1
.

Given empirical distributions
\hat{\mu}_{n}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}y_{n}^{(i)}
,
\hat{\pi}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}x^{(i)}
and Gram matrices
K_{xx}=k(x^{1:M},x^{1:M})\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times M}
and
K_{xy}=k(x^{1:M},y_{n}^{1:N})\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times N}
, the witness function
h_{\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\pi}}^{\ast}
can be computed as:

\displaystyle h_{\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\pi}}^{\ast}(\cdot)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{N\lambda_{n}}k(\cdot,y^{1:N})\mathbbm{1}_{N}-\frac{2}{M\lambda_{n}}k(\cdot,x^{1:M})\mathbbm{1}_{M}-\frac{2}{N\lambda_{n}}k(\cdot,x^{1:M})(M\lambda_{n}\mathrm{I}+K_{xx})^{-1}K_{xy}\mathbbm{1}_{N}

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{M\lambda_{n}}k(\cdot,x^{1:M})(M\lambda_{n}\mathrm{I}+K_{xx})^{-1}K_{xx}\mathbbm{1}_{M},

(26)

where
\mathbbm{1}_{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{M},\mathbbm{1}_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
