are column vectors of ones.

The proof of
Proposition

6.1
can be found in
Section

10.14
.
The gradient of
h_{\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\pi}}^{\ast}
can be obtained using automatic differentiation libraries such as JAX
(
Bradbury et al., 2018
)
.

As indicated in (
18
), the regularization parameter
\lambda_{n}
should be chosen to be proportional to
\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)^{\frac{1}{r+1}}
. In practice, however, both
\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)
and
r
are not accessible and
\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)
is not even well-defined for the particle descent algorithm. To address this, we use
\mathrm{DrMMD}(\hat{\mu}_{n}\|\hat{\pi})
as a proxy for
\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)
(see Algorithm 1), which admits a closed-form expression with particles. The parameter
r
is picked via a search over a pre-defined set
\{0.1,0.5,1.0\}
. The step size
\gamma
should be chosen to satisfy the upper bound (21), which contains several constants that cannot generally be computed. In practice, our approach has been to select
\gamma
to be sufficiently small for the flow to converge empirically.
The final algorithm is summarized in
Algorithm

1
.

At every iteration, computing
h_{\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\pi}}^{\ast}
with adaptive regularization
\lambda_{n}
has a time complexity of
\mathcal{O}(M^{3}+NM+N^{2})
due to matrix inversion and multiplication. For
\operatorname{DrMMD}
particle descent with fixed
\lambda
, however, the total computational cost can be reduced to
\mathcal{O}(NM+N^{2})
, which is exactly the same as
\operatorname{MMD}
flow, because inversion of the
M\times M
Gram matrix is only required once, and so it can be pre-computed at initialization (see
Remark

4.4
).
In contrast, when
N=M
, the complexity of Sinkhorn flow is
\mathcal{O}(N^{2}/\epsilon^{3})

(
Feydy et al., 2019
)
with
\epsilon
being the hyperparameter in Sinkhorn divergence, and the complexity of KALE flow is
\mathcal{O}(N^{3})

(
Glaser et al., 2021
)
.

Algorithm 1

\operatorname{DrMMD}
particle descent

Input:
Target samples
\{x^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{M}\sim\pi
and initial source samples
\{y^{(i)}_{0}\}_{i=1}^{N}\sim\mu_{0}
. Hyperparameters: step size
\gamma
, initial (de)-regularization coefficient
\lambda_{0}
, maximum number of iterations
n_{max}
and regularity
r
.

For

n=0
to
n_{max}
:

1. Compute witness function
h_{\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\pi}}
from (
26
).

2. Compute
\operatorname{DrMMD}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\pi})
with
h_{\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\pi}}
from (
71
).

3. Rescale regularization coefficient
\lambda_{n}\propto\operatorname{DrMMD}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\pi})^{\frac{1}{r+1}}
.

4. Update particles using (
24
):

\displaystyle y_{n+1}^{(i)}=y_{n}^{(i)}-\gamma(1+\lambda_{n})\nabla h_{\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\pi}}^{\ast}(y_{n}^{(i)})\vskip-10.0pt

EndFor

Output:

\{y^{(i)}_{n_{max}}\}_{i=1}^{N}
.

7
Related Work

In this section, we discuss the works in the literature that are related to our proposed
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow and spectral (de)-regularization.

7.1
Gradient flows

Stein Variational Gradient Descent (SVGD) is a popular algorithm for sampling from distributions using only an unnormalized density.
It can be written as either a gradient flow of the Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence where the Wasserstein gradient of the KL is preconditioned by
\mathcal{T}_{\mu_{t}}

(
Liu and Wang, 2016
;
Liu, 2017
;
Korba et al., 2020
)
, or as a gradient flow of the
\chi^{2}
-divergence whose Wasserstein gradient is preconditioned by
\mathcal{T}_{\pi}

(
Chewi et al., 2020
)
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mu_{t}}{\partial t}=\nabla\cdot\left(\mu_{t}\mathcal{T}_{\mu_{t}}\nabla\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\pi}\right)=\nabla\cdot\left(\mu_{t}\mathcal{T}_{\pi}\nabla\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\pi}\right).

Since SVGD may smooth the trajectory too much,
He et al. (2024)
considered a (de)-regularized SVGD flow,

\frac{\partial\mu_{t}}{\partial t}=\nabla\cdot\left(\mu_{t}(\mathcal{T}_{\mu_{t}}+\lambda\mathrm{I})^{-1}\mathcal{T}_{\mu_{t}}\nabla\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\pi}\right),

(27)

which approaches the KL gradient flow (Langevin diffusion) as
\lambda\rightarrow 0
, demonstrating faster convergence than SVGD. A key difference between (de)-regularization in (
27
) of
He et al. (2024)
and our
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow is that the flow in (
27
) is driven by the regularized version of the Wasserstein gradient of KL divergence while
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow is driven by the Wasserstein gradient of the regularized
\chi^{2}
-divergence.
An alternative interpretation of this difference is that the flow in (
27
) is the gradient flow of KL divergence w.r.t. the regularized Stein geometry
(
Duncan et al., 2023
)
, whereas the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow is the gradient flow of regularized
\chi^{2}
-divergence w.r.t. the Wasserstein geometry.

In addition to sampling from unnormalized distributions, Wasserstein gradient flows (particularly
\operatorname{MMD}
flows) are widely used in the field of generative modelling
(
Birrell et al., 2022
;
Gu et al., 2024
;
Hertrich et al., 2023
;
Hertrich et al., 2024b
;
Hertrich et al., 2024a
;
Galashov et al., 2025
)
.
The MMD flow (with a smooth kernel)
(
Arbel et al., 2019
)
can be written as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mu_{t}}{\partial t}=\nabla\cdot\left(\mu_{t}\nabla\mathcal{T}_{\pi}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\pi}-1\right)\right).

(28)

The MMD flow is known to get trapped in local minima, and several modifications have been proposed to avoid this in practice, such as noise injection
(see
Arbel et al., 2019
, Proposition 8)
or non-smooth kernels, e.g., based on negative distances
(
Sejdinovic et al., 2013
)
.

\operatorname{MMD}
gradient flows with non-smooth kernels have better empirical performance
(
Hertrich et al., 2024a
;
Hertrich et al., 2024b
)
, but they do not preserve discrete measure and rely on approximating implicit time discretizations
(
Hertrich et al., 2024a
)
or slicing
(
Hertrich et al., 2024b
)
; and they have no local minima apart from the global one
(
Boufadène and Vialard, 2024
)
.

Recall that our
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow takes the form

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mu_{t}}{\partial t}=\nabla\cdot\left(\mu_{t}\nabla(\mathcal{T}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I})^{-1}\mathcal{T}_{\pi}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\pi}-1\right)\right),

which (de)-regularizes the
\operatorname{MMD}
flow similarly to how (
27
) (de)-regularizes SVGD.
It is both proved theoretically in
Theorem

4.1
,
5.1
,
6.1
, and verified empirically in
Section

8
, that (de)-regularization results in faster convergence than
\operatorname{MMD}
flow.

Another closely related flow called LAWGD is considered in
Chewi et al. (2020)
, which swaps the gradient and integral operators of SVGD, leading to the following flow:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mu_{t}}{\partial t}=\nabla\cdot\left(\mu_{t}\nabla\mathcal{T}_{\pi}\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\pi}\right).

LAWGD closely resembles the MMD flow in (
28
), but
Chewi et al. (2020)
proposes to replace
\mathcal{T}_{\pi}
with an inverse diffusion operator, which requires computing the eigenspectrum of the latter and is unlikely to scale in high dimensions.

KALE
(
Glaser et al., 2021
)
kernelizes the variational formulation of the KL divergence in a similar way as
\operatorname{DrMMD}
kernelizes the
\chi^{2}
-divergence in (
5
), but the KALE witness function does not have a closed form expression, so it requires solving a convex optimization problem, which makes the simulation of KALE gradient flow with particles computationally more expensive.
Recently,
Stein et al. (2025)
studied kernelized variational formulation of
f
-divergences (referred to as Moreau envelopes of
f
-divergences in RKHS), which subsume both KALE
(
Glaser et al., 2021
)
and
\operatorname{DrMMD}
. They prove that these functionals are lower semi-continuous and that their Wasserstein gradient flows are well-defined for smooth kernels. They do not study the convergence properties of their proposed flows, however.

The proposed DrMMD interpolates between MMD and
\chi^{2}
-divergence by using a specific spectral regularization known as Tikhnov regularization
(1+\lambda)(\mathcal{T}+\lambda\mathrm{I})^{-1}\mathcal{T}
, which interpolates between the identity operator
\mathrm{I}
as
\lambda\to 0
and
\mathcal{T}
as
\lambda\to\infty
.
DrMMD and its associated Wasserstein gradient flow can be easily extended to other spectral regularization strategies, such as the Showalter regularization, Landweber iteration, or cutoff regularization
(
Engl et al., 1996
)
using the techniques in
(
Bauer et al., 2007
;
Hagrass et al., 2024a
)
. Alternative approximations to
\chi^{2}
-divergence have been proposed in the literature based on the idea of mollifiers, whose Wasserstein gradient flows have been constructed and for which convergence of the flows has been analyzed
(
Li et al., 2023
;
Craig et al., 2023
;
Craig et al., 2025
)
. Compared to DrMMD flow, these gradient flows rely on additional approximations, such as the use of log-sum-exp in
Li et al. (2023)
and the use of numerical integration to estimate convolution in
Craig et al. (2023)
;
Craig et al. (2025)
—and are not directly applicable in generative modeling settings where only samples are available.

7.2
Comparison with diffusion-based generative models

Diffusion-based generative models have been widely adopted in practice and are closely related to Wasserstein gradient flows
(
Song et al., 2021
;
Ho et al., 2020
)
. These models generate high-quality samples by reversing a pre-defined forward diffusion process, which gradually corrupts data with noise. To implement the reverse process, the established practice is to estimate the score function via denoising score matching
(
Song et al., 2021
)
. In contrast, Wasserstein gradient flows directly construct a trajectory by descending the objective in the steepest direction with respect to the Wasserstein metric. In particular, our proposed DrMMD gradient flow offers a tractable velocity field with a consistent finite-sample estimator without solving an additional optimization problem like score matching.

We emphasize that the main contribution of our paper is to establish convergence of the DrMMD flow, and that diffusion models for image generation require additional implementation details—most notably, the inductive biases introduced by deep neural networks.
One possible avenue for future work is to simulate DrMMD gradient flows with kernels induced by learned deep neural network features on the data. In the case of MMD, this idea has been explored by
Galashov et al. (2025)
, who demonstrate generation performance comparable to established diffusion models.
Another promising direction, proposed by
Hertrich et al. (2024b)
, involves first using MMD gradient flow with Riesz kernels to generate particle trajectories, and then distilling these trajectories into a neural network-based generator. Both approaches would be of interest to extend our DrMMD gradient flow to domains such as image generation, as a topic for future work.

7.3
(De)-regularization for supervised learning and hypothesis testing

The idea of (de)-regularization is not new, and has been used in kernel Fisher discriminant analysis
(
Mika et al., 1999
)
and kernel ridge regression
(
Caponnetto and De Vito, 2007
;
Schölkopf and Smola, 2002
)
.
Subsequently,
Harchaoui et al. (2007)
employed this statistic in two-sample testing, where they constructed a test statistic that (de)-regularizes
\operatorname{MMD}(\mu\|\pi)
with both covariance operators
\Sigma_{\mu},\Sigma_{\pi}
. This work has been recently generalized in
Hagrass et al. (2024a)
to more general spectral regularizations.
A (de)-regularized statistic is also employed by
Balasubramanian et al. (2021)
;
Hagrass et al. (2024b)
in the context of a goodness-of-fit test.
Balasubramanian et al. (2021)
refers to (de)-regularized
\operatorname{MMD}
as ‘Moderated MMD’. To the best of our knowledge, the present work represents the first instance of the (de)-regularized
\operatorname{MMD}
being used as a distance functional in Wasserstein gradient flow.
By only (de)-regularizing with
\Sigma_{\pi}
,
\operatorname{DrMMD}
approaches the
\chi^{2}
-divergence in the limit, a crucial property that is exploited in the proofs of the convergence results of Theorems
4.1
and
5.1
.

8
Experiments

In this section, we demonstrate the superior empirical performance of the proposed
\operatorname{DrMMD}
descent in various experimental settings.

8.1
Three ring experiment

We follow the experimental set-up in
Glaser et al. (2021)
in which the target distribution
\pi
(
{\color[rgb]{0.121,0.467,0.706}\bullet}
) is defined on a manifold in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
consisting of three non-overlapping rings.
The initial source distribution
\mu_{0}
(
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\bullet}
) is a Gaussian distribution close to the vicinity of the first ring.
In this setting, all
f
-divergence gradient flows, including Langevin diffusion, are ill-defined because the target
\pi
is not absolutely continuous with respect to the initial source
\mu_{0}
.
Nevertheless, we will simulate
\chi^{2}
flow with an existing implementation of
Liu et al. (2024a)
that estimates the velocity field with a local linear estimator as one of the baseline methods.
In contrast, kernel-based gradient flows like
\operatorname{MMD}
,
\operatorname{KALE}
, and
\operatorname{DrMMD}
gradient flows are well-defined in this setting, and are also used as baseline methods for comparison.

We sample
N=M=300
samples from the initial source and the target distributions and run
\operatorname{DrMMD}
descent with adaptive
\lambda
for
n_{max}=100,000
iterations, at which point all methods have converged. As in
Glaser et al. (2021)
, we use a Gaussian kernel
k(x,x^{\prime})=\exp\left(-0.5\|x-x^{\prime}\|^{2}/l^{2}\right)
with bandwidth
l=0.3
. The step size for
\operatorname{MMD}
descent is
\gamma=10^{-2}
and the step size for
\operatorname{KALE}
and
\operatorname{DrMMD}
descent is
\gamma=10^{-3}
. We enforce a positive lower bound
\tilde{\lambda}=10^{-3}
for numerical stability and the regularity hyperparameter
r
is optimized over the set of
\{0.1,0.5,1.0\}
.

From
Figure

2

Left
and
Middle
, we can see that
\operatorname{DrMMD}
descent outperforms
\operatorname{MMD}
,
\operatorname{KALE}
, and
\chi^{2}
descent in terms of all dissimilarity metrics with respect to the target
\pi
:
\operatorname{MMD}
and Wasserstein-2 distance.

Figure

1
is an animation plot visualizing the evolution of particles under these descent schemes, which demonstrates that both
\operatorname{KALE}
and
\operatorname{DrMMD}
descent are sensitive to the mismatch of support and stay concentrated in the support of the target
\pi
, while particles of
\operatorname{MMD}
descent can diffuse outside the support of
\pi
.
Note that "
\chi^{2}
" denotes an alternate estimate of the
\chi^{2}
divergence due to
Liu et al. (2024a)
: being an
f
-divergence, we would expect "
\chi^{2}
descent" to match the support of the target (as in
\operatorname{KALE}
and
\operatorname{DrMMD}
). This is not the case due to bias in the velocity field being learned from samples.
Compared to
\operatorname{KALE}
descent,
\operatorname{DrMMD}
descent does not suffer from the numerical approximation error of the optimization routine when solving the velocity field of
\operatorname{KALE}
, which explains its improved performance.

Figure 1
:
Animation of MMD, KALE,
\chi^{2}
and DrMMD gradient descent on the Three-ring dataset.

8.2
Gradient flow for training student/teacher networks

Next, we consider a large-scale setting following
Arbel et al. (2019)
, where a student network is trained to imitate the outputs of a
teacher network.
We consider a two-layer neural network of the form

\displaystyle\psi(z,x)=G\left(b^{1}+W^{1}\sigma\left(W^{0}z+b^{0}\right)\right),

where
\sigma
is the ReLU non-linearity and
x
is the concatenation of all network parameters
\left(b^{1},W^{1},b^{0},W^{0}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
G
is an element-wise non-linear function
G:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},x\mapsto\exp(-\frac{1}{4}x^{2})
.
The teacher network is of the form:
\Psi^{T}(z,\pi)=\int\psi(z,x)d\pi(x)
where
\pi
denotes the teacher distribution, and the student network is
\Psi^{S}(z,\mu)=\int\psi(z,x)d\mu(x)
where
\mu
denotes the student distribution.
Here we consider Gaussian distributed
\mu
and
\pi
for simplicity.
The student network can imitate the behavior of the teacher network by minimizing the objective
6
6

6

Note that our setting is slightly different from
Chizat and Bach (2018)
in which
\mu,\pi
are measures over the hidden neurons, while our setting follows
Arbel et al. (2019)
in which
\mu,\pi
are measures over all the network parameters.

\displaystyle\min_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\E_{z\sim\mathbb{P}_{\text{data}}}\left(\Psi^{T}(z,\pi)-\Psi^{S}(z,\mu)\right)^{2},

(29)

where
\mathbb{P}_{\text{data}}
is the distribution of the input data.
If we define the kernel as the inner product of the neural network feature maps,

\displaystyle k(x,x^{\prime})=\E_{z\sim\mathbb{P}_{\text{data}}}[\psi(z,x)^{\top}\psi(z,x^{\prime})],

then the objective of (
29
) can be equivalently expressed as

\displaystyle\min_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\iint_{\mathcal{X}}k(x,x^{\prime})d(\pi-\mu)(x)d(\pi-\mu)(x^{\prime}),

which is precisely the
\operatorname{MMD}(\mu\|\pi)^{2}
under the kernel
k
. Since
G(x)=\exp(-\frac{1}{4}x^{2})
, the kernel is bounded and so the
\operatorname{MMD}
is well-defined. Also, since the kernel
k
has bounded first and second-order derivatives, it satisfies the
Assumption

2
.

Figure 2
:
Left & Middle:
Comparison of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
descent with adaptive
\lambda
,
\operatorname{MMD}
and
\operatorname{KALE}
descent on three-ring synthetic data in terms of
\operatorname{MMD}
and Wasserstein-2 distance with respect to the target
\pi
.
Right:
Comparison of MMD descent with and without noise injection,
\operatorname{DrMMD}
descent with and without noise injection on training
student/teacher networks in terms of validation
\operatorname{MMD}^{2}
distance.

Therefore, the training of the student network with objective (
29
) can be treated as an optimization problem of
\operatorname{MMD}^{2}
distance in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, i.e., as
\operatorname{MMD}
gradient flow.
It is shown in
Arbel et al. (2019)
that
\operatorname{MMD}
flow and its descent scheme will generally get stuck in local optima because

\operatorname{MMD}
is not
geodesically
convex; therefore, noise injection has been proposed to escape these local optima.

With support from Theorems
4.1
and
Theorem

5.1
on the convergence of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow and its descent scheme, we propose to minimize
\operatorname{DrMMD}(\mu\|\pi)
and use
\operatorname{DrMMD}
descent rather than minimizing
\operatorname{MMD}(\mu\|\pi)^{2}
directly.
Although this does not directly minimize the objective in (
29
), the favorable convergence performance of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
descent should result in a smaller
\operatorname{MMD}(\mu\|\pi)^{2}
at convergence.

In our experimental setting, we are given
M=10
particles
x^{(1)},\cdots,x^{(M)}
from the teacher distribution
\pi=\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{I})
and
N=1000
particle
y_{0}^{(1)},\cdots,y_{0}^{(N)}
from the initial student distribution
\mu_{0}=\mathcal{N}(0,10^{-3}\mathrm{I})
. The teacher particles are fixed while
the student particles are updated according to
Algorithm

1
at each time step.
The initial (de)-regularization parameter is
\lambda_{0}=0.1
, the step size is
\gamma=0.1
, we apply a lower bound
\tilde{\lambda}=10^{-3}
, and the regularity
r
is optimized over the set of
\{0.1,0.5,1.0\}
.
For the architecture of the neural network, there are
3
neurons in the hidden layer, and the output dimension is
1
.
The data distribution
\mathbb{P}_{\text{data}}
is a uniform distribution on the sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
with
p=50
.

2000
data are sampled from
\mathbb{P}_{\text{data}}
with
1000
as training dataset and another
1000
as validation dataset.
The kernel
k(x,x^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathbb{P}_{\text{data}}}\left[\psi(z,x)^{\top}\psi\left(z,x^{\prime}\right)\right]
is estimated by the average over
100
randomly selected samples from the training dataset at each iteration.

\operatorname{DrMMD}
and
\operatorname{MMD}
descent stop after
n_{max}=15,000
iterations when both converge.
The final performance is evaluated in terms of
\operatorname{MMD}^{2}(\mu_{n_{max}}\|\pi)
with kernel
k
estimated by the average of
1000
samples in the validation dataset.

In
Figure

2

Right
, we report the performance of
\operatorname{MMD}
descent (with and without noise injection) along with the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
descent (with and without noise injection) in terms of
\operatorname{MMD}
distance on the validation dataset.
We can see that the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
descent does not get stuck in a local optimum, and leads to much lower validation
\operatorname{MMD}(\mu_{n_{max}}\|\pi)^{2}
even without noise injection.
We also run
\operatorname{DrMMD}
descent with the noise injection scheme and find that noise injection can further improve the performance of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
descent and outperforms
\operatorname{MMD}
descent with noise injection.
Although it is unclear whether the density ratio has enough regularity to meet the condition of
Theorem

5.1
, the kernel
k
satisfies the boundedness and smoothness conditions of
Assumption

2
and the target
\pi
satisfies the Poincaré inequality since it is Gaussian. The
\operatorname{DrMMD}
descent benefits from more favorable convergence properties, which explains its superior performance.

The code to reproduce all the experiments can be found in the following GitHub repository.
https://github.com/hudsonchen/DrMMD
.

9
Discussion

In this paper, we introduced (de)-regularization of the MMD (called
\operatorname{DrMMD}
) and its associated Wasserstein gradient flow.
As an interpolation between the
\operatorname{MMD}
and
\chi^{2}
-divergence, the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
gradient flow inherits strengths from both sides: it is easy to simulate in closed form with particles, and it has an exponential rate of convergence towards the global minimum up to a controllable barrier term when the target
\pi
satisfies a Poincaré inequality.
Additionally, we provide the optimal adaptive selection of a regularization coefficient
that best balances the approximation and time discretization errors in
\operatorname{DrMMD}
gradient descent.
Our work is the first to prove theoretically that an adaptive kernel through adaptive regularization can result in improved convergence of MMD gradient flow. The theoretical results are consistent with the empirical evidence in several numerical experiments.

Following our work, there remain a number of interesting open problems. For example,
(i) Since the kernel bandwidth has been known to play an important role in the performance of kernel-based algorithms, it is of interest to study the adaptive choice of kernel bandwidth in the context of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
gradient flow. (ii) To generalize our convergence analysis to the Wasserstein gradient flow of all Moreau envelopes of
f
-divergences in reproducing kernel Hilbert space, even when they do not have a closed-form expression as
\operatorname{DrMMD}
. (iii) While the current work proposes an approximation to the
\chi^{2}
-squared flow in the generative modeling setting, i.e., where the target distribution
\pi
is known only through samples, it will be interesting to construct approximations to
\chi^{2}
-flow in the sampling setting, i.e., where
\pi
is known in closed form (at least up to normalization).

10
Proofs

In this section, we present the proofs of the results presented in Sections
3
–
6
.

10.1
Proof of
Proposition

3.1

Note that

\displaystyle\operatorname{MMD}(\mu\|\pi)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|m_{\mu}-m_{\pi}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\left\|\int k(\cdot,x)d\mu(x)-\int k(\cdot,x)d\pi(x)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\int k(\cdot,x)\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}(x)-1\right)\,d\pi(x)\right\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}=\left\|\iota^{*}_{\pi}\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right)\right\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathcal{T}_{\pi}\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right),\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}=\left\|\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}.

Also recall that the
\chi^{2}
-divergence between
\mu
and
\pi
is

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(\mu\|\pi)=\int\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right)^{2}d\pi=\left\|\mathrm{I}\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}.

10.2
Proof of
Proposition

3.2

Let
\mu\ll\pi
.
In order to prove the alternative form of
\operatorname{DrMMD}
in (
4
), we start from (
4
) and show that it recovers (
3
).

\displaystyle\operatorname{DrMMD}(\mu||\pi)

\displaystyle=(1+\lambda)\left\|\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(m_{\mu}-m_{\pi}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=(1+\lambda)\left\|\left(\iota_{\pi}^{\ast}\iota_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\iota_{\pi}^{\ast}\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=(1+\lambda)\left\langle\left(\iota_{\pi}^{\ast}\iota_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\iota_{\pi}^{\ast}\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right),\left(\iota_{\pi}^{\ast}\iota_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\iota_{\pi}^{\ast}\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=(1+\lambda)\left\langle\iota_{\pi}\left(\iota_{\pi}^{\ast}\iota_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1}\iota_{\pi}^{\ast}\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right),\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}

\displaystyle=(1+\lambda)\left\langle\iota_{\pi}\iota_{\pi}^{\ast}\left(\iota_{\pi}\iota_{\pi}^{\ast}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right),\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)},

(30)

where the last equality follows by noticing
\iota_{\pi}\left(\iota_{\pi}^{*}\iota_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1}\iota_{\pi}^{*}=\iota_{\pi}\iota_{\pi}^{*}\left(\iota_{\pi}\iota_{\pi}^{*}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\quad\operatorname{DrMMD}(\mu||\pi)

\displaystyle=(1+\lambda)\left\langle\mathcal{T}_{\pi}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right),\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}

\displaystyle=(1+\lambda)\left\|\left(\left(\mathcal{T}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1}\mathcal{T}_{\pi}\right)^{1/2}\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2},

which follows from the positivity and self-adjointness of
\mathcal{T}_{\pi}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1}
.
So (
4
) is proved.
Next, we are going to prove the variational formulation in (
5
). Similarly, we start from (
5
) and show it recovers (
3
). Consider

\displaystyle\quad(1+\lambda)\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\left\{\int h\mathrm{d}\mu-\int\left(\frac{h^{2}}{4}+h\right)\mathrm{d}\pi-\frac{\lambda}{4}\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle=-(1+\lambda)\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\left\{\int\frac{h^{2}}{4}\mathrm{d}\pi-\int h(\mathrm{d}\mu-\mathrm{d}\pi)+\frac{\lambda}{4}\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle=-(1+\lambda)\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\left\{\frac{1}{4}\left\langle h,\Sigma_{\pi}h\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\left\langle h,m_{\mu}-m_{\pi}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}+\frac{\lambda}{4}\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle=-(1+\lambda)\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\left\{\left\|\left(\frac{1}{4}\Sigma_{\pi}+\frac{\lambda}{4}\mathrm{I}\right)^{1/2}h-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{4}\Sigma_{\pi}+\frac{\lambda}{4}\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1/2}(m_{\mu}-m_{\pi})\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{(1+\lambda)}{4}\left\|\left(\frac{1}{4}\Sigma_{\pi}+\frac{\lambda}{4}\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1/2}(m_{\mu}-m_{\pi})\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}.

(31)

The last equality follows from completing the squares, based on which it is easy to see that the infimum is achieved at
h_{\mu,\pi}^{\ast}=2\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1}\left(m_{\mu}-m_{\pi}\right)
.
For
\mu\ll\pi
, following the same derivations in (
30
),
h_{\mu,\pi}^{\ast}
can be alternatively expressed as

\displaystyle h_{\mu,\pi}^{\ast}=2\left(\mathcal{T}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1}\mathcal{T}_{\pi}\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right)

(32)

Plugging
h_{\mu,\pi}^{\ast}
back into (
31
) recovers (
3
), so (
5
) is proved.

10.3
\operatorname{DrMMD}
is
\operatorname{MMD}
with a regularized kernel
\tilde{k}

Given the definition of
\tilde{k}(x,x^{\prime})=\left\langle\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}k(\cdot,x),\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}k(\cdot,x^{\prime})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
, it is clear that
\tilde{k}
is symmetric and positive definite so it has a unique associated reproducing kernel Hilbert space
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}

(
Steinwart and Christmann, 2008
, Theorem 4.21)
with canonical feature map
\tilde{k}(x,\cdot)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\quad\operatorname{DrMMD}(\mu\|\pi)

\displaystyle=(1+\lambda)\left\|\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}(m_{\mu}-m_{\pi})\right\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=(1+\lambda)\left\langle\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\int k(x,\cdot)d\left(\pi-\mu\right)(x),\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\int k(x^{\prime},\cdot)d\left(\pi-\mu\right)(x^{\prime})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=(1+\lambda)\left\langle\int\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}k(x,\cdot)d\left(\pi-\mu\right)(x),\int\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}k(x^{\prime},\cdot)d\left(\pi-\mu\right)(x^{\prime})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=(1+\lambda)\iint\left\langle\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}k(x,\cdot),\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}k(x^{\prime},\cdot)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}d\left(\pi-\mu\right)(x)d\left(\pi-\mu\right)(x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(1+\lambda)\iint\tilde{k}(x,x^{\prime})d\left(\pi-\mu\right)(x)d\left(\pi-\mu\right)(x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(1+\lambda)\left\|\int\tilde{k}(x,\cdot)d(\mu-\pi)(x)\right\|^{2}_{\tilde{\mathcal{H}}}.

In the third and fourth equality above, we are using the fact that
\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}k(x,\cdot)\in\mathcal{H}
is Bochner integrable and the Bochner integral preserves inner product structure. So
\operatorname{DrMMD}
is essentially
\operatorname{MMD}^{2}
with a different kernel
\tilde{k}
up to a multiplicative factor of
1+\lambda
.

Next, we present the Mercer decomposition of
\tilde{k}
.
Notice that
\{e_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
are the eigenfunctions of
\mathcal{T}_{\pi}
, so
\{\sqrt{\varrho_{i}}e_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
are the eigenfunctions of
\Sigma_{\pi}
.
For
x
and
x^{\prime}
in the support of
\pi
,
\tilde{k}
also enjoys a pointwise convergent Mercer decomposition

\displaystyle\tilde{k}(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\left\langle\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}k(\cdot,x),\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}k(\cdot,x^{\prime})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\varrho_{i}e_{i}(x)e_{i}\right),\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\varrho_{i}e_{i}(x^{\prime})e_{i}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\sqrt{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}}e_{i}(x)e_{i},\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\sqrt{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}}e_{i}(x^{\prime})e_{i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}e_{i}(x)e_{i}(x^{\prime}).

(33)

More properties of the regularized kernel
\tilde{k}
are provided in
Lemma

B.3
.

10.4
Proof of
Proposition

3.3

Given that
\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\in L^{2}(\pi)
, so

\displaystyle\operatorname{DrMMD}(\mu\|\pi)=(1+\lambda)\left\|\left(\left(\mathcal{T}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1}\mathcal{T}_{\pi}\right)^{1/2}\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}\leq(1+\lambda)\left\|\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2},

which is finite for any
\lambda\geq 0
.
We are allowed to interchange the limit and integration according to the dominated convergence theorem
(
Rudin, 1976
)
to achieve,

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\operatorname{DrMMD}(\mu\|\pi)

\displaystyle=\left\|\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\left(\left(\mathcal{T}_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1}\mathcal{T}_{\pi}\right)^{1/2}\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}=\chi^{2}(\mu\|\pi).

From (
4
), we have that,

\displaystyle\sqrt{\operatorname{DrMMD}(\mu\|\pi)}=\sqrt{1+\lambda}\left\|\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}(m_{\mu}-m_{\pi})\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{1+\lambda}\left\|\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{op}\left\|m_{\mu}-m_{\pi}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{1+\lambda}{\lambda}}\times\operatorname{MMD}(\mu,\pi),

(34)

where the last inequality follows by noticing that
\Sigma_{\pi}=\iota_{\pi}^{\ast}\iota_{\pi}
shares the same
eigenvalues as
\mathcal{T}_{\pi}=\iota_{\pi}\iota_{\pi}^{\ast}
, and hence the eigenvalues of
(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I})^{-1}
are
{(\varrho_{i}+\lambda)}^{-1}
which all smaller than
\frac{1}{\lambda}
.
Therefore,
\operatorname{DrMMD}(\mu\|\pi)\leq\frac{1+\lambda}{\lambda}\operatorname{MMD}^{2}(\mu\|\pi)
.

On the other hand, using Lemma A.10 from
(
Hagrass et al., 2024a
)
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{MMD}\left(\mu\|\pi\right)=\left\|m_{\mu}-m_{\pi}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\left\|(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{op}\left\|\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}(m_{\mu}-m_{\pi})\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{1}{1+\lambda}}\left\|(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{op}\sqrt{\operatorname{DrMMD}\left(\mu\|\pi\right)}\leq\sqrt{\frac{K+\lambda}{1+\lambda}}\sqrt{\operatorname{DrMMD}\left(\mu\|\pi\right)},

(35)

where
K
is the upper bound on kernel
k
in
Assumption

2
and hence an upper bound on the operator norm of
\Sigma_{\pi}
.
Combining (
34
) and (
35
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{1+\lambda}{K+\lambda}\operatorname{MMD}^{2}(\mu\|\pi)\leq\operatorname{DrMMD}\left(\mu\|\pi\right)\leq\frac{1+\lambda}{\lambda}\operatorname{MMD}^{2}(\mu\|\pi).

Therefore,
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\operatorname{DrMMD}(\mu\|\pi)=\operatorname{MMD}^{2}(\mu\|\pi)
, and the proposition is proved.

10.5
Proof of
Proposition

3.4

To show that
\operatorname{DrMMD}
is a probability divergence, we need to show that
\operatorname{DrMMD}
enjoys non-negativity and definiteness.
It is easy to see that
\operatorname{DrMMD}(\mu\|\pi)
is non-negative from its definition in
Definition

1
.
Then, we prove definiteness, i.e.,
\operatorname{DrMMD}(\mu||\pi)=0
if and only if
\mu=\pi
.
For the first direction, assume
\operatorname{DrMMD}(\mu\|\pi)=0
, so
\|(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I})^{-1/2}\left(m_{\mu}-m_{\pi}\right)\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=0
.
Since
\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1/2}
is a non-singular operator, we must have that
m_{\mu}=m_{\pi}
which implies
\mu=\pi
as
k
is
c_{0}
-universal and hence characteristic
(
Sriperumbudur et al., 2011
)
.
For the other direction, when
\mu=\pi
, immediately we can see
\operatorname{DrMMD}(\mu||\pi)=0
.

Then we prove that
\operatorname{DrMMD}
metrizes weak convergence.
For the first direction, we know from (
34
) that
\operatorname{DrMMD}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\leq\frac{1+\lambda}{\lambda}\operatorname{MMD}^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)
and
\operatorname{MMD}^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\to 0
as
\mu_{n}
converges weakly to
\pi

(
Simon-Gabriel et al., 2023
)
.
For the converse direction, we assume that
\operatorname{DrMMD}\left(\mu_{n}\|\pi\right)\to 0
.
From (
35
), we know that
\operatorname{MMD}^{2}\left(\mu\|\pi\right)\leq\frac{K+\lambda}{1+\lambda}\operatorname{DrMMD}\left(\mu\|\pi\right)
, therefore
\operatorname{DrMMD}\left(\mu_{n}\|\pi\right)\to 0
implies
\operatorname{MMD}\left(\mu_{n}\|\pi\right)\to 0
, implying the weak convergence of
\mu_{n}
to
\pi
, if
k
is characteristic
(
Simon-Gabriel et al., 2023
)
.

10.6
Proof of
Proposition

4.1

In order to show that
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{DrMMD}}(\cdot)=\operatorname{DrMMD}(\cdot\|\pi)
admits a well-defined gradient flow, we follow the same techniques in Proposition 7 of
Glaser et al. (2021)
and Lemma B.2 of
Chizat and Bach (2018)
, where the key is to show that
(1+\lambda)\nabla h_{\mu,\pi}^{\ast}
is the Fréchet subdifferential of
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{DrMMD}}
evaluated at
\mu
.
7
7

7

Although
\operatorname{DrMMD}
can be viewed as squared
\operatorname{MMD}
with a regularized kernel
\tilde{k}
, we are not using the technique in
Arbel et al. (2019)
because it relies on Lemma 10.4.1 of
Ambrosio et al. (2005)
which only provides the Fréchet subdifferential on probability measures
\mu
that admit density functions. To construct the Wasserstein gradient flow of
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{DrMMD}}
up to full generality, we resort to the techniques of
Glaser et al. (2021)
and
Chizat and Bach (2018)
instead.

According to Definition 10.1.1 of
Ambrosio et al. (2005)
, it is equivalent to prove that, for any
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{DrMMD}\left((\mathrm{I}+\nabla\phi)_{\#}\mu\|\pi\right)-\operatorname{DrMMD}\left(\mu\|\pi\right)\geq(1+\lambda)\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\nabla h_{\mu,\pi}^{\ast}(x)d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+o\left(\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\right).

(36)

Define
\rho_{t}=(\mathrm{I}+t\nabla\phi)_{\#}\mu
,
\varphi_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},x\mapsto x+t\nabla\phi(x)
and
g(t)=\operatorname{DrMMD}(\rho_{t}||\pi)
. Then from
Lemma

B.6
we know that

g(t)
is continuous and differentiable with respect to
t
and

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}g(t)=(1+\lambda)\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\nabla h_{\mu,\pi}^{\ast}(x)d\mu(x).

Since
t\mapsto g(t)
is differentiable, using Taylor’s theorem and mean value theorem
(
Rudin, 1976
)
, we know that there exists
0<\kappa<1
such that

\displaystyle\quad\operatorname{DrMMD}\left((\mathrm{I}+\nabla\phi)_{\#}\mu\|\pi\right)-\operatorname{DrMMD}\left(\mu\|\pi\right)=g(1)-g(0)=\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}g(t)+\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\Big|_{t=\kappa}g(t).

Therefore, to prove (
36
), the goal is to prove that
\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\big|_{t=\kappa}g(t)\geq o\left(\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\right)
. To this end, since we know from
Lemma

B.6
that
t\mapsto\frac{d}{dt}g(t)
is continuous and differentiable, we have

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\operatorname{DrMMD}(\rho_{t}||\pi)=2(1+\lambda)\iint\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\nabla_{1}\nabla_{2}\tilde{k}\left(\varphi_{t}(x),\varphi_{t}(y)\right)\nabla\phi(y)d\mu(x)d\mu(y)

\displaystyle+2(1+\lambda)\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\left(\int\mathbf{H}_{1}\tilde{k}\left(\varphi_{t}(x),\varphi_{t}(y)\right)d\mu(y)-\int\mathbf{H}_{1}\tilde{k}\left(\varphi_{t}(x),y\right)d\pi(y)\right)\nabla\phi(x)d\mu(x),

where
\tilde{k}
is the regularized kernel defined in (
6
) and
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}
is the associated RKHS.
Using
Lemma

B.3
, the first term above can be upper-bounded by,

\displaystyle\quad\left|2(1+\lambda)\iint\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\nabla_{1}\nabla_{2}\tilde{k}\left(\varphi_{t}(x),\varphi_{t}(y)\right)\nabla\phi(y)d\mu(x)d\mu(y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2(1+\lambda)\iint\left\|\nabla\phi(x)\right\|\left\|\nabla_{1}\nabla_{2}\tilde{k}\left(\varphi_{t}(x),\varphi_{t}(y)\right)\right\|_{F}\left\|\nabla\phi(y)\right\|d\mu(x)d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\leq 2(1+\lambda)\frac{K_{1d}}{\lambda}\left(\int\left\|\nabla\phi(y)\right\|d\mu(y)\right)^{2}\leq 2(1+\lambda)\frac{K_{1d}}{\lambda}\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}.

Using
Lemma

B.3
again, the second term can be upper-bounded by

\displaystyle\quad\left|\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\left(\int\mathbf{H}_{1}\tilde{k}\left(\varphi_{t}(x),\varphi_{t}(y)\right)d\mu(y)-\int\mathbf{H}_{1}\tilde{k}\left(\varphi_{t}(x),y\right)d\pi(y)\right)\nabla\phi(x)d\mu(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\left(\int\mathbf{H}_{1}\tilde{k}\left(\varphi_{t}(x),\varphi_{t}(y)\right)d\mu(y)\right)\nabla\phi(x)d\mu(x)\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\left|\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\left(\int\mathbf{H}_{1}\tilde{k}\left(\varphi_{t}(x),y\right)d\pi(y)\right)\nabla\phi(x)d\mu(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int\left\|\nabla\phi(x)\right\|\left(\int\left\|\mathbf{H}_{1}\tilde{k}\left(\varphi_{t}(x),\varphi_{t}(y)\right)\right\|_{F}d\mu(y)\right)\left\|\nabla\phi(x)\right\|d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\int\left\|\nabla\phi(x)\right\|\left(\int\left\|\mathbf{H}_{1}\tilde{k}\left(\varphi_{t}(x),y\right)\right\|_{F}d\pi(y)\right)\left\|\nabla\phi(x)\right\|d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq 2\frac{\sqrt{KK_{2d}}}{\lambda}\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}.

Combining the above two inequalities to have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\operatorname{DrMMD}(\rho_{t}||\pi)\right|\leq 2(1+\lambda)\frac{K_{1d}}{\lambda}\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}+4(1+\lambda)\frac{\sqrt{KK_{2d}}}{\lambda}\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}.

(37)

Therefore, we have
\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\big|_{t=\kappa}g(t)=\mathcal{O}(\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2})=o(\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)})
as
\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\to 0
.
So (
36
) is proved, which means that
(1+\lambda)\nabla h_{\mu,\pi}^{\ast}(x)
is the Fréchet subdifferential of
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{DrMMD}}
evaluated at
\mu
.
According to Definition 11.1.1 of
Ambrosio et al. (2005)
, there exists a solution
(\mu_{t})_{t\geq 0}
such that the following equation holds in the sense of distributions,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mu_{t}-(1+\lambda)\nabla\cdot\left(\mu_{t}\nabla h_{\mu_{t},\pi}^{\ast}\right)=0,

and such
(\mu_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is indeed the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
gradient flow, so existence is proved.

Next, we are going to prove uniqueness.
(
37
) indicates that
\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname{DrMMD}}
is geodesically
-(1+\lambda)\frac{4\sqrt{KK_{2d}}+2K_{1d}}{\lambda}
semiconvex. Therefore the uniqueness of
(\mu_{t})_{t}
follows from Theorem 11.2.1 of
Ambrosio et al. (2005)
.
(
8
) is proved in (
37
).

10.7
Proof of
Proposition

4.2

Given
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)
and
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)
, consider the path
(\rho_{t})_{0\leq t\leq 1}
from
\mu
to
(\mathrm{I}+\nabla\phi)_{\#}\mu
given by
\rho_{t}=(\mathrm{I}+t\nabla\phi)_{\#}\mu
.
(\rho_{t})_{0\leq t\leq 1}
is a constant-time geodesic in the Wasserstein-2 space by construction (
Appendix A: Further Background on
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2})
).
Define
\varphi_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},x\mapsto x+t\nabla\phi(x)
. We know from
(
Villani and others, 2009
, Example 15.9)
(by taking
m=2
) that,

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\Big|_{t=0}\chi^{2}(\rho_{t}\|\pi)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}(x)\left(\nabla V(x)^{\top}\nabla\phi(x)-\nabla\cdot\nabla\phi(x)\right)^{2}d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\qquad+\int\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}(x)\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\mathbf{H}V(x)\nabla\phi(x)d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\int\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}(x)\left\|\mathbf{H}\phi(x)\right\|_{F}^{2}d\mu(x).

(38)

V
is twice differentiable, so
\nabla V
as a function from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is continuous.

\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
has compact support, so
x\mapsto\nabla V(x)^{\top}\nabla\phi(x)-\nabla\cdot\nabla\phi(x)
is a continuous function over a compact domain, so its image is also compact and hence bounded.
Using similar arguments,
x\mapsto\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\mathbf{H}V(x)\nabla\phi(x)
and
x\mapsto\|\mathbf{H}\phi(x)\|_{F}^{2}
are also continuous functions over compact domains, so they are all bounded.

Since
\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\in\mathcal{H}\subset L^{2}(\pi)
and
\int\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}(x)d\mu(x)=\|\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}+1<\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\big|_{t=0}\chi^{2}(\rho_{t}\|\pi)<\infty.

(39)

Next, from
Lemma

B.9
we know that
\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\big|_{t=0}\chi^{2}(\rho_{t}\|\pi)
can be alternatively expressed as,

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\Big|_{t=0}\chi^{2}(\rho_{t}\|\pi)

\displaystyle=2\int\left(\nabla\cdot\left(\nabla\phi(x)\mu(x)\right)\frac{1}{\pi(x)}\right)^{2}\pi(x)dx

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+2\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\mathbf{H}\left(\frac{\mu(x)}{\pi(x)}\right)\nabla\phi(x)\mu(x)dx.

(40)

Recall from
Lemma

B.6
that

\displaystyle\quad\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\Big|_{t=0}\operatorname{DrMMD}(\rho_{t}||\pi)

\displaystyle=2(1+\lambda)\iint\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\nabla_{1}\nabla_{2}\tilde{k}\left(x,y\right)\nabla\phi(y)d\mu(x)d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\qquad+2(1+\lambda)\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\left(\int\mathbf{H}_{1}\tilde{k}\left(x,y\right)d\mu(y)-\int\mathbf{H}_{1}\tilde{k}\left(x,y\right)d\pi(y)\right)\nabla\phi(x)d\mu(x).

(41)

To prove (
9
), our aim then is to compare and bound the difference of
\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\big|_{t=0}\chi^{2}(\rho_{t}\|\pi)
in (
40
) and
\frac{1}{1+\lambda}\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\big|_{t=0}\operatorname{DrMMD}(\rho_{t}||\pi)
in (
41
), so we compare and bound their first and second term separately.

The first term of (
40
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\int\left(\nabla\cdot(\nabla\phi(x)\mu(x))\frac{1}{\pi(x)}\right)^{2}\pi(x)dx=\sum_{i\geq 1}{\left\langle\nabla\cdot(\nabla\phi\mu)\frac{1}{\pi},e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\geq 1}\left(\int\nabla\cdot\left(\nabla\phi(x)\mu(x)\right)e_{i}(x)dx\right)^{2}=\sum_{i\geq 1}\left(\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\nabla e_{i}(x)\mu(x)dx\right)^{2},

(42)

where we use integration by parts in the last line since
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
And the first term of (
41
) after rescaling by
\frac{1}{1+\lambda}
can be rewritten as,

\displaystyle\quad 2\iint\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\nabla_{1}\nabla_{2}\tilde{k}\left(x,y\right)\nabla\phi(y)d\mu(x)d\mu(y)

\displaystyle=2\iint\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\nabla_{x}\nabla_{y}\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}e_{i}(x)e_{i}(y)\right)\nabla\phi(y)d\mu(x)d\mu(y)

\displaystyle=2\iint\Big(\nabla\cdot\left(\nabla\phi(x)\mu(x)\right)\Big)\Big(\nabla\cdot\left(\nabla\phi(y)\mu(y)\right)\Big)\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}e_{i}(x)e_{i}(y)dxdy.

(43)

Since
\pi\ll\mathcal{L}^{d}
so (
33
) is true for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, hence the second equality is true, and the last equality uses integration by parts since
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Notice that

\displaystyle\quad\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\iint\left|e_{i}(x)e_{i}(y)\Big(\nabla\cdot(\nabla\phi(x)\mu(x))\Big)\Big(\nabla\cdot(\nabla\phi(y)\mu(y))\Big)\right|dxdy

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\left(\int\left|\Big(\nabla\cdot\left(\nabla\phi(x)\mu(x)\right)\Big)e_{i}(x)\right|dx\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\sum_{i\geq 1}\varrho_{i}\left(\int\left(\frac{\nabla\cdot\left(\nabla\phi(x)\mu(x)\right)}{\mu(x)}\right)^{2}\mu(x)dx\right)\left(\int e_{i}(x)^{2}\mu(x)dx\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\int\left(\nabla\log\mu(x)^{\top}\nabla\phi(x)+\nabla\cdot\nabla\phi(x)\right)^{2}d\mu(x)\right)\left(\int\sum_{i\geq 1}\varrho_{i}e_{i}(x)^{2}d\mu(x)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K}{\lambda}\left(\int\left(\nabla\log\mu(x)^{\top}\nabla\phi(x)+\nabla\cdot\nabla\phi(x)\right)^{2}d\mu(x)\right)<+\infty.

The first inequality uses Cauchy-Schwartz, the second inequality uses
\sum_{i\geq 1}\varrho_{i}(e_{i}(x))^{2}=k(x,x)\leq K
.
The last quantity is finite because
x\to\nabla\log\mu(x)
is a continuous function and
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
has compact support, hence the integral of a continuous function over a compact domain is always finite.
Then, by using Fubini’s theorem
(
Rudin, 1976
)
,
we are allowed to interchange the infinite sum and integration of (
43
) to reach,

\displaystyle\quad 2\iint\Big(\nabla\cdot\left(\nabla\phi(x)\mu(x)\right)\Big)\Big(\nabla\cdot\left(\nabla\phi(y)\mu(y)\right)\Big)\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}e_{i}(x)e_{i}(y)dxdy

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\iint\Big(\nabla\cdot\left(\nabla\phi(x)\mu(x)\right)\Big)\Big(\nabla\cdot\left(\nabla\phi(y)\mu(y)\right)\Big)e_{i}(x)e_{i}(y)dxdy

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\left(\int\Big(\nabla\cdot\left(\nabla\phi(x)\mu(x)\right)\Big)e_{i}(x)dx\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\left(\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\nabla e_{i}(x)\mu(x)dx\right)^{2},

where the last equality uses integration by parts.

So the difference between the first term of (
40
) and (
41
) rescaled by
\frac{1}{1+\lambda}
is,

\displaystyle\quad 2\left|\sum_{i\geq 1}\left(\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\nabla e_{i}(x)\mu(x)dx\right)^{2}-\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\left(\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\nabla e_{i}(x)\mu(x)dx\right)^{2}\right|

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\lambda}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\left(\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\nabla e_{i}(x)\mu(x)dx\right)^{2}.

(44)

Now we turn to the second term.
The second term of (
40
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\quad 2\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\mathbf{H}\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}(x)\right)\nabla\phi(x)\mu(x)dx

\displaystyle=2\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\mathbf{H}\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}(x)-1\right)\nabla\phi(x)\mu(x)dx

\displaystyle=2\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\mathbf{H}\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}{\left\langle\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}e_{i}(x)\right)\nabla\phi(x)\mu(x)dx,

(45)

and the second term of (
41
) rescaled by
\frac{1}{1+\lambda}
can be rewritten as,

\displaystyle\quad 2\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\left(\int\mathbf{H}_{1}\tilde{k}\left(x,y\right)d\mu(y)-\int\mathbf{H}_{1}\tilde{k}\left(x,y\right)d\pi(y)\right)\nabla\phi(x)d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=2\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\mathbf{H}\left(\int\tilde{k}\left(x,y\right)d\mu(y)-\int\tilde{k}\left(x,y\right)d\pi(y)\right)\nabla\phi(x)d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=2\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\mathbf{H}\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}e_{i}(x)\int e_{i}(y)d(\mu-\pi)(y)\right)\nabla\phi(x)d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=2\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\mathbf{H}\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}{\left\langle\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}e_{i}(x)\right)\nabla\phi(x)d\mu(x).

(46)

Since
\pi\ll\mathcal{L}^{d}
, so (
33
) is true for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
hence the third equality is true.
From
Lemma

B.3
, we have
\int\tilde{k}(x,y)d\mu(y)\leq\frac{K}{\lambda}
,
x\mapsto\tilde{k}(x,y)
is second-order differentiable,
\sup_{x}|\mathbf{H}_{1}\tilde{k}(x,y)|\leq\frac{\sqrt{KK_{2d}}}{\lambda}
. So we are allowed to interchange integration and Hessian in the second equality using the differentiation lemma
(
Klenke, 2013
, Theorem 6.28)
.
Consider the difference of (
46
) and (
45
), we have

\displaystyle\quad 2\Bigg|\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\mathbf{H}\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}{\left\langle\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}e_{i}(x)\right)\nabla\phi(x)\mu(x)dx

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\mathbf{H}\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}{\left\langle\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}e_{i}(x)\right)\nabla\phi(x)d\mu(x)\Bigg|

\displaystyle=2\left|\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\mathbf{H}\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\lambda}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}{\left\langle\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}e_{i}(x)\right)\nabla\phi(x)\mu(x)dx\right|.

(47)

Given
\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\in\mathcal{H}
, there exists
q\in L^{2}(\pi)
such that
\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1=\mathcal{T}^{1/2}_{\pi}q
so that
\langle\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\rangle=\varrho_{i}^{1/2}\langle q,e_{i}\rangle
for all
i
.
For
j,r\in\{1,\cdots,d\}
, we have

\displaystyle g_{M_{0}}(x)

\displaystyle:=\left|\sum_{i\geq M_{0}}\frac{\lambda}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}{\left\langle\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}\partial_{j}\partial_{r}e_{i}(x)\right|=\left|\sum_{i\geq M_{0}}\frac{\lambda}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\varrho_{i}^{1/2}{\left\langle q,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}\partial_{j}\partial_{r}e_{i}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{i\geq M_{0}}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\right)^{2}{\left\langle q,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{i\geq M_{0}}\varrho_{i}\left(\partial_{j}\partial_{r}e_{i}(x)\right)^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{i\geq M_{0}}{\left\langle q,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{i\geq M_{0}}\varrho_{i}\left(\partial_{j}\partial_{r}e_{i}(x)\right)^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq\sqrt{K_{2d}}\left(\sum_{i\geq M_{0}}{\left\langle q,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

The final inequality holds because,

\displaystyle\sum_{j,r=1}^{d}\sum_{i\geq 1}\varrho_{i}\left(\partial_{j}\partial_{r}e_{i}(x)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j,r=1}^{d}\sum_{i\geq 1}\varrho_{i}{\left\langle\partial_{j}\partial_{r}k(x,\cdot),e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\sum_{j,r=1}^{d}\sum_{i\geq 1}{\left\langle\partial_{j}\partial_{r}k(x,\cdot),\sqrt{\varrho_{i}}e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,r=1}^{d}\left\|\partial_{j}\partial_{r}k(x,\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\left\|\mathbf{H}_{1}k(x,\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{d\times d}}^{2}\leq K_{2d}.

(48)

Since
\left\|sdq\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}
is bounded, so
\sum_{i\geq M_{0}}{\left\langle q,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}
converges to
0
uniformly as
M_{0}\to\infty
and hence
g_{M_{0}}(x)
converge to
0
uniformly. Therefore, we are allowed to interchange the Hessian and the infinite sum
(
Rudin, 1976
)
in (
47
) to achieve,

\displaystyle\quad 2\left|\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\mathbf{H}\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\lambda}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}{\left\langle\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}e_{i}(x)\right)\nabla\phi(x)\mu(x)dx\right|

\displaystyle=2\left|\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\lambda}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\varrho_{i}^{1/2}{\left\langle q,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}\mathbf{H}e_{i}(x)\right)\nabla\phi(x)\mu(x)dx\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\right)^{2}{\left\langle q,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\varrho_{i}\left(\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\mathbf{H}e_{i}(x)\nabla\phi(x)\mu(x)dx\right)^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\right)^{2}{\left\langle q,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\varrho_{i}\left\|\mathbf{H}e_{i}(x)\right\|_{op}^{2}\left(\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\nabla\phi(x)\mu(x)dx\right)^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\right)^{2}{\left\langle q,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\varrho_{i}\left\|\mathbf{H}e_{i}(x)\right\|_{op}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\right)^{2}{\left\langle q,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\varrho_{i}\left\|\mathbf{H}e_{i}(x)\right\|_{F}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\right)^{2}{\left\langle q,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\sqrt{K_{2d}}\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}.

(49)

The first inequality uses Cauchy Schwartz, the second last inequality uses that matrix operator norm is smaller than matrix Frobenius norm, and the last inequality uses (
48
).
Combining together (
44
) and (
49
), we reach

\displaystyle\quad\left|\frac{1}{1+\lambda}\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\Big|_{t=0}\operatorname{DrMMD}(\rho_{t}||\pi)-\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\Big|_{t=0}\chi^{2}(\rho_{t}||\pi)\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\lambda}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\left(\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\nabla e_{i}(x)\mu(x)dx\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+2\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\right)^{2}{\left\langle q,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\sqrt{K_{2d}}\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}

\displaystyle=:\bar{R}(\lambda,\mu,\nabla\phi).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\Big|_{t=0}\operatorname{DrMMD}(\rho_{t}||\pi)

\displaystyle\geq(1+\lambda)\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\Big|_{t=0}\chi^{2}(\rho_{t}||\pi)-(1+\lambda)\bar{R}(\lambda,\mu,\nabla\phi)

\displaystyle\geq(1+\lambda)\int\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}(x)\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\mathbf{H}V(x)\nabla\phi(x)d\mu(x)-(1+\lambda)\bar{R}(\lambda,\mu,\nabla\phi)

\displaystyle\geq(1+\lambda)\alpha\int\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}(x)\|\nabla\phi(x)\|^{2}d\mu(x)-(1+\lambda)\bar{R}(\lambda,\mu,\nabla\phi),

where the second inequality is using (
38
) and the last inequality is using
\mathbf{H}V\succeq\alpha\mathrm{I}
.
So (
9
) is proved.

Define
R(\lambda,\mu,\nabla\phi):=(1+\lambda)\bar{R}(\lambda,\mu,\nabla\phi)
.
The final thing to check is
\lim_{\lambda\to 0}R(\lambda,\mu,\nabla\phi)=0
, which is equivalent to check that
\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\bar{R}(\lambda,\mu,\nabla\phi)=0
.
Since we know from (
39
) and (
42
) that

\displaystyle\sum_{i\geq 1}\left(\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\nabla e_{i}(x)\mu(x)dx\right)^{2}<\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\Big|_{t=0}\chi^{2}(\rho_{t}\|\pi)<\infty,

using the dominated convergence theorem
(
Rudin, 1976
)
, we are allowed to interchange infinite sum and taking limits,

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\lambda}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\left(\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\nabla e_{i}(x)\mu(x)dx\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\geq 1}\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\frac{\lambda}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\left(\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\nabla e_{i}(x)\mu(x)dx\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=0.

Similarly, because
\sum_{i\geq 1}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\right)^{2}{\left\langle q,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}<\left\|q\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}<\infty
, using dominated convergence theorem
(
Rudin, 1976
)
again, we have,

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\sum_{i\geq 1}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\right)^{2}{\left\langle q,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}=\sum_{i\geq 1}\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\right)^{2}{\left\langle q,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}=0.

Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\to 0}R(\lambda,\mu,\nabla\phi)=\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\bar{R}(\lambda,\mu,\nabla\phi)=0.

And the proof of the proposition is finished.

10.8
Proof of
Theorem

4.1

Considering the time derivative of
\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{t}\|\pi)
, we have

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{d}{dt}\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{t}\|\pi)

\displaystyle=-(1+\lambda)\int\nabla h_{\mu_{t},\pi}^{\ast}(x)^{\top}\nabla\log\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)\mu_{t}(x)dx

\displaystyle=-(1+\lambda)\int\nabla h_{\mu_{t},\pi}^{\ast}(x)^{\top}\nabla\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)\pi(x)dx

\displaystyle=-(1+\lambda)\int\left(\nabla h_{\mu_{t},\pi}^{\ast}(x)-2\nabla\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)\right)^{\top}\nabla\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)\pi(x)dx-2(1+\lambda)\int\pi(x)\left\|\nabla\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)\right\|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=-(1+\lambda)\int\left(\nabla h_{\mu_{t},\pi}^{\ast}(x)-2\nabla\left(\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)-1\right)\right)^{\top}\nabla\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)\pi(x)dx

\displaystyle-2(1+\lambda)\int\pi(x)\left\|\nabla\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)\right\|^{2}dx.

(50)

Case one:

\int\pi(x)\left\|\nabla\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)\right\|^{2}dx<\infty
.
We use integration by parts for the first term in (
50
) and we can safely ignore the boundary term due to condition 5 that for
i=1,\ldots,d
,
\lim\limits_{x\to\infty}\left(h_{\mu_{t},\pi}^{\ast}(x)-2\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)\right)\left(\partial_{i}\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)\right)\pi(x)\to 0
. So, we obtain

\displaystyle\quad\frac{d}{dt}\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{t}\|\pi)=(1+\lambda)\int\left(h_{\mu_{t},\pi}^{\ast}(x)-2\left(\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)-1\right)\right)\nabla\cdot\left(\pi(x)\nabla\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)\right)dx

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-2(1+\lambda)\int\pi(x)\left\|\nabla\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)\right\|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=(1+\lambda)\int\left(h_{\mu_{t},\pi}^{\ast}(x)-2\left(\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)-1\right)\right)\frac{\nabla\cdot\left(\pi(x)\nabla\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)\right)}{\pi(x)}\pi(x)dx

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-2(1+\lambda)\int\pi(x)\left\|\nabla\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)\right\|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq(1+\lambda)\left\|h_{\mu_{t},\pi}^{\ast}-2\left(\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}-1\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}\left\|\frac{\nabla\cdot\left(\pi\nabla\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}\right)}{\pi}\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}-\frac{2(1+\lambda)}{C_{P}}\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{t}\|\pi),

(51)

where the first part of the last inequality holds by using Cauchy Schwartz, and the second part holds by the fact that
\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{t}\|\pi)\leq\chi^{2}(\mu_{t}\|\pi)

(
Van Erven and Harremos, 2014
)
and by applying the Poincaré inequality with
f=\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}-1
(notice that
\|\nabla f\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}<\infty
from
Case one
and
\|f\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}<\infty
from condition 3),

\displaystyle\int\pi(x)\left\|\nabla\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)\right\|^{2}dx\geq\frac{1}{C_{P}}\chi^{2}(\mu_{t}\|\pi)\geq\frac{1}{C_{P}}\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{t}\|\pi).

Since
\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}-1\in\operatorname{Ran}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{r}\right)
with
r>0
, using
Lemma

B.5
we have

\displaystyle\left\|h_{\mu_{t},\pi}^{\ast}-2\left(\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}-1\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}\leq 2\lambda^{r}\left\|q_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}.

(52)

Then, notice that

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\nabla\cdot\left(\pi\nabla\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}\right)}{\pi}\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}=\int\frac{\left[\nabla\cdot\left(\pi(x)\nabla\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)\right)\right]^{2}}{\pi(x)^{2}}d\pi(x)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\left(\nabla\pi(x)^{\top}\nabla\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)+\pi(x)\nabla\cdot\nabla\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)\right)^{2}}{\pi(x)^{2}}d\pi(x)

\displaystyle=\int\left(\nabla\log\pi(x)^{\top}\nabla\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)+\Delta\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)\right)^{2}d\pi(x)

\displaystyle\leq\int 2\left(\nabla\log\pi(x)^{\top}\nabla\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)\right)^{2}+2\left(\Delta\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)\right)^{2}d\pi(x)

\displaystyle=2\left\|\nabla\left(\log\pi\right)^{\top}\nabla\left(\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}+2\left\|\Delta\left(\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}\leq 2\mathcal{J}_{t}^{2}+2\mathcal{I}_{t}^{2}.

(53)

Therefore, plugging (
52
) and (
53
) back to (
51
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{t}\|\pi)\leq 4(1+\lambda)\lambda^{r}\left\|q_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}\left(\mathcal{J}_{t}+\mathcal{I}_{t}\right)-\frac{2(1+\lambda)}{C_{P}}\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{t}\|\pi).

(54)

Case two:

\int\pi(x)\left\|\nabla\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}(x)\right\|^{2}dx=\infty
.
The first term of (
50
) remains the same, and the second term of (
50
) now equals infinity which is larger than the finite
\frac{2(1+\lambda)}{C_{P}}\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{t}\|\pi)
, so we also obtain (
54
) as in
Case one
.

Therefore, both
Case one
and
Case two
result in (
54
). Using the Gronwall lemma, we obtain that for any
T>0
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \operatorname{KL}(\mu_{T}\|\pi)&\leq\exp\left(-\frac{2(1+\lambda)}{C_{P}}T\right)\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{0}\|\pi)\\
&+4(1+\lambda)\lambda^{r}\int_{0}^{T}\exp\left(-\frac{2(1+\lambda)}{C_{P}}(T-t)\right)\left\|q_{t}\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}(\mathcal{J}_{t}+\mathcal{I}_{t})dt,\end{aligned}

which concludes the proof of
Theorem

4.1
.

10.8.1
Derivation of (
14
) under stronger range assumption
r=\frac{1}{2}
.

Notice that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, since
k
is differentiable

\displaystyle\nabla\left(\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}-1\right)(x)=\left\langle\nabla k(x,\cdot),\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}-1\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{d}}\leq\sqrt{K_{1d}}\left\|\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}-1\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}.

And since
\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}-1\in\mathcal{H}
, there exists
q_{t}\in L^{2}(\pi)
such that
\left\langle\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}=\varrho_{i}^{1/2}\left\langle q_{t},e_{i}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}
for all
i
, so

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}-1\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{1}{\varrho_{i}}\left\langle\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}=\sum_{i\geq 1}\left\langle q_{t},e_{i}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}=\|q_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}.

Combining the above two equations, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\left(\log\pi\right)^{\top}\nabla\left(\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}-1\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}\leq\sqrt{K_{1d}}\|q_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}\left\|\nabla\log\pi\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}.

Also, for the other one, notice that

\displaystyle\Delta\left(\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}\right)(x)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\mathbf{H}\left(\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}-1\right)(x)\right\|_{F}=\left\|\left\langle\mathbf{H}k(x,\cdot),\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}-1\right\rangle\right\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\mathbf{H}k(x,\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{d\times d}}\left\|\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}-1\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\sqrt{K_{2d}}\|q_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta\left(\frac{d\mu_{t}}{d\pi}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}\leq\sqrt{K_{2d}}\|q_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}.

10.9
Proof of
Proposition

5.1

We know that
\mu_{n+1}=(\mathrm{I}-\gamma(1+\lambda)\nabla h)_{\#}\mu_{n}
where we drop the subscripts of the witness function
h_{\mu_{n},\pi}
when it causes no ambiguity.
Denote
\rho_{t}=(\mathrm{I}-t(1+\lambda)\nabla h)_{\#}\mu_{n}
, so
\rho_{0}=\mu_{n}
, and
\rho_{\gamma}=\mu_{n+1}
.
Consider the difference of KL divergence between the two iterates
\mu_{n+1}
and
\mu_{n}
along the time-discretized
\operatorname{DrMMD}
flow:

\displaystyle\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n+1}\|\pi)-\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{KL}(\rho_{\gamma}\|\pi)-\operatorname{KL}(\rho_{0}\|\pi)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}\operatorname{KL}(\rho_{t}\|\pi)\gamma+\int_{0}^{\gamma}(\gamma-t)\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\operatorname{KL}(\rho_{t}\|\pi)dt.

(55)

For the first term of (
55
),

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}\operatorname{KL}(\rho_{t}\|\pi)

\displaystyle=-(1+\lambda)\E_{\mu_{n}}\left[\nabla\log\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}^{\top}\nabla h\right]=-(1+\lambda)\E_{\pi}\left[\nabla\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}^{\top}\nabla h\right]

\displaystyle=-2(1+\lambda)\left\|\nabla\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}+(1+\lambda)\E_{\pi}\left[\nabla\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}^{\top}\left(2\nabla\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}-\nabla h\right)\right]

\displaystyle=-2(1+\lambda)\left\|\nabla\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}+(1+\lambda)\int\left(\nabla\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}(x)\right)^{\top}\left(2\nabla\left(\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}(x)-1\right)-\nabla h(x)\right)\pi(x)dx

\displaystyle=-2(1+\lambda)\left\|\nabla\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}-(1+\lambda)\int\left(2\left(\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}(x)-1\right)-h(x)\right)\nabla\cdot\left(\pi(x)\nabla\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}(x)\right)dx

\displaystyle\leq-(1+\lambda)\frac{2}{C_{P}}\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)+(1+\lambda)\left\|h-2\left(\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}-1\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}\left\|\frac{\nabla\cdot\left(\pi\nabla\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}\right)}{\pi}\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)},

(56)

where the fourth equality uses an integration by parts, and the last inequality uses Poincaré inequality for the first term under similar arguments in
Section

10.8
and uses Cauchy-Schwarz for the second term.
Using
Lemma

B.5
and the derivations in (
53
), (
56
) can be further upper bounded by

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}\operatorname{KL}(\rho_{t}\|\pi)\leq-(1+\lambda)\frac{2}{C_{P}}\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)+2(1+\lambda)\lambda^{r}Q\left(\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I}\right).

(57)

Then, for the second term of (
55
), we know from Example 15.9 of
Villani and others (2009)
(taking
m=1
) that,

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\operatorname{KL}(\rho_{t}\|\pi)

\displaystyle=(1+\lambda)^{2}\int\nabla h(x)^{\top}\mathbf{H}V(\varphi_{t}(x))\nabla h(x)d\mu_{n}(x)

\displaystyle+(1+\lambda)^{2}\int\left\|\mathbf{H}h(x)\left(\mathrm{I}-t(1+\lambda)\mathbf{H}h(x)\right)^{-1}\right\|_{F}^{2}d\mu_{n}(x).

Because
2(1+\lambda)\gamma\sqrt{\chi^{2}\left(\mu_{0}\|\pi\right)\frac{K_{2d}}{\lambda}}\leq\frac{\zeta-1}{\zeta}
for
1<\zeta<2
, applying
Lemma

B.7
we have,

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\operatorname{KL}(\rho_{t}\|\pi)\leq 4(1+\lambda)^{2}\beta\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\frac{K_{1d}}{\lambda}+4(1+\lambda)^{2}\zeta^{2}\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\frac{K_{2d}}{\lambda}.

(58)

Combining the above two inequalities (
57
) and (
58
) and plugging them back into (
55
), we obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n+1}\|\pi)-\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{2}{C_{P}}(1+\lambda)\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\gamma+2(1+\lambda)\gamma\lambda^{r}Q\left(\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I}\right)

\displaystyle+2(1+\lambda)^{2}\gamma^{2}(\beta+\zeta^{2})\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\frac{K_{1d}+K_{2d}}{\lambda}

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{2}{C_{P}}\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\gamma

\displaystyle+4\gamma\lambda^{r}Q\left(\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I}\right)+8\gamma^{2}(\beta+\zeta^{2})\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\frac{K_{1d}+K_{2d}}{\lambda},

where the last inequality holds by using
0<\lambda\leq 1
, and the result follows.

10.10
Proof of
Theorem

5.1

In order to use
Proposition

5.1
in the proof, first we are going to show that
Proposition

5.1
holds under the conditions of
Theorem

5.1
.
Notice that conditions 1-4 of
Proposition

5.1
are precisely the conditions 1-4 of
Theorem

5.1
. To use
Proposition

5.1
in the proof of
Theorem

5.1
, the only thing left is to check that the condition of step size
\gamma
in (
16
) is satisfied.

In
Theorem

5.1
,
\lambda_{n}
is selected to be
\left(2\gamma\frac{\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)(\beta+\zeta^{2})(K_{1d}+K_{2d})}{Q(\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I})}\right)^{\frac{1}{r+1}}\wedge 1
.
If
\lambda_{n}
is taken to be the former, then

\displaystyle\quad 2\gamma(1+\lambda_{n})\sqrt{\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\frac{K_{2d}}{\lambda_{n}}}\leq 4\gamma\sqrt{\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\frac{K_{2d}}{\lambda_{n}}}

\displaystyle=2(2\gamma)^{\frac{2r+1}{2r+2}}\chi^{2}\left(\mu_{n}\|\pi\right)^{\frac{r}{2r+2}}\left(Q(\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I})\right)^{\frac{1}{2r+2}}\left(\frac{1}{K_{1d}+K_{2d}}\frac{1}{\beta+\zeta^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2r+2}}K_{2d}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{\leq}}\left(8\gamma\right)^{\frac{2r+1}{2r+2}}Q^{\frac{2r+1}{2r+2}}(\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I})^{\frac{1}{2r+2}}\left(\frac{1}{\beta+\zeta^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2r+2}}K_{2d}^{\frac{r}{2r+2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\zeta-1}{\zeta}\left(\frac{1}{\beta+\zeta^{2}}\right)^{\frac{r}{2r+2}}\leq\frac{\zeta-1}{\zeta}.

(59)

(*)
holds because
\frac{K_{2d}}{K_{1d}+K_{2d}}\leq 1
and

\displaystyle\chi^{2}\left(\mu_{n}\|\pi\right)=\left\|\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}-1\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}=\left\|\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{r}q_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}\leq K^{2r}Q^{2}\leq Q^{2}.

(60)

The second last inequality of (
59
) holds due to the constraint on
\gamma
in (
19
), and the last inequality of (
59
) holds because
\beta+\zeta^{2}\geq 1
.

On the other hand, if
\lambda_{n}
is chosen to be
1
, similarly based on the constraint on
\gamma
in (
19
), we have

\displaystyle 2\gamma(1+\lambda_{n})\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\frac{K_{2d}}{\lambda_{n}}

\displaystyle=4\gamma\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)K_{2d}\leq\frac{\zeta-1}{\zeta}.

Therefore, all the conditions of
Proposition

5.1
have been verified.
So, if we select
\lambda_{n}=\left(2\gamma\frac{\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)(\beta+\zeta^{2})(K_{1d}+K_{2d})}{Q(\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I})}\right)^{\frac{1}{r+1}}\wedge 1
, then

\displaystyle\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n+1}\|\pi)-\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)

\displaystyle\leq-\gamma\frac{2}{C_{P}}\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)

\displaystyle\qquad+\underbrace{4\gamma\lambda_{n}^{r}Q\left(\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I}\right)}_{(\Delta_{1})}+\underbrace{8\gamma^{2}(\beta+\zeta^{2})\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\frac{K_{1d}+K_{2d}}{\lambda_{n}}}_{(\Delta_{2})}.

(61)

By observing (
61
), the first term on the right-hand side
-\frac{2}{C_{P}}\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\gamma
is strictly negative and is decreasing KL divergence at each iteration
n
of the
\operatorname{DrMMD}
gradient descent.
In contrast, the second term
(\Delta_{1}):=4\gamma\lambda_{n}^{r}Q(\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I})
and the third term
(\Delta_{2}):=8\gamma^{2}(\beta+\zeta^{2})\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\frac{K_{1d}+K_{2d}}{\lambda_{n}}
are positive and prevent the KL divergence from decreasing.
Denote
G(\lambda_{n})=(\Delta_{1})+(\Delta_{2})
and the optimal
\lambda_{n}
is achieved by taking
\frac{d}{d\lambda_{n}}G(\lambda_{n})=0
, which leads to
\lambda_{n}=\left(2\gamma\frac{\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)(\beta+\zeta^{2})(K_{1d}+K_{2d})}{Q(\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I})}\right)^{\frac{1}{r+1}}
.
Plugging the value of
\lambda_{n}
back to (
61
) to obtain,

\displaystyle\quad\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n+1}\|\pi)-\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{2}{C_{P}}\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\gamma+4\gamma\left(2\gamma\right)^{\frac{r}{r+1}}\left(\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)(\beta+\zeta^{2})(K_{1d}+K_{2d})\right)^{\frac{r}{r+1}}\left(Q(\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I})\right)^{\frac{1}{r+1}}

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{2}{C_{P}}\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\gamma+8\gamma^{1+\frac{r}{r+1}}\left(\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)(\beta+\zeta^{2})(K_{1d}+K_{2d})\right)^{\frac{r}{r+1}}\left(Q(\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I})\right)^{\frac{1}{r+1}}

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{2}{C_{P}}\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\gamma+8\gamma^{1+\frac{r}{r+1}}Q^{\frac{2r+1}{r+1}}\Big((K_{1d}+K_{2d})(\beta+\zeta^{2})\Big)^{\frac{r}{r+1}}(\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I})^{\frac{1}{r+1}},

(62)

where the last inequality holds because of (
60
).
Since
\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\geq\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)

(
Van Erven and Harremos, 2014
, Equation (7))
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n+1}\|\pi)

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\gamma\frac{2}{C_{P}}\right)\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+8\gamma^{1+\frac{r}{r+1}}Q^{\frac{2r+1}{r+1}}\Big((K_{1d}+K_{2d})(\beta+\zeta^{2})\Big)^{\frac{r}{r+1}}(\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I})^{\frac{1}{r+1}}.

After iterating, we obtain

\displaystyle\quad\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n_{max}}\|\pi)

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\gamma\frac{2}{C_{P}}\right)^{n_{max}}\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{0}\|\pi)+4\gamma^{\frac{r}{r+1}}C_{P}Q^{\frac{2r+1}{r+1}}\Big((K_{1d}+K_{2d})(\beta+\zeta^{2})\Big)^{\frac{r}{r+1}}(\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I})^{\frac{1}{r+1}}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{2n_{max}\gamma}{C_{P}}\right)\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{0}\|\pi)+4\gamma^{\frac{r}{r+1}}C_{P}Q^{\frac{2r+1}{r+1}}\Big((K_{1d}+K_{2d})(\beta+\zeta^{2})\Big)^{\frac{r}{r+1}}(\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I})^{\frac{1}{r+1}}

and the result follows.

10.11
Proof of
Theorem

5.2

The proof of
Theorem

5.2
is also based on
Proposition

5.1
proved in the last section.
Recalling (
62
) yet with adaptive step size
\gamma_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle\quad\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n+1}\|\pi)-\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{2}{C_{P}}\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\gamma_{n}+8\gamma_{n}^{1+\frac{r}{r+1}}\left(\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)(\beta+\zeta^{2})(K_{1d}+K_{2d})\right)^{\frac{r}{r+1}}\left(Q(\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I})\right)^{\frac{1}{r+1}}.

From (
21
), we have

\displaystyle 8\gamma_{n}^{\frac{r}{r+1}}\Big((K_{1d}+K_{2d})(\beta+\zeta^{2})\Big)^{\frac{r}{r+1}}(Q(\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{I}))^{\frac{1}{r+1}}\leq\frac{1}{C_{P}}\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)^{\frac{1}{r+1}},

so that we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n+1}\|\pi)-\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\leq-\frac{1}{C_{P}}\chi^{2}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\gamma_{n}\leq-\frac{1}{C_{P}}\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n}\|\pi)\gamma_{n}.

Hence

\displaystyle\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n+1}\|\pi)\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{C_{P}}\gamma_{n}\right)\operatorname{KL}(\mu_{n}\|\pi).

(63)

After iterating
n
from
1
to
n_{\max}
, the theorem is proved.

10.12
Proof of
Theorem

6.1

In order to analyze the error of space discretization, we introduce another particle descent scheme using the population witness function
h_{\mu_{n},\pi}^{\ast}
defined in (
15
) starting from the same initialization as that of (
24
),

\displaystyle\overline{y}_{n+1}^{(i)}=\overline{y}_{n}^{(i)}-\gamma(1+\lambda_{n})\nabla h_{\mu_{n},\pi}^{\ast}(\overline{y}_{n}^{(i)}),\quad\overline{y}_{0}^{(i)}=y_{0}^{(i)}.\vskip-3.0pt

(64)

The corresponding empirical distribution of the particles at time step
n
is defined as
\overline{\mu}_{n}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\overline{y}_{n}^{(i)}
.
Note that (
64
) is an unbiased sampled version (since it is composed of
N
i.i.d. realizations) of (
15
).
The following proposition shows that
W_{2}(\overline{\mu}_{n},\hat{\mu}_{n})\to 0
as
N,M\to\infty
, i.e., with a sufficient number of samples from
\mu_{0}
and
\pi
,
(
24
) can approximate (
64
) with
arbitrary precision.
The proof of
Proposition

10.1
is provided in
Section

10.13
.

Proposition 10.1
.

Suppose
k
satisfies
Assumption

1
and
2
. Given initial particles
\{y_{0}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N}
that are i.i.d sampled from
\mu_{0}
, a sequence
\left(\overline{\mu}_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of empirical distributions arising from (
64
), and a sequence
\left(\hat{\mu}_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
arising from (
24
), then for all
n\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\E[W_{2}\left(\hat{\mu}_{n},\overline{\mu}_{n}\right)]\leq A\left(\frac{K}{\sqrt{M}\tilde{\lambda}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{M}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\right)\left(\exp\left(\gamma n\frac{2(1+\tilde{\lambda})R}{\tilde{\lambda}}\right)-1\right),

where
A=\frac{2\sqrt{KK_{1d}}}{\sqrt{KK_{2d}}+K_{1d}}
and
R=K_{1d}+\sqrt{KK_{2d}}
are constants that only depend on the kernel, and
\tilde{\lambda}=\min\limits_{i=1,\ldots,n}\lambda_{i}
denotes the smallest regularization coefficient.

Now we are ready to prove
Theorem

6.1
. By the triangle inequality, we have,

\displaystyle\E\left[W_{2}\left(\hat{\mu}_{n},\pi\right)\right]\leq\E\left[W_{2}\left(\hat{\mu}_{n},\bar{\mu}_{n}\right)\right]+\E\left[W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu}_{n},\mu_{n}\right)\right]+W_{2}\left(\mu_{n},\pi\right).

From
Proposition

10.1
, the first term is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\E[W_{2}\left(\hat{\mu}_{n},\overline{\mu}_{n}\right)]

\displaystyle\leq A\left(\frac{K}{\sqrt{M}\tilde{\lambda}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{M}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\right)\left(\exp\left(n\gamma\frac{2(1+\tilde{\lambda})R}{\tilde{\lambda}}\right)-1\right)

\displaystyle\leq A\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{M}\tilde{\lambda}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\right)\left(\exp\left(\frac{4n\gamma R}{\tilde{\lambda}}\right)-1\right),

where the second inequality uses
\tilde{\lambda}\leq 1
and
K\leq 1
. Since
(\mu_{n})_{n}
has finite fourth moment, then by taking
p=2,q=4
in
(
Lei, 2020
, Theorem 3.1)
and
(
Fournier and Guillin, 2015
, Theorem 1)
, the second term is upper bounded by,

\displaystyle\E\left[W_{2}\left(\bar{\mu}_{n},\mu_{n}\right)\right]=\mathcal{O}\left(N^{-\frac{1}{d\vee 4}}\right).

For the third term, since the Wasserstein-2 distance is upper bounded by the square root of the KL divergence, if the target
\pi
that satisfies Talagrand-2 inequality with constant
C_{T}

(
Villani and others, 2009
, Definition 22.1)
, we have

\displaystyle W_{2}\left(\mu_{n},\pi\right)\leq\sqrt{2C_{T}}\sqrt{\operatorname{KL}\left(\mu_{n}\|\pi\right)}\leq\sqrt{2C_{T}}\exp\left(-\frac{n\gamma}{C_{P}}\right)\sqrt{\operatorname{KL}\left(\mu_{0}\|\pi\right)}+\mathcal{O}\left(\gamma^{\frac{r}{2r+2}}\right),

where the last inequality follows from
Theorem

5.1
.
Combining the above three terms, we obtain

\displaystyle\E\left[W_{2}\left(\hat{\mu}_{n},\pi\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq A\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{M}\tilde{\lambda}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\right)\exp\left(\frac{4n\gamma R}{\tilde{\lambda}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(N^{-\frac{1}{d\vee 4}}\right)

\displaystyle+\sqrt{2C_{T}}\exp\left(-\frac{n\gamma}{C_{P}}\right)\sqrt{\operatorname{KL}\left(\mu_{0}\|\pi\right)}+\mathcal{O}\left(\gamma^{\frac{r}{2r+2}}\right),

(65)

where
