Appendix A: Further Background on
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2})

Let
\mu
and
\pi
be two probability measures in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and let
\Pi(\mu,\pi)
denote the set of all admissible transport plans between
\mu
and
\pi
, i.e.,
\Pi(\mu,\pi)=\{\Gamma\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d});\left(\operatorname{proj}_{1}\right)_{\#}\Gamma=\mu,\left(\operatorname{proj}_{2}\right)_{\#}\Gamma=\pi\}
, where
\operatorname{proj}_{1}
and
\operatorname{proj}_{2}
respectively stand for projection maps
(x,y)\mapsto x
and
(x,y)\mapsto y
, and
\#
is the pushforward operator. The Wasserstein-2 distance between
\mu
and
\pi
is then defined as

W_{2}(\mu,\pi)=\left(\inf_{\Gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\pi)}\int\|x-y\|^{2}d\Gamma(x,y)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

and
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2})
is a metric space called the Wasserstein space
(
Panaretos and Zemel, 2020
)
.
Brenier’s theorem guarantees that if
\mu
is an absolutely continuous measure, then the optimal transport map is unique and is of the form
\Gamma^{\ast}=(\mathrm{I},T)_{\#}\mu
, i.e.,
T_{\#}\mu=\pi

(
Santambrogio, 2017
)
.
T
can also be expressed as
T(x)=x+\nabla\phi(x)
, where
\phi
is known as the Kantorovich potential function and is differentiable
\mu-
a.e.

For an absolutely continuous
\mu
and the optimal transport plan
T
such that
T_{\#}\mu=\pi
, the shortest path
(\rho_{t})_{0\leq t\leq 1}
from
\mu
to
\pi
is called the (Wasserstein) geodesic given by the following form:

\displaystyle\rho_{t}=\left((1-t)\mathrm{I}+tT\right)_{\#}\mu=\left(\mathrm{I}+t\nabla\phi\right)_{\#}\mu.

Therefore, in this paper, we always use
(\mathrm{I}+t\nabla\phi)_{\#}\mu
with

\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
8
8

8

\phi
is compactly supported because the tangent space of
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is
\overline{\{\nabla\psi,\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\}}^{L^{2}(\mu)}

(
Ambrosio et al., 2005
, Definition 8.4.1)
.
to define a geodesic curve that starts at
\mu
.
Define
\varphi_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},x\mapsto x+t\nabla\phi(x)
, then
\omega_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},x\mapsto\left[\nabla\phi\circ\varphi_{t}^{-1}\right](x)
becomes the optimal transport map from
\rho_{t}
to
\pi
. Notice that
\|\omega_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\rho_{t})}=\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
for all
t\in[0,1]
, so

(\rho_{t})_{0\leq t\leq 1}
is also a constant-speed geodesic.
The notion of a constant-speed geodesic is crucial in the introduction of geodesic convexity below.

A functional
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
is geodesically convex if for any
\mu
and
\pi
, the following inequality holds:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\left(\rho_{t}\right)\leq(1-t)\mathcal{F}\left(\mu\right)+t\mathcal{F}\left(\pi\right),\quad\forall t\in[0,1],

(A.1)

where
\left(\rho_{t}\right)_{t\in[0,1]}
is the constant-speed geodesic between
\mu
and
\pi
.
The geodesic convexity of
\mathcal{F}
can be equivalently characterized through the Wasserstein Hessian
(
Villani and others, 2009
)
.
The geodesic convexity ought not to be confused with mixture convexity, which replaces displacement geodesic
\rho_{t}
in (
A.1
) with the mixture geodesic
\nu_{t}=(1-t)\mu+t\pi
.

Appendix B: Auxiliary Results

In this appendix, we collect all technical results required to prove the main results of the paper.

Lemma B.1
.

For
\mu\ll\pi
,
\chi^{2}
-divergence admits the following variational form:

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(\mu\|\pi)=\sup_{h\in L^{2}(\pi)}\int hd\mu-\int\left(h+\frac{1}{4}h^{2}\right)d\pi,

where it is sufficient to restrict the variational set to
L^{2}(\pi)
in contrast to the set of all measurable functions for general
f
-divergences
(
Nowozin et al., 2016
;
Nguyen et al., 2010
)
.

Proof.

For
\mu\ll\pi
, we have:

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(\mu\|\pi)=\sup_{h}\left\{\int hd\mu-\int\left(\frac{h^{2}}{4}+h\right)d\pi\right\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{h}\left\{\int h\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}d\pi-\int\left(\frac{h^{2}}{4}+h\right)d\pi\right\}=\sup_{h}\left\{\int\left(h\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-\frac{h^{2}}{4}-h\right)d\pi\right\}

\displaystyle=-\inf_{h}\left\{\int\left[\frac{h^{2}}{4}-h\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right)\right]d\pi\right\}

\displaystyle=-\inf_{h}\left\{\int\left[\frac{h}{2}-\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right)\right]^{2}d\pi\right\}+\int\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right)^{2}d\pi.

Clearly, the above equation is minimized at
h^{*}=2(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1)
and
\chi^{2}(\mu\|\pi)=\int(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1)^{2}d\pi
which is finite if and only if
\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\in L^{2}(\pi)
.
Therefore, it is sufficient to consider the above maximization over
L^{2}(\pi)
.
∎

Lemma B.2
.

Under
Assumption

1
and
2
, the mappings
x\mapsto k(x,\cdot)
and
x\mapsto\nabla_{1}k(x,\cdot)
are differentiable and Lipschitz:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \left\|k(x,\cdot)-k(y,\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}&\leq\sqrt{K_{1d}}\|x-y\|,\\
\left\|\nabla_{1}k(x,\cdot)-\nabla_{1}k(y,\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{d}}&\leq\sqrt{K_{2d}}\|x-y\|.\end{aligned}

Proof.

This is Lemma 7 from
Glaser et al. (2021)
.
∎

Lemma B.3
.

Under
Assumption

1
and
2
, the regularized kernel
\tilde{k}
defined in (
6
) satisfies the following properties:

1.

\big|\tilde{k}(x,y)\big|\leq\frac{K}{\lambda}
;

2.

\partial_{i}\tilde{k}(x,y)=\big\langle(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I})^{-1}\partial_{i}k(x,\cdot),k(y,\cdot)\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
;

3.

\big\|\nabla_{1}\tilde{k}(x,y)\big\|^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\partial_{i}\tilde{k}(x,y)^{2}\leq\frac{KK_{1d}}{\lambda^{2}}
;

4.

\partial_{i}\partial_{i+d}\tilde{k}(x,y)=\big\langle\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1}\partial_{i}k(x,\cdot),\partial_{i}k(y,\cdot)\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
;

5.

\big\|\nabla_{1}\nabla_{2}\tilde{k}(x,y)\big\|_{F}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\partial_{i}\partial_{i+d}\tilde{k}(x,y)^{2}\leq\frac{K_{1d}}{\lambda^{2}}
;

6.

\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\tilde{k}(x,y)=\big\langle\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}k(x,\cdot),k(y,\cdot)\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
;

7.

\big\|\mathbf{H}_{1}\tilde{k}(x,y)\big\|_{F}^{2}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\tilde{k}(x,y)^{2}\leq\frac{KK_{2d}}{\lambda^{2}}
;

8.

\big\|\nabla_{1}\tilde{k}\left(x,x^{\prime}\right)-\nabla_{1}\tilde{k}\left(y,y^{\prime}\right)\big\|\leq\frac{\sqrt{KK_{2d}}}{\lambda}\left(\left\|x-y\right\|+\left\|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}\right\|\right)
.

Proof.

Notice that

\displaystyle\tilde{k}(x,y)=\left\langle\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1}k(x,\cdot),k(y,\cdot)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\left\|k(x,\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\left\|k(y,\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\frac{K}{\lambda},

so the first bullet point is proved.
Before we prove the second bullet point, we first prove the differentiability of
x\mapsto\tilde{k}(x,y)
.
For
i\in\{1,\cdots,d\}
, consider
h\in\mathbb{R}
and denote
\Delta_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
as a vector of all
0
except the value at
i
being equal to
h
. Then, for any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\tilde{k}(x+\Delta_{i},y)-\tilde{k}(x,y)}{h}=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\left\langle(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I})^{-1}\left(k(x+\Delta_{i},\cdot)-k(x,\cdot)\right),k(y,\cdot)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}}{h}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\sqrt{K}}{\lambda}\frac{\left\|k(x,\cdot)-k(x+\Delta_{i},\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}}{h}\leq\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\sqrt{K}}{\lambda}\sqrt{K_{1d}}\frac{\|\Delta_{i}\|}{h}=\frac{\sqrt{K}}{\lambda}\sqrt{K_{1d}}.

So
x\mapsto\tilde{k}(x,y)
is differentiable for any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Since the kernel
k
is differentiable per
Assumption

2
, for any

f\in\mathcal{H}
,
\partial_{x_{i}}f(x)=\big\langle\partial_{i}k(x,\cdot),f\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\tilde{k}(x,y)=\partial_{x_{i}}\left\langle k(x,\cdot),\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1}k(y,\cdot)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\left\langle\partial_{i}k(x,\cdot),\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1}k(y,\cdot)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}.

So the second bullet point is proved.

Next, notice that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{1}\tilde{k}(x,y)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left\langle\partial_{i}k(x,\cdot),\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1}k(y,\cdot)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left\|\partial_{i}k(x,\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\left\|k(y,\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\left\|\nabla_{1}k(x,\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{d}}^{2}\left\|k(y,\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\frac{KK_{1d}}{\lambda^{2}}.

So the third bullet point is proved.
Similar arguments above lead to bullet points
4
to
7
.

Finally, to prove bullet point
8
, notice that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{1}\tilde{k}(x,x^{\prime})-\nabla_{1}\tilde{k}(y,x^{\prime})\right\|^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left\langle\left(\partial_{i}k(x,\cdot)-\partial_{i}k(y,\cdot)\right),\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1}k(x^{\prime},\cdot)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left\|\partial_{i}k(x,\cdot)-\partial_{i}k(y,\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\left\|k(x^{\prime},\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\left\|\nabla_{1}k(x,\cdot)-\nabla_{1}k(y,\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{d}}^{2}\left\|k(x^{\prime},\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{KK_{2d}}{\lambda^{2}}\left\|x-y\right\|^{2},

where the last inequality uses
Lemma

B.2
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{1}\tilde{k}(x,x^{\prime})-\nabla_{1}\tilde{k}(y,y^{\prime})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\nabla_{1}\tilde{k}(x,x^{\prime})-\nabla_{1}\tilde{k}(y,x^{\prime})\right\|+\left\|\nabla_{1}\tilde{k}(y,x^{\prime})-\nabla_{1}\tilde{k}(y,y^{\prime})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{KK_{2d}}}{\lambda}\left(\|x-y\|+\|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}\|\right).

So the bullet point
8
is proved.
∎

Lemma B.4
.

For any two distributions
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with associated
\operatorname{DrMMD}
witness functions
h_{\mu_{0},\pi}^{\ast},h_{\mu_{1},\pi}^{\ast}
defined in
Proposition

3.2
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|h_{\mu_{1},\pi}^{\ast}-h_{\mu_{0},\pi}^{\ast}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\frac{2\sqrt{K_{1d}}}{\lambda}W_{2}\left(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\right),\quad\text{and}\quad\left\|h_{\mu_{0},\pi}^{\ast}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\frac{2\sqrt{K}}{\lambda}.

Both the witness function
h_{\mu,\pi}^{\ast}
and its gradient
\nabla h_{\mu,\pi}^{\ast}
are Lipschitz continuous, i.e.,

\displaystyle\Big|h_{\mu_{1},\pi}^{\ast}(x)-h_{\mu_{0},\pi}^{\ast}(y)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq L\Big(W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})+\left\|x-y\right\|\Big);

\displaystyle\Big\|\nabla h_{\mu_{1},\pi}^{\ast}(x)-\nabla h_{\mu_{0},\pi}^{\ast}(y)\Big\|

\displaystyle\leq L\Big(W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})+\left\|x-y\right\|\Big),

where the constant
L=\frac{1}{\lambda}\max\left\{2\sqrt{KK_{1d}},2\sqrt{KK_{2d}},2K_{1d}\right\}
.

Proof.

Let
\gamma\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
be the optimal coupling between
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
. Then

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\left\|h_{\mu_{1},\pi}^{\ast}-h_{\mu_{0},\pi}^{\ast}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=4\left\|\Big(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\Big)^{-1}\left(\int k(x,\cdot)d(\mu_{1}-\mu_{0})\right)\right\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}\\
&\leq\frac{4}{\lambda^{2}}\left\|\int k(x,\cdot)-k(y,\cdot)d\gamma(x,y)\right\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\frac{4K_{1d}}{\lambda^{2}}\int\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}d\gamma(x,y)=\frac{4K_{1d}}{\lambda^{2}}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}),\end{aligned}

where the first inequality holds because
\Sigma_{\pi}
is a positive and self-adjoint operator, and the second inequality uses
Lemma

B.2
. Also note that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \left\|h_{\mu_{0},\pi}^{\ast}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}=\left\|2\Big(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\Big)^{-1}\left(\int k(x,\cdot)d\mu_{0}-\int k(x,\cdot)d\pi\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\frac{2\sqrt{K}}{\lambda}.\end{aligned}

So the first part has been proved. Furthermore, note that

\displaystyle\Big|h_{\mu_{1},\pi}^{\ast}(x)-h_{\mu_{0},\pi}^{\ast}(y)\Big|\leq\Big|h_{\mu_{1},\pi}^{\ast}(x)-h_{\mu_{0},\pi}^{\ast}(x)\Big|+\Big|h_{\mu_{0},\pi}^{\ast}(x)-h_{\mu_{0},\pi}^{\ast}(y)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|k(x,\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\left\|h_{\mu_{1},\pi}^{\ast}-h_{\mu_{0},\pi}^{\ast}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}+\left\|k(x,\cdot)-k(y,\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\left\|h_{\mu_{0},\pi}^{\ast}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\sqrt{KK_{1d}}}{\lambda}W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})+\frac{2\sqrt{KK_{1d}}}{\lambda}\left\|x-y\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\sqrt{KK_{1d}}}{\lambda}\Big(W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})+\left\|x-y\right\|\Big)\leq L\Big(W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})+\left\|x-y\right\|\Big)

and

\displaystyle\quad\Big\|\nabla h_{\mu_{1},\pi}^{\ast}(x)-\nabla h_{\mu_{0},\pi}^{\ast}(y)\Big\|\leq\Big\|\nabla h_{\mu_{1},\pi}^{\ast}(x)-\nabla h_{\mu_{0},\pi}^{\ast}(x)\Big\|+\Big\|\nabla h_{\mu_{0},\pi}^{\ast}(x)-\nabla h_{\mu_{0},\pi}^{\ast}(y)\Big\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\nabla_{1}k(x,\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{d}}\left\|h_{\mu_{1},\pi}^{\ast}-h_{\mu_{0},\pi}^{\ast}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}+\left\|\nabla_{1}k(x,\cdot)-\nabla_{1}k(y,\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{d}}\left\|h_{\mu_{0},\pi}^{\ast}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2K_{1d}}{\lambda}W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})+\frac{2\sqrt{KK_{2d}}}{\lambda}\left\|x-y\right\|\leq L\Big(W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})+\left\|x-y\right\|\Big)

and the result follows.
∎

Lemma B.5
.

Given two probability measures
\mu\ll\pi
that are both absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure. If
\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\in\operatorname{Ran}(\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{r})
with
r>0
, i.e., there exists
q\in L^{2}(\pi)
such that
\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1=\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{r}q
, then

\displaystyle\left\|h-2\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}\leq 2\lambda^{r}\left\|q\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)},

where
h=2(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I})^{-1}(m_{\pi}-m_{\mu})
is defined in
Proposition

3.2
.

Proof.

Given the assumption that
\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\in\operatorname{Ran}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{r}\right)
with
r>0
, there exists
q\in L^{2}(\pi)
such that
\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1=\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{r}q
and
\langle\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}=\varrho_{i}^{r}{\left\langle q,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}
.
Since
\mu\ll\pi
, the Mercer decomposition of
k
in (
1
) holds for any
x
in the support of
\mu
and in the support of
\pi
,

\displaystyle m_{\mu}-m_{\pi}=\int k(x,\cdot)d(\mu-\pi)(x)=\sum_{i\geq 1}\varrho_{i}\left(\int e_{i}(x)d(\mu-\pi)(x)\right)e_{i}=\varrho_{i}{\left\langle\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}e_{i}.

(B.1)

So we have,

\displaystyle\left\|h-2\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}=2\left\|\left(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\right)^{-1}\left(m_{\mu}-m_{\pi}\right)-\left(\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}

\displaystyle=2\left\|\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}{\left\langle\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}e_{i}-\sum_{i\geq 1}{\left\langle\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}e_{i}\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}

\displaystyle=2\left\|\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\lambda}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}{\left\langle\frac{d\mu}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}e_{i}\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}=2\left\|\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\lambda\varrho_{i}^{r}}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}{\left\langle q,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}e_{i}\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}

\displaystyle\leq 2\lambda^{r}\left\|q\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)},

where the last inequality is obtained by using

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda\varrho_{i}^{r}}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}=\left(\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\right)^{r}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\right)^{1-r}\lambda^{r}\leq\lambda^{r}.

Hence the proof.
∎

Lemma B.6
.

Let
\rho\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)
and
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)
. Consider the path
(\rho_{s})_{0\leq s\leq 1}
from
\rho
to
(\mathrm{I}+\nabla\phi)_{\#}\rho
given by
\rho_{s}=(\mathrm{I}+s\nabla\phi)_{\#}\rho
.
Define
\varphi_{s}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},x\mapsto x+s\nabla\phi(x)
. Let

\tilde{k}
be the regularized kernel defined in (
6
) along with its associated RKHS
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}
.
The mapping
s\mapsto\operatorname{DrMMD}(\rho_{s}\|\pi)
is continuous and differentiable, and its first-order time derivative is given by

\displaystyle\quad\frac{d}{ds}\operatorname{DrMMD}(\rho_{s}||\pi)

\displaystyle=2(1+\lambda)\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\left(\int\nabla_{1}\tilde{k}(\varphi_{s}(x),\varphi_{s}(z))d\rho(z)-\int\nabla_{1}\tilde{k}(\varphi_{s}(x),z)d\pi(z)\right)d\rho(x).

(B.2)

Moreover,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\operatorname{DrMMD}(\rho_{s}||\pi)

\displaystyle=2(1+\lambda)\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\left(\int\nabla_{1}\tilde{k}(x,z)d\rho(z)-\int\nabla_{1}\tilde{k}(x,z)d\pi(z)\right)d\rho(x).

(B.3)

Additionally, the mapping
s\to\frac{d}{ds}\operatorname{DrMMD}(\rho_{s}\|\pi)
is continuous and differentiable, and the second-order time derivative of
\operatorname{DrMMD}(\rho_{s}\|\pi)
is given by

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{d^{2}}{ds^{2}}\operatorname{DrMMD}(\rho_{s}||\pi)=2(1+\lambda)\iint\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\nabla_{1}\nabla_{2}\tilde{k}(\varphi_{s}(x),\varphi_{s}(z))\nabla\phi(z)d\rho(x)d\rho(z)

(B.4)

\displaystyle+2(1+\lambda)\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\left(\int\mathbf{H}_{1}\tilde{k}\left(\varphi_{s}(x),\varphi_{s}(z)\right)d\rho(z)-\int\mathbf{H}_{1}\tilde{k}\left(\varphi_{s}(x),z\right)d\pi(z)\right)\nabla\phi(x)d\rho(x),

with

\displaystyle\quad\frac{d^{2}}{ds^{2}}\Big|_{s=0}\operatorname{DrMMD}(\rho_{s}||\pi)=2(1+\lambda)\iint\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\nabla_{1}\nabla_{2}\tilde{k}(x,z)\nabla\phi(z)d\rho(x)d\rho(z)

\displaystyle+2(1+\lambda)\int\nabla\phi(x)^{\top}\left(\int\mathbf{H}_{1}\tilde{k}\left(x,z\right)d\rho(z)-\int\mathbf{H}_{1}\tilde{k}\left(x,z\right)d\pi(z)\right)\nabla\phi(x)d\rho(x).

(B.5)

Proof.

Recall that
\operatorname{DrMMD}
is, up to a multiplicative factor of
(1+\lambda)
,
\operatorname{MMD}^{2}
of the regularized kernel
\tilde{k}
defined in (
6
) and the associated RKHS
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}
.
From
Lemma

B.3
, we know that assumptions (A) and (B) of
Arbel et al. (2019)
are satisfied for the regularized kernel
\tilde{k}
, so using Lemma 22 and Lemma 23 from
Arbel et al. (2019)
, (
B.2
) and (
B.4
) are proved.
Then (
B.3
) and (
B.5
) are subsequently proved by taking
s=0
.
∎

Lemma B.7
.

For
\mu_{0}\ll\pi
, define
h=2(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I})^{-1}(m_{\pi}-m_{\mu_{0}})
and
\varphi_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},x\mapsto x-t(1+\lambda)\nabla h(x)
. Suppose
\pi\propto\exp(-V)
,
\mathbf{H}V\preceq\beta\mathrm{I}
, and the step size
\gamma
satisfies

\displaystyle 2(1+\lambda)\gamma\sqrt{\chi^{2}(\mu_{0}\|\pi)\frac{K_{2d}}{\lambda}}\leq\frac{\zeta-1}{\zeta}

for some constant
1<\zeta<2
. Then, for
t\in[0,\gamma]
, the following inequalities hold,

\displaystyle\int\nabla h(x)^{\top}\mathbf{H}V(\varphi_{t}(x))\nabla h(x)d\mu_{0}(x)

\displaystyle\leq 4\beta\chi^{2}(\mu_{0}\|\pi)\frac{K_{1d}}{\lambda};

(B.6)

\displaystyle\int\left\|\mathbf{H}h(x)\left(\mathrm{I}-t(1+\lambda)\mathbf{H}h(x)\right)^{-1}\right\|_{F}^{2}d\mu_{0}(x)

\displaystyle\leq 4\zeta^{2}\chi^{2}(\mu_{0}\|\pi)\frac{K_{2d}}{\lambda}.

(B.7)

Proof.

First, recall (
B.1
),

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla h(x)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=4\left\|\nabla(\Sigma_{\pi}+\lambda\mathrm{I})^{-1}(m_{\pi}-m_{\mu_{0}})(x)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=4\left\|\nabla\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}{\left\langle\frac{d\mu_{0}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}e_{i}(x)\right)\right\|^{2}.

(B.8)

For any
j\in\{1,\cdots,d\}
, consider

\displaystyle g_{M_{0}}(x)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i\geq M_{0}}\left|\varrho_{i}^{1/2}\left\langle\frac{d\mu_{0}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}\partial_{j}e_{i}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{i\geq M_{0}}\varrho_{i}\partial_{j}e_{i}(x)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{i\geq M_{0}}{\left\langle\frac{d\mu_{0}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{i\geq M_{0}}\varrho_{i}\partial_{j}e_{i}(x)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\|\frac{d\mu_{0}}{d\pi}-1\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}.

Since

\displaystyle\sum_{i\geq 1}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\varrho_{i}\left(\partial_{j}e_{i}(x)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\geq 1}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\varrho_{i}{\left\langle\partial_{j}k(x,\cdot),e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\sum_{i\geq 1}\sum_{j=1}^{d}{\left\langle\partial_{j}k(x,\cdot),\sqrt{\varrho_{i}}e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left\|\partial_{j}k(x,\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\left\|\nabla_{1}k(x,\cdot)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq K_{1d},

(B.9)

so
\sum_{i\geq M_{0}}\varrho_{i}(\partial_{j}e_{i}(x))^{2}
converges uniformly to
0
, and hence
g_{M_{0}}(x)
also converges uniformly to
0
. Therefore, we are allowed to interchange the derivative and the infinite sum
(
Rudin, 1976
)
in (
B.8
) to achieve,

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla h(x)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=4\left\|\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}{\left\langle\frac{d\mu_{0}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}\nabla e_{i}(x)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\varrho_{i}}{(\varrho_{i}+\lambda)^{2}}{\left\langle\frac{d\mu_{0}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}\right)\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\varrho_{i}\left\|\nabla e_{i}(x)\right\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{\lambda}\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}{\left\langle\frac{d\mu_{0}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle}_{\!L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}\right)\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\varrho_{i}\left\|\nabla e_{i}(x)\right\|^{2}\right)\leq 4\chi^{2}(\mu_{0}\|\pi)\frac{K_{1d}}{\lambda}.

(B.10)

The first inequality follows from Cauchy Schwartz, the penultimate inequality follows by noting that
\frac{\varrho_{i}}{(\varrho_{i}+\lambda)^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}
, and the last inequality follows from (
B.9
).
Given
\mathbf{H}V\preceq\beta\mathrm{I}
, (
B.6
) is proved by the following,

\displaystyle\int\nabla h(x)^{\top}\mathbf{H}V(\varphi_{t}(x))\nabla h(x)d\mu_{0}(x)\leq\beta\int\left\|\nabla h(x)\right\|^{2}d\mu_{0}(x)\leq 4\beta\chi^{2}(\mu_{0}\|\pi)\frac{K_{1d}}{\lambda}.

We now turn to proving the second statement. Similarly to (
B.10
), we have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{H}h(x)\right\|_{F}^{2}\leq 4\chi^{2}(\mu_{0}\|\pi)\frac{K_{2d}}{\lambda}.

(B.11)

Using
2(1+\lambda)\gamma\sqrt{\chi^{2}(\mu_{0}\|\pi)\frac{K_{2d}}{\lambda}}\leq\frac{\zeta-1}{\zeta}
for some constant
1<\zeta<2
, the inverse of
\mathrm{I}-t(1+\lambda)\mathbf{H}h(x)
can be represented by the Neumann series, and hence

\displaystyle\left\|(\mathrm{I}-t(1+\lambda)\mathbf{H}h(x))^{-1}\right\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m\geq 0}\|t(1+\lambda)\mathbf{H}h(x)\|_{F}^{m}\leq\sum_{m\geq 0}\left(\gamma(1+\lambda)2\sqrt{\chi^{2}(\mu_{0}\|\pi)\frac{K_{2d}}{\lambda}}\right)^{m}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m\geq 0}\left(\frac{\zeta-1}{\zeta}\right)^{m}=\zeta.

(B.12)

Therefore, (
B.7
) is proved by combining (
B.11
) and (
B.12
),

\displaystyle\int\left\|\mathbf{H}h(x)\left(\mathrm{I}-t(1+\lambda)\mathbf{H}h(x)\right)^{-1}\right\|_{F}^{2}d\mu_{0}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\int\left\|\mathbf{H}h(x)\right\|_{F}^{2}\left\|(\mathrm{I}-t(1+\lambda)\mathbf{H}h(x))^{-1}\right\|_{F}^{2}d\mu_{0}(x)\leq 4\zeta^{2}\chi^{2}(\mu_{0}\|\pi)\frac{K_{2d}}{\lambda}

and the result follows.
∎

Lemma B.8
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space,
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n}:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
are
n
identical independent
\mathcal{H}
-valued random variables satisfying
\left\|\xi_{i}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq B
. Then

\displaystyle\E\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}-\E[\xi_{1}]\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}B}{2\sqrt{n}},\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\E\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}-\E[\xi_{1}]\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\frac{B^{2}}{n}.

Proof.

We know from Corollary 6.15 of
Steinwart and Christmann (2008)
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}-\mathbb{E}\left[\xi_{1}\right]\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\geq t\right)\leq 2\exp\left(-2nt^{2}/B^{2}\right).

Then, denote
R:=\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}-\E[\xi_{1}]\|_{\mathcal{H}}
, we know that

\displaystyle\E[R]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(R\geq t)dt\leq 2\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp(-2nt^{2}/B^{2})dt=\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}B}{2\sqrt{n}}.

The other part is proved similarly.
∎

Lemma B.9

(Wasserstein Hessian of
\mathcal{F}_{\chi^{2}}
)
.

Let
\rho\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Consider the path
(\rho_{s})_{0\leq s\leq 1}
from
\rho
to
(\mathrm{I}+\nabla\phi)_{\#}\rho
given by
\rho_{s}=(\mathrm{I}+s\nabla\phi)_{\#}\rho
.
Define
\varphi_{s}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},x\mapsto x+s\nabla\phi(x)
and
\omega_{s}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},x\mapsto\left[\nabla\phi\circ\varphi_{s}^{-1}\right](x)
.
For
\pi(x)\propto\exp(-V(x))
, the second order derivative of
s\to\chi^{2}(\rho_{s}\|\pi)
is given by

\displaystyle\quad\frac{d^{2}}{ds^{2}}\chi^{2}(\rho_{s}\|\pi)=\int\frac{\rho_{s}(x)}{\pi(x)}\left(\nabla V(x)^{\top}\omega_{s}(x)-\nabla\cdot\omega_{s}(x)\right)^{2}d\rho_{s}(x)

\displaystyle\quad+\int\frac{\rho_{s}(x)}{\pi(x)}\omega_{s}(x)^{\top}\mathbf{H}V(x)\omega_{s}(x)d\rho_{s}(x)+\int\frac{\rho_{s}(x)}{\pi(x)}\left\|\nabla\omega_{s}(x)\right\|_{F}^{2}d\rho_{s}(x).

(B.13)

Equivalently, the second order derivative of
s\to\chi^{2}(\rho_{s}\|\pi)
can also be written as

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{ds^{2}}\chi^{2}(\rho_{s}\|\pi)

\displaystyle=2\int\left(\nabla\cdot\left(\omega_{s}(x)\rho_{s}(x)\right)\frac{1}{\pi(x)}\right)^{2}\pi(x)dx

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+2\int\omega_{s}(x)^{\top}\mathbf{H}\left(\frac{\rho_{s}(x)}{\pi(x)}\right)\omega_{s}(x)\rho_{s}(x)dx.

(B.14)

Proof.

(
B.13
) is provided in the Example 15.9 of
Villani and others (2009)
by taking
m=2
. Now, we are going to prove (
B.14
). For ease of notation in the following derivations, we are going to drop the function input
x
in
\rho_{s},\pi
, and
\omega_{s}
.
We introduce colors to picture grouping of terms that will carry over during chains of calculation.

In order to prove (
B.14
), we need to expand the terms in (
B.13
) accordingly. We denote the three terms in the RHS of (
B.13
) as
(A)
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.7,0.8}(B)}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1725,0.6275,0.1725}(C)}
.
Consider

\displaystyle(A)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi}\left(\nabla V^{\top}\omega_{s}-\nabla\cdot\omega_{s}\right)^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi}\left(-\frac{1}{\pi}\nabla\pi^{\top}\omega_{s}-\nabla\cdot\omega_{s}\right)^{2}dx

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.5804,0.4039,0.7412}\underbrace{\int\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{3}}(\nabla\pi^{\top}\omega_{s})^{2}dx}_{(A_{1})}}+{\color[rgb]{0.549,0.3373,0.2941}\underbrace{2\int\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}(\nabla\pi^{\top}\omega_{s})\nabla\cdot\omega_{s}dx}_{(A_{2})}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0.8}\underbrace{\int\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi}(\nabla\cdot\omega_{s})^{2}dx}_{(A_{3})}}.

Then we are going to use integration by parts for
{\color[rgb]{0.549,0.3373,0.2941}(A_{2})}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0.8}(A_{3})}
.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.549,0.3373,0.2941}(A_{2})}

\displaystyle=2\int\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}(\nabla\pi^{\top}\omega_{s})\nabla\cdot\omega_{s}dx=-2\int\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\left(\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}(\nabla\pi^{\top}\omega_{s})\right)dx

\displaystyle=-4\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s})(\nabla\pi^{\top}\omega_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi^{2}}dx+4\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\pi)^{2}\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{3}}dx

\displaystyle-2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\mathbf{H}\pi\omega_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}dx-2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\omega_{s}\nabla\pi)\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}dx,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0.8}(A_{3})}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi}(\nabla\cdot\omega_{s})^{2}dx=\int\nabla\cdot(\omega_{s})\nabla\cdot\omega_{s}\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi}dx=-\int\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\left(\nabla\cdot\omega_{s}\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi}\right)dx

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0.2}\underbrace{-\int\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla(\nabla\cdot\omega_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi}dx}_{(A_{31})}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\underbrace{-\int\nabla\cdot\omega_{s}(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s})\frac{2\rho_{s}}{\pi}dx}_{(A_{32})}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.6}\underbrace{+\int\nabla\cdot\omega_{s}(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\pi)\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}dx}_{(A_{33})}}.

Furthermore, we use integration by parts for
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(A_{32})}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.6}(A_{33})}
, we have

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(A_{32})}

\displaystyle=-2\int\nabla\cdot\omega_{s}(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi}dx=2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\left((\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi}\right))dx

\displaystyle=2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\omega_{s}\nabla\rho_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi}dx+2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\mathbf{H}\rho_{s}\omega_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi}dx+2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s})^{2}\frac{1}{\pi}dx

\displaystyle-2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s})(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\pi)\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi^{2}}dx,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.6}(A_{33})}

\displaystyle=\int\nabla\cdot\omega_{s}(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\pi)\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}dx=-\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\left((\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\pi)\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\right)dx

\displaystyle=-\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\omega_{s}\nabla\pi)\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}dx-\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\mathbf{H}\pi\omega_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}dx-2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s})(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\pi)\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi^{2}}dx

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\pi)^{2}\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{3}}dx.

Having completed
(A)
, now we turn to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.7,0.8}(B)}
.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0.7,0.8}(B)}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi}\omega_{s}^{\top}\mathbf{H}V\omega_{s}dx=-\int\omega_{s}^{\top}\mathbf{H}\pi\omega_{s}\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}dx+\int(\nabla\pi^{\top}\omega_{s})^{2}\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{3}}dx.

So, combining
(A)
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.7,0.8}(B)}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1725,0.6275,0.1725}(C)}
, we have

\displaystyle\quad\frac{d^{2}}{ds^{2}}\chi^{2}(\rho_{s}\|\pi)=(A)+{\color[rgb]{1,0.7,0.8}(B)}+{\color[rgb]{0.1725,0.6275,0.1725}(C)}={\color[rgb]{0.5804,0.4039,0.7412}(A_{1})}+{\color[rgb]{0.549,0.3373,0.2941}(A_{2})}+{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0.2}(A_{31})}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(A_{32})}+{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.6}(A_{33})}+{\color[rgb]{1,0.7,0.8}(B)}+{\color[rgb]{0.1725,0.6275,0.1725}(C)}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.5804,0.4039,0.7412}\int\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{3}}(\nabla\pi^{\top}\omega_{s})^{2}dx}

\displaystyle\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.549,0.3373,0.2941}-4\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s})(\nabla\pi^{\top}\omega_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi^{2}}dx+4\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\pi)^{2}\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{3}}dx-2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\mathbf{H}\pi\omega_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}dx}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.549,0.3373,0.2941}-2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\omega_{s}\nabla\pi)\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}dx}+{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0.2}(A_{31})}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(A_{32})}+{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.6}(A_{33})}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad{\color[rgb]{1,0.7,0.8}-\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\mathbf{H}\pi\omega_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}dx+\int(\nabla\pi^{\top}\omega_{s})^{2}\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{3}}dx}+{\color[rgb]{0.1725,0.6275,0.1725}\int\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi}\left\|\nabla\omega_{s}\right\|_{F}^{2}dx}.

Since
\omega_{s}=\nabla\phi\circ\varphi_{s}^{-1}
is a mapping from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, denote
\omega_{s,i}:=\left[\nabla\phi\circ\varphi_{s}^{-1}\right]_{i}
which is a mapping from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{R}
.
Notice that
\omega_{s,i}\partial_{j}\omega_{s,i}
vanishes at boundary because
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}
. Hence,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}\int\partial_{j}\left(\omega_{s,i}\partial_{j}\omega_{s,i}\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi}\right)dx

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}\int\partial_{j}\omega_{s,i}\partial_{j}\omega_{s,i}\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi}dx+\sum_{i,j}\int\omega_{s,i}\partial_{jj}\omega_{s,i}\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi}dx

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum_{i,j}\int\omega_{s,i}\partial_{j}\omega_{s,i}\left(\frac{2\rho_{s}\partial_{j}\rho_{s}}{\pi}-\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}\partial_{j}\pi}{\pi^{2}}\right)dx

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.1725,0.6275,0.1725}\int\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi}\left\|\nabla\omega_{s}\right\|_{F}^{2}dx}+\int\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi}(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla(\nabla\cdot\omega_{s}))dx+\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\omega_{s}\nabla\rho_{s})\frac{2\rho_{s}}{\pi}dx

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\omega_{s}\nabla\pi)\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}dx.

Therefore, by replacing
{\color[rgb]{0.1725,0.6275,0.1725}\int\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi}\left\|\nabla\omega_{s}\right\|_{F}^{2}dx}
, and noticing that
{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0.2}-\int\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi}(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla(\nabla\cdot\omega_{s}))dx}
is exactly
{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0.2}(A_{31})}
, we have the following:

\displaystyle\quad\frac{d^{2}}{ds^{2}}\chi^{2}(\rho_{s}\|\pi)={\color[rgb]{0.5804,0.4039,0.7412}\int\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{3}}(\nabla\pi^{\top}\omega_{s})^{2}dx}-{\color[rgb]{0.549,0.3373,0.2941}4\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s})(\nabla\pi^{\top}\omega_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi^{2}}dx+4\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\pi)^{2}\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{3}}dx}

\displaystyle\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.549,0.3373,0.2941}-2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\mathbf{H}\pi\omega_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}dx-2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\omega_{s}\nabla\pi)\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}dx}+{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0.2}(A_{31})}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(A_{32})}+{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.6}(A_{33})}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad{\color[rgb]{1,0.7,0.8}-\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\mathbf{H}\pi\omega_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}dx+\int(\nabla\pi^{\top}\omega_{s})^{2}\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{3}}dx}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-\int\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi}(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla(\nabla\cdot\omega_{s}))dx-\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\omega_{s}\nabla\rho_{s})\frac{2\rho_{s}}{\pi}dx+\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\omega_{s}\nabla\pi)\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}dx.

Next, we combine the terms and obtain

\displaystyle=2({\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0.2}(A_{31})}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(A_{32})}+{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.6}(A_{33})})+6\int\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{3}}(\nabla\pi^{\top}\omega_{s})^{2}dx-4\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s})(\nabla\pi^{\top}\omega_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi^{2}}dx

\displaystyle\,\,-3\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\mathbf{H}\pi\omega_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}dx-\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\omega_{s}\nabla\pi)\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}dx-2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\omega_{s}\nabla\rho_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi}dx-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(A_{32})}-{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.6}(A_{33})}.

Recall that
{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0.2}(A_{31})}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(A_{32})}+{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.6}(A_{33})}={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0.8}(A_{3})}
, and replacing
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(A_{32})}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.6}(A_{33})}
with their expressions, we have

\displaystyle=2{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0.8}\int\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi}(\nabla\cdot\omega_{s})^{2}dx}+6\int\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{3}}(\nabla\pi^{\top}\omega_{s})^{2}dx-4\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s})(\nabla\pi^{\top}\omega_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi^{2}}dx

\displaystyle-3\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\mathbf{H}\pi\omega_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}dx-\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\omega_{s}\nabla\pi)\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}dx-2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\omega_{s}\nabla\rho_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi}dx

\displaystyle-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\left(2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\omega_{s}\nabla\rho_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi}dx+2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\mathbf{H}\rho_{s}\omega_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi}dx+2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s})^{2}\frac{1}{\pi}dx-2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s})(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\pi)\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi^{2}}dx\right)}

\displaystyle-{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0.6}\left(-\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\omega_{s}\nabla\pi)\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}dx-\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\mathbf{H}\pi\omega_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}dx-2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s})(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\pi)\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi^{2}}dx+2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\pi)^{2}\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{3}}dx\right)}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\mathbf{H}\rho_{s}\omega_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi}dx-2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\mathbf{H}\pi\omega_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}dx-4\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s})(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\pi)\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi^{2}}dx+4\int\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi^{3}}(\nabla\pi^{\top}\omega_{s})^{2}dx}_{(M)}

\displaystyle\hskip 17.00024pt+\underbrace{2\int\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi}(\nabla\cdot\omega_{s})^{2}dx-2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s})^{2}\frac{1}{\pi}dx-4\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\omega_{s}\nabla\rho_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi}dx}_{(N_{1})}

\displaystyle\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt\underbrace{-4\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\mathbf{H}\rho_{s}\omega_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi}dx+4\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s})(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\pi)\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi^{2}}dx}_{(N_{2})}.

Now we analyze
(M)
and
(N_{1})+(N_{2})
separately. Notice that

\displaystyle(M)=2\int\left(\omega_{s}^{\top}\mathbf{H}\left(\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi}\right)\omega_{s}\right)\rho_{s}dx,

and

\displaystyle\quad(N_{1})+(N_{2})=2\int\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi}(\nabla\cdot\omega_{s})^{2}dx+2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s})^{2}\frac{1}{\pi}dx-4\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\omega_{s}\nabla\rho_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi}dx

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-4\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\mathbf{H}\rho_{s}\omega_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi}dx-4\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s})^{2}\frac{1}{\pi}dx+4\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s})(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\pi)\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi^{2}}dx

\displaystyle=2\int\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi}(\nabla\cdot\omega_{s})^{2}dx+2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s})^{2}\frac{1}{\pi}dx-4\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s}))\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi}dx

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-4\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s})(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi})dx

\displaystyle=2\int\frac{\rho_{s}^{2}}{\pi}(\nabla\cdot\omega_{s})^{2}dx+2\int(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s})^{2}\frac{1}{\pi}dx+4\int\nabla\cdot\omega_{s}(\omega_{s}^{\top}\nabla\rho_{s})\frac{\rho_{s}}{\pi}dx

\displaystyle=2\int\left(\nabla\cdot(\omega_{s}\rho_{s})\frac{1}{\pi}\right)^{2}\pi dx.

Since
\frac{d^{2}}{ds^{2}}\chi^{2}(\rho_{s}\|\pi)=(M)+(N_{1})+(N_{2})
,
Lemma

B.9
is proved.
∎

Appendix C: An Illustrative Example for Explicit Forms of
\mathcal{I}_{t}
,
\mathcal{J}_{t}
,
\|q_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}

Consider an illustrative example where we simulate the DrMMD gradient flow when the target is a one-dimensional Gaussian target distribution
\pi=\mathcal{N}(0,\bar{\sigma}^{2})
and the initialization is also a one-dimensional Gaussian
\mu_{0}=\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{1}{2}\bar{\sigma}^{2})
.
We take a Gaussian kernel
k(x,y)=\exp(-\frac{1}{2}(x-y)^{2})
whose eigenvalues and eigenfunctions in its Mercer decomposition have the following closed form expressions
(
Shi et al., 2009
, Proposition 1)
,

\displaystyle\varrho_{i}=\sqrt{\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}}{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}+c+0.5}}\left(\frac{\beta^{2}-1}{\beta^{2}+1}\right)^{i},\quad e_{i}(x)=\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{i!2^{i}}}\exp(-cx^{2})\mathrm{H}_{i}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}}\beta x\right),

(C.1)

where
\beta=(1+4\bar{\sigma}^{2})^{1/4}
,
c=\frac{\beta^{2}-1}{4\bar{\sigma}^{2}}
and
\mathrm{H}_{i}
is the
i
-th Hermite polynomial function.
We pick
\bar{\sigma}^{2}>2
so
\beta^{2}>3
.
The Gaussian kernel is continuous, bounded and
c_{0}
-universal as required in Assumption 1. It also possesses bounded first- and second-order derivatives, thereby satisfying Assumption 2.

Consider the DrMMD gradient flow
(\mu_{t})_{t\geq 0}
defined in (
7
) along with its particle update scheme
dx_{t}=-(1+\lambda)\nabla h_{\mu_{t},\pi}(x_{t})dt
, where
h_{\mu_{t},\pi}
is the witness function defined in (
32
) and
\lambda
is a positive regularization parameter.
Denote
m_{t},\sigma_{t}^{2}
as the mean and covariance of
\mu_{t}
, respectively, then we have the following update scheme for
m_{t},\sigma_{t}^{2}
proved in
Lemma

C.1
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}m_{t}

\displaystyle=-(1+\lambda)\E_{x_{t}\sim\mu_{t}}[\nabla h_{\mu_{t},\pi}(x_{t})]\;\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(\sigma_{t}^{2})

\displaystyle=-2(1+\lambda)\E_{x_{t}\sim\mu_{t}}[\nabla h_{\mu_{t},\pi}(x_{t})\cdot x_{t}]\;\mathrm{d}t+2(1+\lambda)\E_{x_{t}\sim\mu_{t}}[\nabla h_{\mu_{t},\pi}(x_{t})]\cdot m_{t}\;\mathrm{d}t,

where the expectations are taken over
x_{t}\sim\mu_{t}
. While the resulting distribution is not necessarily Gaussian, we may follow the existing analysis of Stein variational gradient descent
(
Liu et al., 2024b
)
and Langevin Monte Carlo dynamics
(
Lambert et al., 2022
)
and approximate
x_{t}\sim\mu_{t}
with a Gaussian random variable
y_{t}\sim\nu_{t}=\mathcal{N}(m_{t},\sigma_{t}^{2})
; this yields the update scheme:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathrm{d}m_{t}&=(1+\lambda)\E_{y_{t}\sim\nu_{t}}[\nabla h_{\nu_{t},\pi}(y_{t})]\;\mathrm{d}t,\\
\mathrm{d}(\sigma_{t}^{2})&=-2(1+\lambda)\E_{y_{t}\sim\nu_{t}}[\nabla h_{\nu_{t},\pi}(y_{t})\cdot y_{t}]\;\mathrm{d}t+2(1+\lambda)\E_{y_{t}\sim\nu_{t}}[\nabla h_{\nu_{t},\pi}(y_{t})]\cdot m_{t}\;\mathrm{d}t,\end{aligned}

(C.2)

which gives an evolution of Gaussian distributions
\nu_{t}
.
From (
32
), the witness function
h_{\nu_{t},\pi}
admits a decomposition with eigenvalues
\varrho_{i}
and eigenfunctions
e_{i}
:
h_{\nu_{t},\pi}(y)=\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\langle\frac{d\nu_{t}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}e_{i}(y)
.
Therefore, the velocity field
\frac{d}{dy}h_{\nu_{t},\pi}(y)
can be written as,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dy}h_{\nu_{t},\pi}(y)=\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda}\left\langle\frac{d\nu_{t}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}\frac{d}{dy}e_{i}(y).

Notice that if
m_{t}=0
, then for odd
i
,
\langle\frac{d\nu_{t}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}=\int e_{i}d\nu_{t}-\int e_{i}d\pi=0
because
e_{i}
is an odd function. When
i
is even,
\E_{\nu_{t}}[\frac{d}{dy}e_{i}(y)]=0
because
y\mapsto\frac{d}{dy}e_{i}(y)
is an odd function. As a result, if
m_{t}=0
, then
\E[\nabla h_{\nu_{t},\pi}(y_{t})]=0
and hence
\frac{dm_{t}}{dt}=0
from the update scheme in (
C.2
). Therefore, as long as we initialize the DrMMD gradient flow with
\nu_{0}=\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{1}{2}\bar{\sigma}^{2})
, a zero mean Gaussian distribution, the entire trajectory will remain a zero mean Gaussian distribution
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}_{t})
.

Next, observe that for a zero-mean Gaussian trajectory, if the initial variance satisfies
\sigma_{0}^{2}<\bar{\sigma}^{2}
, it is natural to expect that the variance increases monotonically toward the target variance
\bar{\sigma}^{2}
as the DrMMD flow evolves, i.e.
\sigma_{0}^{2}<\sigma_{t}^{2}\leq\bar{\sigma}^{2}
for all
t
.
In
Lemma

C.2
, we provide a rigorous proof of this claim in the cases
\lambda=0
and
\lambda=\infty
, by showing that
(1+\lambda)\E_{y_{t}\sim\nu_{t}}[\nabla h_{\nu_{t},\pi}(y_{t})\cdot y_{t}]<0
, which implies that the variance update in (
C.2
) is monotone increasing.
The cases
\lambda=0
and
\lambda=\infty
correspond respectively to the
\chi^{2}
flow and the MMD flow regimes.
For general
\lambda>0
, however, we are unable to establish a rigorous proof.
Our argument in
Lemma

C.2
relies heavily on Mehler’s formula
(
Liang and Tran-Bach, 2022
, Proposition 2.2)
, which requires exponential decay of the spectrum
(\varrho_{i})_{i\geq 1}
. This condition is not satisfied for DrMMD, whose spectrum is modified to
(\frac{\varrho_{i}}{\varrho_{i}+\lambda})_{i\geq 1}
. Nevertheless, we conjecture that the monotonicity property continues to hold for all
\lambda>0
.

Checking assumptions in Theorem 4.1 and Theorem 5.1:

Now we check the assumptions from Theorem 4.1 and Theorem 5.1. The target
\pi
is a Gaussian distribution which automatically satisfies a Poincaré inequality, and its potential
V
is a quadratic function, hence satisfies
\mathbf{H}V\leq\beta\mathrm{I}
.
\nu_{t},\pi
are Gaussians, hence absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue on
\mathbb{R}
.
And most importantly, we have
\frac{d\nu_{t}}{d\pi}-1\in\operatorname{Ran}(\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{0.25})
, i.e., there exists
q_{t}\in L^{2}(\pi)
such that
\frac{d\nu_{t}}{d\pi}-1=\mathcal{T}_{\pi}^{0.25}q_{t}
. To see why, we first need to upper bound
\langle\frac{d\nu_{t}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}
. From
Belafhal et al. (2020, Corollary 2)
, we have the following closed-form expressions for
\langle\frac{d\nu_{t}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}
:

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{i!2^{i}}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}c}}\left(1-\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}}{c+\frac{1}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}}\right)^{\frac{i}{2}}-\sqrt{\frac{1}{1+2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c}}\left(1-\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}}{c+\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}}\right)^{\frac{i}{2}}\right)\mathrm{H}_{i}(0),

when
i
is even and
0
otherwise.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{d\nu_{t}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{1+\bar{\sigma}^{2}c}\cdot\frac{\beta}{i!2^{i}}|\mathrm{H}_{i}(0)|^{2}\cdot 2\left(\left|\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}}{c+\frac{1}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}}-1\right|^{i}+\left|\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}}{c+\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}}-1\right|^{i}\right).

By the monotonicity of the variance,
\sigma_{0}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\bar{\sigma}^{2}\leq\sigma_{t}^{2}<\bar{\sigma}^{2}
and
\beta^{2}>3
, we have

\displaystyle 0<\frac{2\beta^{2}}{\beta^{2}+3}-1=\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}}{c+\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}}-1<\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}}{c+\frac{1}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}}-1<\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}}{c+\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}}-1=\frac{2\beta^{2}}{\beta^{2}+1}-1.

So we have the following upper bound

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{d\nu_{t}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}\leq\frac{4}{1+\bar{\sigma}^{2}c}\cdot\frac{\beta}{i!2^{i}}|\mathrm{H}_{i}(0)|^{2}\cdot\left(\frac{2\beta^{2}}{\beta^{2}+1}-1\right)^{i}.

Now we are ready to study the
L^{2}(\pi)
-norm of
q_{t}
. Recall the formulas of
\varrho_{i}
in (
C.1
), we have

\displaystyle\|q_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\left\langle\frac{d\nu_{t}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}}{\varrho_{i}^{0.5}}\leq\frac{4\beta}{1+\bar{\sigma}^{2}c}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{i!2^{i}}\left|\mathrm{H}_{i}(0)\right|^{2}\left(\frac{\beta^{2}-1}{\beta^{2}+1}\right)^{i}\left(\left(\frac{\beta^{2}+1}{\beta^{2}-1}\right)^{0.5}\right)^{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\beta}{1+\bar{\sigma}^{2}c}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{i!2^{i}}\left|\mathrm{H}_{i}(0)\right|^{2}\left(\left(\frac{\beta^{2}-1}{\beta^{2}+1}\right)^{0.5}\right)^{i}=\frac{4\beta}{1+\bar{\sigma}^{2}c}\sqrt{\frac{\beta^{2}+1}{2}}.

The last equality holds by Mehler’s formula
(
Liang and Tran-Bach, 2022
, Proposition 2.2)
.
A quick sanity check for the above derivations is to take
r=0.5
and see that
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\varrho_{i}^{-1}\langle\frac{d\nu_{t}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}
is divergent. This indicates that
\frac{d\nu_{t}}{d\pi}-1\notin\mathcal{H}
, which verifies the fact that the Gaussian RKHS does not contain constant functions
(
Steinwart and Christmann, 2008
, Corollary 4.44)
.
Finally,
\mathcal{I}_{t}
and
\mathcal{J}_{t}
admit the following explicit formulas as well as uniform upper bounds

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{t}

\displaystyle=\left\|\nabla(\log\pi)^{\top}\nabla\left(\frac{d\nu_{t}}{d\pi}\right)\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\pi)}=\frac{\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}-\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\bar{\sigma}^{3}\sigma_{t}^{2}}\cdot\frac{\Gamma(7/2)}{\left(\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}-\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\right)^{7/2}}\leq\frac{4\Gamma(7/2)}{\sqrt{2\pi}\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{7/2}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{t}

\displaystyle=\left\|\Delta\left(\frac{d\nu_{t}}{d\pi}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}=\left(\frac{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\frac{\left(\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}-\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\right)^{2}}{\sqrt{2\pi\bar{\sigma}^{2}}}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{\Gamma(1/2)}{\left(\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}-\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\right)^{1/2}}+\frac{2\Gamma(3/2)\left(\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}-\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\right)}{\left(\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}-\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\right)^{3/2}}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad+\left.\frac{\Gamma(5/2)\left(\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}-\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\right)^{2}}{\left(\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}-\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\right)^{5/2}}\right)\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{2\pi}\bar{\sigma}^{4}}\left(\frac{\Gamma(1/2)}{\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{5/2}}+\frac{\Gamma(3/2)}{\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{3/2}}+\frac{\Gamma(5/2)}{\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{5/2}}\right).

Empirical verification:

While the above derivations demonstrate that a (Gaussian projected) DrMMD gradient flow satisfies all the required assumptions, it is instructive to demonstrate that a particle implementation in the discrete time setting, without explicit Gaussian projection,
shows the behavior consistent with the theory. We therefore simulate our finite particle DrMMD gradient descent
\hat{\mu}_{n}
with a step size
\gamma=0.01
and particle number
N=10,000
and empirically inspect its convergence properties. We take
\bar{\sigma}^{2}=6
so
\beta^{2}=5
.
We estimate the density of the DrMMD descent
\mu_{n}
from the particles with a kernel density estimator using a Gaussian kernel with lengthscale
0.1

(
Epanechnikov, 1969
)
.
Based on the estimated densities
\mu_{n}
, we compute the following two quantities:
\mathcal{I}_{n}=\|\nabla V^{\top}\nabla(\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi})\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}
and
\mathcal{J}_{n}=\|\Delta(\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi})\|_{L^{2}(\pi)}
.
Their evolution along the DrMMD gradient descent is shown in
Figure

3
.
We observe that both quantities decrease over time as desired, which is a consequence of increasing smoothness of the density ratio
\frac{d\mu_{n}}{d\pi}
as
\mu_{n}
converges to
\pi
.
We also report the evolution of the KL divergence
\widehat{\mathrm{KL}}(\hat{\mu}_{n}\|\hat{\pi})
along the flow, estimated from particles.

Figure 3
:
Evolution of
\widehat{\mathrm{KL}}(\hat{\mu}_{n}\|\hat{\pi})
,
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{n}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{n}
along DrMMD particle descent, where all three terms are estimated with samples.

Lemma C.1
.

Given the DrMMD gradient flow update scheme
dx_{t}=-(1+\lambda)\nabla h_{\mu_{t},\pi}(x_{t})dt
, its mean
m_{t}=\E[x_{t}]
and variance
\sigma_{t}^{2}=\E[x_{t}^{2}]-\E[x_{t}]^{2}
update scheme can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}m_{t}

\displaystyle=-(1+\lambda)\E_{x_{t}\sim\mu_{t}}[\nabla h_{\mu_{t},\pi}(x_{t})]\;\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(\sigma_{t}^{2})

\displaystyle=-2(1+\lambda)\E_{x_{t}\sim\mu_{t}}[\nabla h_{\mu_{t},\pi}(x_{t})\cdot x_{t}]\;\mathrm{d}t+2(1+\lambda)\E_{x_{t}\sim\mu_{t}}[\nabla h_{\mu_{t},\pi}(x_{t})]\cdot m_{t}\;\mathrm{d}t,

Proof.

For the mean update,
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}m_{t}=\E[\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}x_{t}]=-(1+\lambda)\E_{x_{t}\sim\mu_{t}}[\nabla h_{\mu_{t},\pi}(x_{t})]
. For the variance update,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\sigma_{t}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(\E[x_{t}^{2}]-\E[x_{t}]^{2}\right)=2\E[x_{t}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}x_{t}]-2\E[x_{t}]\cdot\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\E[x_{t}]

\displaystyle=-2(1+\lambda)\E_{x_{t}\sim\mu_{t}}[\nabla h_{\mu_{t},\pi}(x_{t})\cdot x_{t}]+2(1+\lambda)\E_{x_{t}\sim\mu_{t}}[\nabla h_{\mu_{t},\pi}(x_{t})]\cdot m_{t}.

Hence the result.
∎

Lemma C.2
.

Let
\nu_{t}
be a zero mean normal distribution
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{t}^{2})
and
0<\sigma_{t}^{2}<\bar{\sigma}^{2}
.
For the eigenvalues
(\varrho_{i})_{i\geq 1}
and eigenfunctions
(e_{i})_{i\geq 1}
defined in (
C.1
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\geq 1}\left\langle\frac{d\nu_{t}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}\E_{x\sim\nu_{t}}\left[\frac{d}{dx}e_{i}(x)x\right]<0,

\displaystyle\sum_{i\geq 1}\varrho_{i}\left\langle\frac{d\nu_{t}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}\E_{x\sim\nu_{t}}\left[\frac{d}{dx}e_{i}(x)x\right]<0.

which correspond to the variance update in (
C.2
) when
\lambda=0
and
\lambda=\infty
, respectively.

Proof.

For the inner product term, we know from Corollary 2 of
Belafhal et al. (2020)
that

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{d\nu_{t}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}_{i}(0)\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{i!2^{i}}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}c}}\left(1-\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}}{c+\frac{1}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}}\right)^{\frac{i}{2}}-\sqrt{\frac{1}{1+2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c}}\left(1-\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}}{c+\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}}\right)^{\frac{i}{2}}\right).

For the expectation term, notice that

\displaystyle\quad\E_{x\sim\nu_{t}}\left[\frac{d}{dx}e_{i}(x)x\right]=\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{i!2^{i}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma_{t}}\int x\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}x^{2}\right)\frac{d}{dx}e_{i}(x)\;\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{=}}-\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{i!2^{i}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma_{t}}\int\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}x^{2}\right)\left(1-\frac{x^{2}}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\right)e_{i}(x)\;\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{i!2^{i}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma_{t}}\int\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}x^{2}\right)\exp(-cx^{2})\mathrm{H}_{i}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}}\beta x\right)\;\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\qquad+\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{i!2^{i}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma_{t}^{3}}\int\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}x^{2}\right)\exp(-cx^{2})\mathrm{H}_{i}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}}\beta x\right)x^{2}\;\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=:E_{i,1}+E_{i,2}.

Here in
(\ast)
, we use integration by parts in which the boundary term vanishes, because
\lim_{x\to\infty}\exp(-x^{2})\mathrm{H}_{i}(x)=0
.
Next, notice that the second term
E_{i,2}
equals precisely the derivative of
E_{i,1}
with respect to
c
, rescaled by
\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}
. To distinguish it from the other
c
that will show up later in
\langle\frac{d\nu_{t}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}
, we denote it as
\mathfrak{c}
.
The original
\sum_{i\geq 1}\langle\frac{d\nu_{t}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}\E_{x\sim\nu_{t}}[\frac{d}{dx}e_{i}(x)x]
can be written as the sum of two components
F_{1}+F_{2}
:

\displaystyle F_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\geq 1}\mathrm{H}_{i}(0)\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{i!2^{i}}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}c}}\left(1-\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}}{c+\frac{1}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}}\right)^{\frac{i}{2}}-\sqrt{\frac{1}{1+2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c}}\left(1-\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}}{c+\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}}\right)^{\frac{i}{2}}\right)E_{i,1},

\displaystyle F_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\geq 1}\mathrm{H}_{i}(0)\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{i!2^{i}}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}c}}\left(1-\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}}{c+\frac{1}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}}\right)^{\frac{i}{2}}-\sqrt{\frac{1}{1+2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c}}\left(1-\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}}{c+\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}}\right)^{\frac{i}{2}}\right)E_{i,2}.

From Corollary 2 of
Belafhal et al. (2020)
, we know

\displaystyle E_{i,1}=-\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{i!2^{i}}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{c}}}\left(1-\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}}{\mathfrak{c}+\frac{1}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}}\right)^{\frac{i}{2}}\mathrm{H}_{i}(0),

and
E_{i,2}=\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\frac{d}{d\mathfrak{c}}E_{i,1}
. The relation between
E_{i,1}
and
E_{i,2}
would carry over to
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
as well, i.e.,
F_{2}=\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\frac{d}{d\mathfrak{c}}F_{1}
.
Next, we compute the first half of
F_{1}
.

\displaystyle\quad\sum_{i\geq 1}\mathrm{H}_{i}(0)\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{i!2^{i}}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}c}}\left(1-\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}}{c+\frac{1}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}}\right)^{\frac{i}{2}}E_{i,1}

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{\frac{1}{1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}c}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{c}}}\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\beta}{i!2^{i}}\left(1-\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}}{c+\frac{1}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}}\right)^{\frac{i}{2}}\left(1-\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}}{\mathfrak{c}+\frac{1}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}}\right)^{\frac{i}{2}}|\mathrm{H}_{i}(0)|^{2}

\displaystyle=-\beta\sqrt{\frac{1}{1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}c}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{c}}}\left(1-\left(1-\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}}{c+\frac{1}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}}\right)\cdot\left(1-\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}}{\mathfrak{c}+\frac{1}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}}\right)\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=-\beta\left((1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}c)(1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{c})-(1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}c-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2})(1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{c}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2})\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}(2+2\sigma_{t}^{2}c+2\sigma_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{c})-\left(\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta\right)^{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

The second last equality holds by the Mehler’s formula
(
Liang and Tran-Bach, 2022
, Proposition 2.2)
.
Similarly, we can also compute the second half of
F_{1}
.

\displaystyle\quad\sum_{i\geq 1}\mathrm{H}_{i}(0)\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{i!2^{i}}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{1+2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c}}\left(1-\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}}{c+\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}}\right)^{\frac{i}{2}}E_{i,1}

\displaystyle=-\beta\left((1+2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c)(1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{c})-(1+2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c-\beta^{2})(1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{c}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2})\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=-\left(1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{c}+(1+2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c)\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Combining the above two equations, we obtain the following formula of
F_{1}
.

\displaystyle F_{1}

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}(2+2\sigma_{t}^{2}c+2\sigma_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{c})-\left(\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta\right)^{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}+\left(1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{c}+(1+2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c)\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Since
F_{2}=\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\frac{d}{d\mathfrak{c}}F_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle F_{2}=\left(\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}(2+2\sigma_{t}^{2}c+2\sigma_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{c})-\left(\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta\right)^{2}\right)^{-\frac{3}{2}}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}-\left(1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{c}+(1+2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c)\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}\right)^{-\frac{3}{2}}.

Recall that the original
\sum_{i\geq 1}\langle\frac{d\nu_{t}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}\E_{x\sim\nu_{t}}[\frac{d}{dx}e_{i}(x)x]
can be written as the sum of two components
F_{1}+F_{2}
, and recall that
\mathfrak{c}=c
and
\beta^{2}=1+4\bar{\sigma}^{2}c
by definition. We obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{i\geq 1}\left\langle\frac{d\nu_{t}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}\E_{x\sim\nu_{t}}\left[\frac{d}{dx}e_{i}(x)x\right]=F_{1}+F_{2}=\left(\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\left(2-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\right)\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\left(-1+\left(2-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\right)^{-1}\right).

which is negative when
0<\sigma_{t}<\bar{\sigma}
. So we have concluded the proof of the first claim.
Now, we are about to prove the second claim.
Following the same derivations as above, we can write
\sum_{i\geq 1}\langle\varrho_{i}\frac{d\nu_{t}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}\E_{x\sim\nu_{t}}[\frac{d}{dx}e_{i}(x)x]
as the sum of two terms
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
.

\displaystyle G_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\geq 1}\varrho_{i}\mathrm{H}_{i}(0)\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{i!2^{i}}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}c}}\left(1-\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}}{c+\frac{1}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}}\right)^{\frac{i}{2}}-\sqrt{\frac{1}{1+2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c}}\left(1-\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}}{c+\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}}\right)^{\frac{i}{2}}\right)E_{i,1},

\displaystyle G_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\geq 1}\varrho_{i}\mathrm{H}_{i}(0)\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{i!2^{i}}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}c}}\left(1-\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}}{c+\frac{1}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}}\right)^{\frac{i}{2}}-\sqrt{\frac{1}{1+2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c}}\left(1-\frac{\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\beta^{2}}{c+\frac{1}{2\bar{\sigma}^{2}}}\right)^{\frac{i}{2}}\right)E_{i,2}.

Since
\varrho_{i}\propto(\frac{\beta^{2}-1}{\beta^{2}+1})^{i}
defined in (
C.1
) has exponential decay, hence Mehler’s formula still hold. Up to some positive multiplier coefficient that do not change the sign,
G_{1}
can be written as the following formula

\displaystyle G_{1}

\displaystyle=-\left((2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c)^{-2}(1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}c)^{2}+2\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{4}}{\bar{\sigma}^{4}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}+\left((2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c)^{-2}(1+2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c)(1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{c})+1\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

And similar to
F_{1},F_{2}
, we have
G_{2}=\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\frac{d}{d\mathfrak{c}}G_{1}
. As a result,

\displaystyle G_{2}

\displaystyle=\left((2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c)^{-2}(1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}c)^{2}+2\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{4}}{\bar{\sigma}^{4}}\right)^{-\frac{3}{2}}\cdot\left(\frac{1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}c}{(2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c)^{2}}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle-\left((2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c)^{-2}(1+2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c)(1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{c})+1\right)^{-\frac{3}{2}}\cdot\left(\frac{1+2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c}{(2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c)^{2}}+1\right).

Combined, we obtain

\displaystyle G_{1}+G_{2}

\displaystyle=-\left((2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c)^{-2}(1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}c)^{2}+2\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{4}}{\bar{\sigma}^{4}}\right)^{-\frac{3}{2}}\cdot\left((1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}c)\frac{2\sigma_{t}^{2}c}{(2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c)^{2}}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{4}}{\bar{\sigma}^{4}}\right)

\displaystyle+\left((2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c)^{-2}(1+2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c)(1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}c)+1\right)^{-\frac{3}{2}}\cdot(1+2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c)\frac{2\sigma_{t}^{2}c}{(2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c)^{2}}.

When
0<\sigma_{t}^{2}<\bar{\sigma}^{2}
, we have the following relations

\displaystyle(2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c)^{-2}(1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}c)^{2}+2\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{4}}{\bar{\sigma}^{4}}

\displaystyle<(2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c)^{-2}(1+2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c)(1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}c)+1,

\displaystyle(1+2\sigma_{t}^{2}c)\frac{2\sigma_{t}^{2}c}{(2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c)^{2}}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{4}}{\bar{\sigma}^{4}}

\displaystyle>(1+2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c)\frac{2\sigma_{t}^{2}c}{(2\bar{\sigma}^{2}c)^{2}}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{i\geq 1}\varrho_{i}\left\langle\frac{d\nu_{t}}{d\pi}-1,e_{i}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}\E_{x\sim\nu_{t}}\left[\frac{d}{dx}e_{i}(x)x\right]=G_{1}+G_{2}<0,

proving the second claim.
∎
